**Readings the gate took:**
- **"Every curve and surface kind"** is every variant of `CurveKind` (15) and of `SurfaceKind` (11), in 2D and 3D for the curves that take both; a kind that only another kind makes (an affine surface, from a non-similarity) passes through the kind that makes it.
- **"The kind laws"** are 28.3's rows for curve and surface kinds and for NURBS, as G69 built them (28.3's as-built note): conversion to NURBS within its stated error, transforms that change a kind only as chapter 7 and each kind's rule say, grid transforms exact, and the NURBS row. Each law fails on any broken case, a refusal included.
- **"Seven consecutive nights"** are seven consecutive scheduled runs of `kernel-nightly.yml`'s `laws` job on main, each green with its own seed, the first after G69's merge. A red night is triaged (the work queue's row G), its case kept as a permanent test once fixed (28.16), and the count starts again.
- **"10,000 systems per dimension"** is per dimension of G53's root engine, one to four unknowns.
- **Decided: a fuzz target per code piece, as 30.1 says.** The dry run asked whether 28.5's "one target per operation" lets phase 2, which has no operations yet, go without targets. It does not. 30.1's standard bar names the target for "every code piece", and principle 12 says every operation ships with fuzz tests besides; 28.5 says which targets the operations add, not which pieces may skip one. A seeded property test is not a substitute: 30.1 lists both, and only a coverage-guided fuzzer steers towards inputs no generator was written to make. So each phase-2 code piece's public entry points are reached by a `geom-fuzz` target, in 28.5's layout, that must not panic or hang on any bytes: transforms and their classification, the dense and spectral decompositions, the box tree and cell index, NURBS evaluation and its operations, every curve and surface kind's evaluation, bounds, conversion and transforms, and the root engine. One target may serve several pieces of a crate when it reaches each piece's code; the gate's record names the target for each piece.
- **Decided: a count benchmark per code piece, as 30.1 says.** The dry run asked whether the phase-2 crates outside geom-num could keep wall-clock benchmarks only. They cannot. 30.1 asks every code piece for "at least one [benchmark], under the 5% count gate", principle 12 blocks every speed regression over 5%, and 28.14 explains why only counts can gate a pull request: wall clock on shared runners varies by more than 5%. So `geom-bench` gains count benchmarks, under the instruction and fuel gates of 23.13, on each phase-2 piece's hot path (a transform's image, a dense solve and a spectral step, a box-tree query and build, a NURBS point and knot insertion, a curve kind's and a surface kind's enclosure, bound and conversion, a root search); the existing wall-clock benchmarks stay for the nightly reference runner. One benchmark may serve several pieces of a crate when it runs each piece's code; the gate's record names the benchmark for each piece.

**Changes:** this record; issue #418. The evidence it cites is the pieces' own (G44 to G69), and G69's law catalogue with its nightly job. The two decisions above add work before the gate: the phase-2 fuzz targets and count benchmarks. The record was drafted as 32.44, a number main gave to another decision first; two history records of `geom-test`'s golden store, for the count benchmarks, still cite it so, since the store's history only grows.

## 32.47 Fixed runs stay: no name-ordered bucket tree (G105)

**What it is.** 31.7 left one question to the piece "Content hashes and cache keys": keep cutting a body's tables into fixed runs of 4,096 rows (24.2), or cut them by name, ending a bucket where a row's name meets a fixed condition, so that adding an entity, which shifts every row after it in name order (32.17), leaves the other chunks alone.

**Measured** by `geom-format`'s `tests/bucket_tree.rs`, in counts, the same on every machine. The body is a strip of 20,000 quads published by name, each table past the chunk size, 460,032 rows and 9,302,983 bytes of chunk payload once one more quad is added with a name in the middle of the order. A row counts as changed if no row of the old version has its values. The bucket column is not one guess at a cut but a lower bound for any cut whose buckets hold at least 1,024 rows, a quarter of the mean: all rows outside runs of that many unchanged rows. A table's rows are counted at its mean bytes.

| Tables | Bytes | Fixed runs rewrite | Any bucket cut rewrites at least |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Faces, loops, fins, edges: rows of links | 3,504,818 | 3,021,772 | 2,915,162 |
| Vertices, points, surfaces, curves, names: rows of values | 5,798,165 | 4,176,392 | 29,226 |
| All | 9,302,983 | 7,198,164 (77%) | 2,944,388 (32%) |

An edit in place, which moves no row, rewrites one chunk under either scheme (241,522 bytes here), and `BodyVersion::next` hashes only that chunk. The first analysis of this question expected nothing from buckets, since links are row numbers. That is true of the link tables, but the tables of values only shift, and buckets could keep nearly all of them.

| Option | What it means |
| --- | --- |
| A. Fixed runs in every table | One rule. A row's chunk is its row divided by 4,096. Adding an entity to a big body rewrites most of it |
| B. Buckets in every table | The link tables gain nothing: every row of them changes, whatever the cut |
| C. Buckets in the tables of values only | Up to 59% fewer bytes for this edit. Two rules in the specification, and the index and chunks change |

**Decided 2026-10-31: A.**
- **C's saving is real but narrow.** It needs a table past 4,096 rows, so a body of about a thousand faces or more: a wall or a door has one chunk per table, rewritten whole under any scheme.
- **It saves bytes stored, not time.** A cut has to read every row to find its ends, and buckets lie across the columns' chunks, so `BodyVersion::next` could not skip them: hashing a new version would cost no less.
- **It costs what the fixed rule gives.** With fixed runs a reader finds a row's chunk by division, which the browser's chunks in view rely on (23.9). Buckets need a search over row counts, a chunk's place taken out of its payload (a shifted bucket must keep its ID), and a second cutting rule in `SPEC.md`.
- **It can come later.** The cut is fixed by the specification and versioned (24.2), and a chunk record has a schema, so a later format can cut the tables of values differently and still read every file (24.6). 32.17's remedy, a canonical key before the name, stays open too.

Reopen this if real containers show saved versions dominated by the tables of values of big bodies; `tests/bucket_tree.rs` then gives the numbers again.

**Changes:** 24.2, 23.8's as-built note, 31.7, `tests/bucket_tree.rs`.

## 32.48 What a release build checks: the shipped-checker leg

**What it is.** Two findings that are one question.
- Gate 3's dry run could not find the "shipped-checker leg" that G98's and G118's done-when name. 8.3 lets a shipped release build run only the cheap checker groups, 1 and 4, after each operation, and 28.4 asks for a leg of the determinism matrix in that configuration whose hashes equal the full-checker legs'. 28.13's as-built note said the golden step "in release as shipped" was that leg.
- Workshop, the first program to run the kernel in a browser, turned a part by 35 degrees (13 November 2026). Its debug and test builds refused the turn as `NotCertifiedYet`, since the checker cannot certify planes off the integers until G134. Its release builds, the browser's WebAssembly among them, returned the body.

The question: may an operation's result be accepted in one build and refused in another? And what must a release build of the kernel check?

**Found.**
- **There was no shipped-checker leg.** `geom-test` depended on `geom-check` with its `full` feature on, and every kernel crate's tests depend on `geom-test`. Cargo gives a crate one set of features in one build, so `cargo tree -e features -i geom-check` showed `full` on in the test build of every kernel crate, `geom-bench`'s benchmarks included. The step "Every golden in release as shipped" ran the full checker. No build in continuous integration ran an operation with the cheap groups, and 28.13's note was wrong.
- **The corpus could not have shown it.** Its transforms are a move, a mirror and a quarter turn of an exact box.
- **The difference was written into the operation.** `Level::of_build()` is read in one place, `geom-make`'s check of a result (`build.rs`, `finish`). For a primitive as written, and for an exact transform of an exact body, a result that fails is a defect (`ResultInvalid`): the operation has by construction what groups 2 and 3 would prove. For a transform that rounds a corner or moves geometry by a deviation, and for an exact transform of a body already less than exact, it has no such proof, the refusal `NotCertifiedYet` is an ordinary answer, and only groups 2 and 3 give it. A shipped build ran neither, so it returned every such body. Some are worse than uncertified: a thin prism halved rounds one corner onto another, and the full set reports two edges of a face that meet (`geom-make/tests/not_certified.rs`).
- **Seen in a build that really checks as shipped** (release, `geom-test` built alone without the full checker, `tests/shipped.rs`): before the change the hinge was returned, recorded Certified within 2 steps, and the sweep below stopped at a box moved and then turned 35 degrees, returned with a pair of faces the full set reports as unchecked.

| Option | What it means |
| --- | --- |
| A. Every group after every operation in every build | The simplest rule. It ends 8.3's cheap level for every operation of every program, though group 3 is quadratic in faces and in shells, to fix two routes of one crate |
| B. Every group in every build wherever the checker's verdict is the operation's answer | Two routes of `geom-make` today. 8.3's levels stay, no interface changes, and a shipped build pays for the full set only on a result that is less than exact |
| C. Every result records the level that checked it, and the caller chooses the level | The level becomes an input, so two builds agree by definition. But a caller can then ask for a body principle 4 forbids, every program must learn the question, and `OpContext` and every operation's output change |
| D. Leave the kernel, and have each program ask `query.validity` for the full set | What Workshop did the same day. The kernel still answers differently by build |

**Decided 2026-11-13, under the standing rule to decide by research (the chapters, and the measurements below): no, and B.**
- **Principle 5 names no build.** "The same inputs give bit-identical outputs on every supported target and thread count." Chapter 21 runs debug and release as legs of that one promise; a build mode is not an input. 28.4 says it of the checker by name: the shipped leg's hashes "must equal those of the full-checker leg. That proves the checker never changes a result." And 28.13 has already ruled on one such difference: the check once charged the operation's budget at a level that differs by build, "so a budget could stop an operation in a test build and not in a shipped one", and that was fixed as a defect.
- **Principle 4 needs the full set exactly where the operation has no proof.** "Every operation returns a model that passes the validity checker, or an error." 8.3's cheap level keeps that promise where a result passes groups 2 and 3 by construction, and the full-checker builds of continuous integration are there to find a defect in the construction (28.4: the checker "catches a skipped check or a broken invariant"). It breaks the promise where a body the full set refuses is an expected outcome.
- **So a build's level may decide how much of a proof is repeated, never an answer.** An operation checks at `Level::of_build()` only a result that passes groups 2 and 3 by construction. Where the checker's verdict is the operation's answer, every group runs in every build.
- **A is more than the finding needs, C moves a kernel promise onto its callers, and D is not a fix.** C stays open to a later decision: nothing here closes it.

**What a release build of the kernel checks.**
1. After every operation, groups 1 and 4 (8.3).
2. After an operation whose result does not pass groups 2 and 3 by construction, those two as well. Today that is a transform or a pattern whose result rounded or moved by a deviation, or whose input was less than exact (`geom-make`'s `Unpassed::Rounded` and `Unpassed::Inherited`). Every later operation that can return a body it has not proved is on this side until its piece shows otherwise, and says which in its module header.
3. On an open and on request, what the caller asks: `trust::open` and `query.validity` take the level as an input, so they answer the same in every build already.

A result recorded Exact from inputs that came from an operation or a checked open (24.7) is the only kind checked at the build's level. If the full set refuses one, that is a defect, reported `ResultInvalid` by a test build and returned by a shipped one; the shipped-checker leg and the full-checker legs together are what must find it first.

**Measured.** `geom-test`'s `tests/shipped.rs`, in counts that are the same on every machine.
- *The answers.* Every primitive at three sizes and places, under ten transforms (a move, a mirror, a quarter turn, a doubling, a tripling, turns by 35 degrees and by the 3-4-5 angle, scales by 3/2, 1/2 and 7/3 along one axis), and each body returned transformed again by the same ten: 1,590 operations. With the full checker and with the shipped one alike: 1,263 bodies returned, each passing the full set when asked; 123 refusals waiting for G134 and 96 for G177; 108 refusals that are not the checker's, which no level touches (a scale along one axis of a curved body, for one); no defect.
- *The cost.* The full set in place of the cheap groups, in the checker's work units (23.10: one per row or pair visited), on the 30 bodies of that sweep's first step that are less than exact (the twelve round primitives moved, all eighteen primitives scaled by 3/2): 4,320 units against 2,088, a factor of 2.07. The largest of each kind:

| Body | Cheap groups | Full set |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Box (6 faces) | 227 | 520 |
| Prism (6 faces) | 227 | 536 |
| Cylinder | 48 | 79 |
| Cone | 27 | 46 |
| Sphere | 6 | 13 |
| Torus | 40 | 54 |

A work unit of group 2 or 3 is a certified test of geometry, which costs far more than a row of links, so instructions tell more. Callgrind's instruction counts, taken once (Valgrind 3.22 on x86-64 Linux, rustc 1.99.0, a release build with the shipped checker; a probe that is not in the tree, so these are a record, not a gate), over the same 30 bodies by kind: first the check alone, then four transforms of each body (a move, a quarter turn, a doubling and a scale by 3/2) before the change, when a shipped build checked them with the cheap groups, and after it.

| Bodies | Check, cheap groups | Check, full set | Four transforms of each, before | After | Rise |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 3 boxes | 105,566 | 1,800,396 | 7,189,110 | 13,975,301 | 94% |
| 3 prisms | 106,013 | 1,963,175 | 7,189,449 | 14,589,860 | 103% |
| 6 cylinders | 53,298 | 553,608 | 6,852,106 | 8,854,604 | 29% |
| 6 cones | 37,434 | 260,550 | 5,467,548 | 6,360,536 | 16% |
| 6 spheres | 16,386 | 24,084 | 2,498,267 | 2,529,107 | 1% |
| 6 tori | 47,702 | 765,468 | 6,003,735 | 8,879,667 | 48% |
| All 30 | 366,399 | 5,367,281 | 35,200,215 | 55,189,075 | 57% |

So in a shipped build a transform of a six-faced body that is less than exact about doubles, some 570,000 instructions more each, and a transform of an exact body costs what it did. Group 3 compares pairs of faces, so the rise grows with the body: about linear in faces for the cheap groups, quadratic for the full set. That is the price of B on a large body that is less than exact, and it is the price of knowing the body is valid, which nothing else in the operation establishes. Every test build has always paid it, and so have the count benchmarks, which build with the full checker. G134 and G177 widen what the checker certifies; they do not remove the check.

**With G134 (the Phase 4 train, 13 November 2026).** The decision does not move. What the full set answers does, and `tests/shipped.rs` holds the numbers below in place of the ones above. A rounded flat face now stands for its star of triangles (8.3), so the full set passes the Workshop hinge and every build returns it, recorded Certified within two steps; the thin prism halved is still refused, with two edges of a face that meet, and waits for G417. In the sweep the 123 results that waited for G134 are returned, and the fifteen of them that are a primitive's first transform are transformed again, so it is 1,740 operations: 1,536 bodies returned, each passing the full set; 96 refusals waiting for G177; 108 that are not the checker's; no defect; the same with the full checker and the shipped one. On the 30 bodies the full set is 4,275 work units against the cheap groups' 2,088, a factor of 2.05, and the largest of each kind are a box 522, a prism 522, a cylinder 80, a cone 46, a sphere 12 and a torus 54, the cheap groups' as in the table. G134 reads each pair of flat faces on their outlines, and settles a pair one of whose faces keeps to one side of the other's plane by a sign for each of its corners, as it does every pair of a box's faces. The instruction counts above were taken before G134 and are left as taken.

**Gate 3's reading of "the shipped-checker leg"** (G118's fourth condition; the dry run's row 4b). The leg is `kernel.yml`'s step "The replay corpus and the transform sweep with the checker as shipped" in the job `test` (six native targets, release) and its twin in the job `wasm` (wasmtime and Node): `cargo test --locked --release -p geom-test --no-default-features --test shipped --test determinism`. It is not the golden step named "as shipped", which builds without overflow checks and runs the full checker. Its evidence, test by test:
- `shipped.rs::the_build_checks_at_the_level_its_switches_say`: the leg ran the cheap groups, and no dependency turned the full set back on;
- `determinism.rs::replay_corpus_golden` and `shipped.rs::the_replay_corpus_gives_the_stores_digest`: the corpus's digest is the full-checker legs';
- `shipped.rs::every_body_the_replay_corpus_makes_passes_the_full_checker` and `every_returned_body_passes_the_full_checker_and_the_answers_are_the_full_builds`: nothing is returned that the full set refuses, and the answers are the full-checker build's;
- `shipped.rs::kernel_yml_runs_the_shipped_checker_leg_natively_and_on_wasm32`: the leg cannot be dropped from the workflow unseen.

**Left for later, each small.**
- The replay corpus has no case that rounds. A `rounded` family (a turn that is not a quarter turn, a scale that rounds, a move of a round primitive) would put the two routes into the one digest every leg holds. It moves the golden `geom-test/replay-corpus`, so it needs its record in the store (32.12, 32.54).
- `geom-bench`'s count benchmarks build with the full checker, through the same dependency, so `make/*` measures a test build's check, not a shipped one's. Whether the count gate should measure the shipped configuration is 23.13's to say.
- Workshop's own full check of a turned part (its piece A25) now repeats what the kernel does, and can go.
- The step costs one more build of `geom-test` per release leg; if that weighs on the matrix, the leg can move to a job of its own.

Reopen this if a measured operation on real models spends most of its time in groups 2 and 3 on bodies less than exact; C is then the option to weigh again, with the level as a recorded input.

**Changes:** 8.3 and its as-built note, 28.4's and 28.13's as-built notes, 31.7; `geom-make` (`build.rs`, `Unpassed::level`), `geom-test` (the feature `full-checker`, `tests/shipped.rs`), `kernel.yml`'s two steps, AGENTS.md's gate table.

## 32.49 No patent searches

**Decided by the owner, 2026-11-13 (17:24 NZ): no patent searches.** The owner's words: "What patents? Just use them. Should have lots of free stuff to use like solid works or parts of things rewritten then it's not patents". Asked later that day (about 19:00 NZ) whether this also lifts the rules for patents already known, the owner answered: "Yes, lift them".

What it changed:
- No work stops or waits for a patent, known or not. The kernel's methods come from the free sources it already uses, existing code first: solidcore, SMLib and other permissive sources, ported to Rust (the kernel's AGENTS.md).
- 32.7's recommendation is set aside. 29.17's table of patents already known stays as a record only, with each patent's expiry date.
- The board's patent pieces are removed with every need on them: owner actions G73, G74, G119, G122, G182, G183, G234 to G237, G286, G287, G310, G311, G356, G383, G445 and G446, and piece G115. G309 and G313 stay for their non-patent work: chapter 13's legal check of Lifted Surfacing's dependencies, and the OpenSubdiv licence review (30.4, 30.5). The owner asked on 2026-11-14 for the rows to go rather than stay as closed records.
- The risk that remains is R16 (31): a method someone has patented may be used.

**Changes:** this record; 32.7; chapters 11, 13, 16, 19, 26, 27, 29, 30 and 31, and `30-build-order.json`; the root AGENTS.md and README.md; GLOSSARY.md.

## 32.51 Shape identity: the same shape under any placement (G484)

**What it is.** Spare Construct gives one part number (a mark) to every piece of the same shape, wherever and however it is placed. Nothing in the kernel answered that. The content hash (23.8) is over name order with the names included; `BodyVersion::geometry_id` leaves the names out but keeps name order and where the body sits; and placed instances (G145) match only when the program already knew they were one definition. The design for G483 proposed a comparison that ignores names, order and placement and is exact, and asked for three calls: what "the same shape" means, the canonical form and its scheme, and the integer widths it needs on corners where three planes meet.

**Decided 2026-11-14 (G484).**
- **The same shape means equal under a rotation and a move only.** Two bodies are the same shape when a proper rigid motion, with no mirror and no scale, carries the stored boundary of one exactly onto the stored boundary of the other. Exact, with no tolerance anywhere: "equal within 1 mm" is not transitive, so marks would depend on the order parts were compared in. Names, roles, history, attributes and how the boundary is cut into faces are ignored: neighbouring faces on one surface count as one face, an edge that only separates them is dropped, and so are a vertex left in the middle of a straight edge and the seams of closed faces. Holes, slots, copes, cut-backs, bevels and weld preparations are faces of the body, so they count. A mirror image is a different shape, reported apart as `MirrorOnly`, and each body's `mirror_id`, the id of its mirror image, pairs the opposite hands; a part with a mirror plane of its own is turned onto its mirror by a rotation, so it comes out `Same` with no special rule. A bigger part is never the same. Material, finish and every other property are the program's to compare beside the id.
- **The canonical form and `ShapeScheme v1` as G483's design gives them.** A key of exact numbers that no motion changes (counts by kind, squared edge lengths, G102's area vectors and six times the volume, the radii of analytic faces) is compared first, and different keys prove different shapes. Then the id: from each candidate anchor (three corners in a row round a loop, kept only where their triangle is least), every corner is written as its three squared distances to the anchor and an `orient3d` side, the corners are sorted, edges, loops, faces, shells and regions are written by those places, each candidate is encoded in canonical CBOR, and the least bytes are kept. The id is SHA-256, with G86's encoder and hash (32.20), over `SKG ShapeScheme v1`, a zero byte and those bytes, in the manner of `NameScheme` (32.19). The merge rules, the anchor rule, the order and the encoding are all part of v1: a program stores the scheme beside each id, and a change to any of them is a new scheme, never a quiet change to v1. `shape_match` compares the canonical bytes themselves, not the hashes, and returns the exact motion it found, never one of 32.14's approximations. A body the scheme does not cover is `NotCanonical` or `NotCompared`, never `Different` and never guessed.
- **The wide arithmetic is accepted into 6.2 on 32.29's terms.** A corner where three planes meet is a 288-bit numerator over a 248-bit weight (6.2). The squared distance of two such corners is about 1,076 bits over 992, and the side test about 1,120 bits. 19.10 does not compare distances on implicit corners and commits them instead; this query cannot commit, because that rounds. So these widths join 6.2's list, for this query only and off every boolean's path, with a greatest common divisor on `Wide<N>` in `geom-num` to put fractions in lowest terms, so that equal values have equal bytes. Each meets what 32.29 asked: a written derivation in its module header, a certificate, tests at every coordinate ±(2^40 − 1), a filter so the wide path runs only where narrower integers cannot hold the values, and instruction counts natively and in WebAssembly under the count gate. The divisor is fixed-size, checked and allocation-free like the rest of `geom-num`, which is why it is ours and not a crate's; the big-integer crates stay oracles (29.3), and G483 fuzzes it against `num-bigint`.
- **Where the pieces sit.** G483 (flat solids, with corners on the grid or where three planes meet) is in phase 4 but does not gate G156: gate 4 proves the exact core, and this is a query a program needs. G485 (analytic bodies: plane, cylinder, cone, sphere and torus faces, and line, circle and ellipse edges) is in phase 7 after G248, and does not gate G284 for the same reason. G484 is this record, so G483 needs nothing from an owner.

The price falls on Spare Construct, not the kernel. A copy rotated so that its corners were rounded to the grid, or its planes committed, is honestly a different exact shape and answers `Different`. So a fabricated part is modelled in its own frame and placed by a transform, as 32.14's option C already asks, and a connection's cuts are worked out in the part's frame.

Why: one mark for two shapes cuts a wrong part, while two marks for one shape cost only a spare drawing. An exact comparison that answers `NotCompared` for whatever it cannot settle only ever errs the safe way, and the kernel's exact corners are what make it possible.

**Changes:** this record; 6.2's list; 8.1 and 10.2; 30.4, 30.5 and 30.7, and `30-build-order.json` (G483, G484 and G485).

## 32.52 Fuzzing as tests in the suite

**Decided by the owner, 2026-11-15.** The owner's words: "we're over fuzzing... do it more like solidcore's testing"; earlier the same morning, "Only fuzz what needs to be"; and on the tests themselves, that they should go after the extreme inputs that break things, as solidcore's do, and may take "slightly longer if needed, a few more seconds if it helps".

What solidcore does (tangency-labs/solidcore at commit `a3a8244566081e0777284dc182f77a91bb1a456d`, `crates/solidcore/tests/support/panic_fuzz.rs` and the tests that use it): its fuzz tests are seeded random tests inside the normal test suite, mixing a fixed list of hostile values (NaN, infinities, huge, tiny, zero) into generated cases, with case counts sized so each family takes about two seconds optimised; they run on every pull request, and each finding is saved as a case every run replays.

What it changed here:
- Every `geom-fuzz` harness's seeded test (`geom_fuzz::seeded`) draws half its cases hostile: solidcore's `HOSTILE` values, ported, integer words at their domains' ends, repeated and nearly repeated runs of bytes (coincident and near-coincident values, zero lengths and areas, slivers), evenly spaced words (collinear and coplanar values) and fills of one byte, each cut to every length. Each harness's count is sized by the release suite's measured time for about two seconds of an optimised build, and up to ten where two would give too few cases; a build with debug assertions runs the first tenth.
- Every saved corpus and crash file of every target replays on every run (`geom-fuzz`'s `tests/attack_g14.rs`).
- `kernel-fuzz-campaign.yml`, the pull requests' libFuzzer and ClusterFuzzLite jobs in `kernel-fuzz.yml`, `.clusterfuzzlite/` and the tests that held only their shape are gone, with the planted violations `trip libfuzzer` and `trip clusterfuzzlite`. The Kani proofs stay: they are proofs, not fuzzing, and still run only where the crate graph says a change reaches them.
- A gate or piece condition that named a fuzz campaign, hours of fuzzing or clean nights of fuzzing reads instead: the harness's seeded test passes in the suite on the gate's commit, and its saved cases replay. Records of gates already passed stay as they were, as facts about what was run.

Why: a campaign re-fuzzed code that had not changed for hours each night, and most of its time went after coverage had stopped growing (the last campaign, run 43596836655: orient2d's coverage stopped at its first minute, of five hours). Seeded hostile cases in the suite give the same answer on every machine, run where the change is, and keep every finding.

**Changes:** this record; 28.5 and 28.17; `30-build-order.json`'s done-when texts that named campaigns; `geom-fuzz` (`seeded`, its tests' counts, its README and header); `geom-test` (`fuzz_scope`, `trip`, `kernel-quality fuzz-scope`, the attack tests); `kernel-fuzz.yml` and `kernel-release.yml`.

## 32.53 Testing once per change: no timed runs, the merge queue

**Decided by the owner, 2026-11-15.** The owner's testing principles, like solidcore's (tangency-labs/solidcore's `.github/workflows/ci.yml` at commit `4f850b0ad9a876272d447876d8bf7844e2297f37`: a merge queue, a push to main that skips what the queue tested on the identical tree, and the tests run once, optimised), and the orders that came with them:

| Order | What it says | What it changed |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Principle 2 | A change tests only what it can reach (the crate graph); a file outside every crate, the merge queue and a release run everything; nothing reruns on code that has not changed: no timed runs, and a push to main skips what the merge queue already tested on the same tree | No workflow has a `schedule:` trigger but the pieces bot and the cache pruning, which test nothing (`geom-test`'s `tests/attack_g10.rs::no_workflow_runs_on_a_timer`). `kernel-certificates.yml` proves a problem again only when it, its scripts or this workflow (which pins every tool's image) changed, on a pull request or a push to main. `kernel-quality.yml`'s full mutation run is a push to main's: every mutant of the files it changed, and of every file whose ledger entries are in a function its diff declares (`kernel-quality mutants-files`); Miri runs over the crates the push reaches. `kernel-oracles.yml` runs the slow fixtures, the fetched corpora and the outside oracles on a push to main, for the crates it reaches |
| Delete the timed runs, 10:40 | Timed runs are deleted, not switched off, so they can never come back; what they checked that the suite does not moves into the suite as quick seeded tests | `kernel-nightly.yml` is gone. The predicates' verification suite and attack tests and the kinds' and NURBS laws were already seeded tests of the suite; the proven maths functions' ten million inputs per function against the big-integer reference became a seeded sample of 4,000 per function (1,000 with debug assertions on) in `geom-num`'s `tests/math_oracle.rs`, about 2 s per function on one core, beside the 400-input prefix every build ran; CORE-MATH's full worst-case lists stay an ignored test to run by hand, the suite keeping a sample of each. Its container and download jobs moved to `kernel-oracles.yml` |
| Principle 3 | On each push to a pull request: format and lints, then the reachable tests once, optimised with overflow checks and debug assertions on, on Linux; the other platforms and one plain debug run in the merge queue, once per change, just before it lands | `kernel.yml`'s job `linux` runs on a pull request; `test`, `suite`, `wasm`, `wasm-suite` and `big-endian` in the merge queue (`merge_group`), with `suite`'s one debug run on Linux x86-64; a push to main whose tree the queue passed runs nothing again. Main's ruleset (`.github/rulesets/protect-main.json`) adds the merge queue, merging with a merge commit, and requires "Kernel checks pass", "Kernel quality checks pass" and "Piece is checked out", each its workflow's last job, which reports on every pull request and queued change (`geom-test`'s `tests/queue_wiring.rs`). `geom-fuzz`'s seeded tests run a tenth of their cases only in an unoptimised build, no longer in every build with debug assertions on |
| Principle 5 | Mutation testing and coverage once per change, on the lines it changed, on GitHub, when the change is ready to land | `kernel-quality.yml` runs mutants in the diff and coverage when a pull request is ready for review, never on a push to a draft, and in the merge queue unless they passed on the same tree; the diff-mode judge and its ledger are unchanged |
| Principle 6 | Gates count one clean run on the gate's commit, never nights or days; a gate's timing runs once on that commit | The done-when texts of G10, G18, G21, G34, G40, G69, G107, G118, G154 to G156, G161, G178, G214, G308, G353, G381, G440 to G443, G449, G451 and G452 say so; 28.17's tiers and machines and 23.13's wall-clock rule likewise; the reference machine is the owner's home desktop, available at any time |
| Nothing waits on the owner, 10:35 | Wherever a gate's done-when, a gate record or the process text says the owner accepts, approves, reads or acts, the main session records the decision with its reason | The done-when of every gate not yet passed (G118, G156, G181, G233, G284, G308, G355, G382, G406, G444) and 30.1's gate lane say the main session records the result, with its reason and the CI runs that show it; so does 23.13's acceptance of a deliberate rise. Records of gates already passed (32.43, 32.46) stay as they were |

Why: a timed run re-tests code that has not changed, and finds nothing the change's own run did not; running every platform on every push to a draft spends runners on code that will change again. Each check now runs once, where the change is, and the merge queue is the one place every platform sees a change before it lands.

**Changes:** this record; 23.13, 28 (28.5, 28.8, 28.10, 28.12, 28.13, 28.16, 28.17), 30.1 and `30-build-order.json`'s done-when texts; `kernel.yml`, `kernel-quality.yml`, `kernel-certificates.yml`, `kernel-oracles.yml` (new), `kernel-release.yml`, `pieces.yml` and `dco.yml`; `.github/rulesets/protect-main.json` (new); `geom-test` (`mutants::push_files`, `kernel-quality mutants-files` and `miri-packages --changed`, the workflow tests, `tests/queue_wiring.rs`); `geom-fuzz` (`seeded::for_this_build`, `build.rs`); `geom-num`'s `tests/math_oracle.rs`.

## 32.54 The golden store check reads the store's record, not a review

**Decided by the main session, 2026-11-15**, under the rule that every decision is the main session's, recorded with its reason.

**What it is.** The job "Golden store" (`kernel-golden.yml`; `geom-test`'s `golden::diff`) asked for an approving review from someone other than the pull request's author whenever a stored answer moved, and for every other edit to an existing golden and every release of an edition. One account opens every pull request and no kernel owner reviews them, so that review never came: the check was red on every change that moved a stored answer, and the red carried no signal. What a reviewer would have read is the change's history record in the store, so the check now reads that record, all of it.

**What the check requires now.**
- Every stored answer that moves carries exactly one new history record in the store: the old digest (`sha256`), its `kind`, its `piece` and `why`, as #885's record on the replay corpus does.
- The kind is one the store's rules allow for that golden (`Golden::allows`). A golden that covers operation versions records only `corpus` (its inputs or their encoding changed, no result did), because fixing a bug or improving a result makes a new operation version, a new golden beside the old (26.2, 28.13). A golden with no operation versions, which every golden is until the first Stable release, may record `bug`, `improvement` or `corpus`. The store's reader refuses a kind the golden does not allow anywhere in its history.
- A move with no record, with two, with a record whose `sha256` is not the old digest, with a kind the golden does not allow, or with an empty piece or reason fails the check.
- Everything else the check refused it still refuses: any change to a frozen edition; an edition removed, renamed or moved; a golden removed or its key changed; a record added without a move; history rewritten or removed; two open editions.
- Releasing an edition, which also waited on a review, is refused in a pull request until the first Stable release (G482) writes the rule a release is held to: nothing a machine can check says yet what a release must hold, and a frozen edition never changes again.
- An edit that moves no answer (where a golden is checked, the file it pins, what it covers), which also waited on a review, passes: `golden check`, in the same job, proves each golden is checked where the store says and each pinned file has the store's digest.
- The job prints each moved answer with its record. The main session's merge record names each one; that part is the main session's, not the code's.
- Every step of the job runs on a review as on the pull request itself, so a review's run gives the same answer. Before, a review's run skipped `golden check` and could show green beside the pull request's own result.

The fixtures' job (`fixture diff`, 28.16) still asked for a kernel owner's review of a changed expectation when this decision was made. 32.55 holds it to the fixtures' own records the same way and drops the workflow's review trigger, so a review now starts no run of either job.

Why: the record is what a reviewer would have read, and the check now holds every part of it on every pull request, the same way each time. Nothing the old rule refused outright is accepted, two things it let through with a review (a release, and a record of a kind the golden's operation versions rule out) are now refused, and a red now means a record is missing or wrong.

**Changes:** this record; 32.12's row in the first table; 28.13; 32.43's row for the job `store` (its end-to-end test renamed); the glossary's golden store; `kernel-golden.yml`'s job `store`; `geom-test`'s `golden::diff`, `golden::store` (`Golden::allows`, and each kind's name), the `golden` binary (no review options), the store's `about` text and `tests/attack_g19.rs`.

## 32.55 The fixtures check reads each changed expectation's record, not a review

**Decided by the main session, 2026-11-15**, under the rule that every decision is the main session's, recorded with its reason: the fixtures' half of 32.54.

**What it is.** The job "Fixtures" (`kernel-golden.yml`; `geom-test`'s `fixture::diff`) let an expectation of the open edition change only with a history record holding what it was, and then also asked for a kernel owner's approving review on the pull request's head commit; a change to a `Known` fixture's wanted result asked for the review alone. That review never comes, for the reason 32.54 gives: one account opens every pull request and no kernel owner reviews them. What a reviewer would have read is the change's history record in the fixture, so the check now reads that record, all of it, and no check of the kernel reads a review.

**What the check requires now.**
- Every changed expectation of the open edition carries a new history record in its fixture at its key (edition and operation versions): what it was (`was`), its `kind`, its `piece` and `why`.
- The kind is one the fixture's rules allow for that change (`Change::allows`). `corpus` (the inputs or their encoding changed, no result did) fits only a success whose measures and counts stayed, so that its hash alone moved: a fixture's log never changes, so only the canonical encoding of what it gives can. `bug` and `improvement` fit only in `pre-edition`: once the first Stable release starts operation versions (G482), a fixed or improved result is a new operation version, a new expectation beside the old (26.2, 28.13), as a golden that covers operation versions records only `corpus`.
- A `Known` fixture's changed wanted result carries a record on the same terms: the old wanted result, at the log's own operation versions in the open edition.
- A change with no record, a record of another old value, a kind the rules do not allow, or an empty piece or reason fails, and so do more new records than changed values.
- Everything else the check refused it still refuses: a removed fixture; a changed log, source or recorded fix; `Pass` become `Known`; a work count that moves alone; a removed expectation; any change to a frozen edition; history rewritten. Adding a fixture or an expectation, recording a fix for the first time, a change of tier, and `Known` moved to `Pass` with what it wanted still need no record.
- The job prints each changed expectation with its record. The main session's merge record names each one.
- No review is read. The review rule (`golden::diff::approved`), the step that fetched a pull request's reviews and its reviewers' permissions, the options of `fixture diff` that took them, the workflow's `pull_request_review` trigger and its token's `pull-requests: read` are gone, so a review starts no run of either job.

Why: as in 32.54, the record is what a reviewer would have read, and the check now holds every part of it on every pull request, the same way each time. Nothing the old rule refused is accepted, two things it let through with a review (a record of a kind the rules rule out, and a wanted result changed with no record) are now refused, and a red means a record is missing or wrong.

**Changes:** this record; 32.54's note on the fixtures' job; 32.43's row for the job `fixtures` (a new end-to-end test); 32.48's note on a `rounded` family; 28.16's as-built note; `kernel-golden.yml` (its header, trigger and token, and the job `fixtures`); `geom-test`'s `fixture::diff`, `fixture::Change::allows`, the `fixture` binary (no review options), `golden::diff` (the review rule removed), `tests/fixtures.rs`, `tests/fixture_tool.rs` and `tests/attack_g19.rs`; MAP.json; the kernel README's row for `kernel-golden.yml`, `kernel.yml`'s header, and the notes of the determinism goldens in `geom-num`, `geom-math` and `geom-run`, which still said a kernel owner approves a golden's change.

**Readings the gate took**, each decided by the main session with its reason (32.53: nothing waits on an owner):
- **The gate's commit** is 301153516, main's head when the evidence was collected, its push runs all green (the merge gates). Decided by the main session, 2026-11-15, and accepted. #898 touched no kernel file, so the kernel's tree there is 916425ec9's, whose push runs tested all 95 crates. A workflow that a push skips because the push changed nothing it reads is shown by its run on the last commit with the same files: here 916425ec9. Why: principle 2 (32.53) runs nothing again on code that has not changed, so the gate's one clean run of the kernel is the run of the commit that last changed it, and a run on a later commit with the same tree would test the same bytes.
- **The timing stands**: the reference runner's record on `kernel-timings`, be3ba857, on 7693eaf38, is the gate commit's times. Decided by the main session, 2026-11-15. Why: since 7693eaf38 the only changed code the timed programs link is geom-surface's `cache.rs` (#894), and no catalogued benchmark reaches it (only `Offset::to_nurbs` and `to_nurbs_within` call it), so no timed code changed, and principle 6 (32.53) times a commit again only when timed code changes.
- **"Every primitive and transform passes ... all four checker groups"** means that every primitive and transform either returns a body that passes all four groups or refuses with a typed error that names what it waits for: kind `Unsupported`, detail `MakeDetail::NotCertifiedYet { piece }` and remedy `AwaitPiece { piece }`, the piece G134 (planes off the integers) or G177 (the curved checker). 32.48's sweep counts them: of 1,590 operations, 1,263 bodies are returned, each passing the full set; 123 refusals wait for G134 and 96 for G177; 108 refusals are not the checker's. Why: a body the checker cannot yet certify is never returned, so the condition holds of everything returned, and a refusal that names its piece is the contract's answer, not a failure of it.
- **"The shipped-checker leg"** is 32.48's: `kernel.yml`'s step "The replay corpus and the transform sweep with the checker as shipped", natively and on wasm32, not the golden step named "as shipped". Why: 32.48 decided it.
- **"Reference mode"** is one leg, Linux x86-64, in `kernel-golden.yml`'s `Fixtures` job. Why: reference mode turns off the caches and the generated filters (23.13, G106), so a second target adds no path that the fast legs' equality across targets does not already hold. That job runs only when its path filter matches, so the gate's tree must show it run, as run 43688164982 does.
- **"Proven in Verus"**: `geom-topo/verus/lib.rs` models the Euler operators, and the lockstep test of 100,000 steps ties the model to the editor; no token-for-token twin does, unlike geom-num's. Kani's `kani-topo` harnesses check the arenas and the builder themselves. Why: G111's done-when asks for the model and the lockstep test, which is what was built and runs.
- **"The sequence harness's seeded test passes in the suite on the gate's commit, and its saved cases replay"** is `sequence_seeded` green in `kernel.yml`'s optimised Linux run on the gate's tree, with `sequence_fuzz` and `attack_g14` replaying the stored files. The draft's "seven consecutive nights of sequence fuzzing" and its "whole campaign green on the gate's commit" read the same way: the sequence target's seeded test, and every other target's, green in the suite on the gate's commit; the campaign runs of 12 to 14 November are history. Why: 32.52 replaced campaigns with the seeded tests, and 32.53's principle 6 counts one clean run on the gate's commit, never nights; the done-when's words are 32.53's.
- **"Records the gate commit's times"** is one record on `kernel-timings`: every catalogued benchmark, natively, at 1 and 8 threads, timed once on a commit whose timed code is the gate's, and again only when timed code changes (32.53, principle 6). Why: the condition names no engine, and 23.1's shares are for native builds (32.16); the browser timings in G107's done-when are asserted against their shares by G451 and gated by G452, so they are left to those pieces. The runner does time them on this machine: a smoke run (`--any-time --cases '^topo/'`) timed Chrome 157, Firefox 137.0 and WebKit 18.4 (Playwright 1.52.0) with no failures; WebKit stands in for Safari (31.7). `nightly.sh --push` refuses `--engines`, so the record was committed with the script's own README and commit subject, not by `--push`. The runner reports without blocking until G452.
- **The machine.** The record was taken on the home desktop (Intel Core i7-3770, 4 cores and 8 threads, Windows 10 Pro), which 32.53 names the reference machine, not 32.16's spare tower. `machine.ps1` finds one efficiency class there, so the 8-thread figures are 4 cores with hyperthreads. Why: the record names the processor in its `machine` line, and its figures are not read against 23.1's shares, which no benchmark measures yet (every share is "not measured yet" until G451).
- **Which pieces are "code pieces"** for the standard bar's fuzz and benchmark rows: those whose code a shipped build links (geom-topo, geom-op, geom-check, geom-name, geom-format, geom-make and engine-geometry), so G80 to G92, G94 to G96, G100 to G102, G104, G105, G108 and G109. G93 is a specification. G97 to G99, G103, G106, G107 and G110 to G117 build the tools behind the bar (geom-test, geom-fuzz, geom-bench, CI, the Verus and Kani models, the playbooks) and are held by their own tests and the gates they run. Why: 30.1's bar is for "every piece that adds kernel code"; a tool's fuzz target or benchmark would measure the tool, not the kernel.
- **A benchmark or fuzz target in another crate's group.** Gate 2 let one target or benchmark "serve several pieces of a crate when it reaches each piece's code". Here `make/*`, `batch/run_items` and `query/validity` serve G82 to G85, which live in geom-op and geom-check, and the `make` and `sequence` targets serve G83 to G85 and G88. Decided: "of a crate" widens to "reaching each piece's code, shown by coverage", as #821 measured. Why: an operation's contract, budget and checker run only inside an operation, so a benchmark or target of the operation is the one that reaches them as a program does.
- **G100, the facade**, has the `contract` fuzz target and the count benchmarks `facade/lookup` (an operation found by name in `OPERATIONS`, its schema read) and `facade/dispatch` (`make.box` found by name and run through the facade's re-export), merged by #857. Why: the bar asks every code piece for a benchmark, and a dispatch by name is the facade's own work.
- **G91's "24-hour clean fuzz"** reads, under 32.52, as the `cbor` harness's seeded test passing in the suite with its saved cases replaying; G91's done-when now says so. `cbor_seeded` passed in `kernel.yml` run 43688164956. History only: `cbor_fuzz` ran five hours green on each campaign run of 12 to 14 November listed above. Why: 32.52 turns every condition that named hours of fuzzing into the seeded test.

**Found by the gate's full run, not a gate condition.** Miri's partition 2 in run 43696026626 ran for 3 hours 31 minutes (08:46 to 12:17 UTC), over three and a half hours and the run's last job to finish: 188 tests, 62 of them stopped at the 600 s budget. Work under way (the `slow-tests` lane) is cutting it.

## 32.58 Miri is aimed at what it can find

**Decided by the main session, 2026-11-16**, under the rule that every decision is the main session's, recorded with its reason. (32.57 is kept for Gate 4's record.)

**What it is.** Miri interprets the kernel's tests to find undefined behaviour. Every kernel crate forbids unsafe code: each crate's `Cargo.toml` sets `unsafe_code = "forbid"` in `[lints.rust]`, and no crate's `src` holds `unsafe` (`geom-test`, a development crate, names the word once, as a token its map reader skips). So Miri cannot find a fault in the kernel's own code. What it checks is the unsafe code of the libraries the kernel uses, and of the standard library, as the kernel drives them, and leaks and results that differ between machines: it found the `powi` in a test (#940). Meanwhile 1,179 of main's 3,443 rows in Miri's table sat at the 600 s budget, where Miri stops a test with no verdict or where a test was never timed; the Phase 5 train has 278 more tests over 120 s; and the pilot (#942) measured cutting tests down by hand at about 10 tests for 100,000 tokens, which is about 2.7 million tokens for the train and over 11 million for main, more than the week holds.

**What it decides.**
1. A guard holds that every kernel crate forbids unsafe code. The day a crate allows it, every test of that crate must finish under Miri again.
2. Miri runs every test it finishes. A test measured within the measuring run's stop gets a row with its real time; a test measured over it is listed as native-only with its measured time, in a table made from measurements and never edited by hand, in place of a 600 s row that pretends.
3. Each library with unsafe code that the kernel uses is named with a finishing test that drives it, and that list is guarded.
4. The cuts already made (#924, #934, #942) stay: more tests finishing is better.
5. No more lanes cut tests by hand for Miri; the three `intersect-miri` lanes planned are dropped.

This replaces 32.43's sentence "No test is ignored under Miri". Why: a test that Miri stops at its budget passed every step Miri interpreted and nothing more, and a row at the budget charged a partition 600 s for that. With no unsafe code in the kernel, the steps Miri can judge are the libraries' and the standard library's, and a test that finishes is a better witness of those than one that is stopped; the list of libraries says which test witnesses each. The table stops pretending, the 600 s rows leave every push, and the Phase 5 train's table can be written at once.

**How it is built.**
- **The guard (point 1)** is engine-geometry's scaffold check, rule `UnsafeNotForbidden` (`tests/scaffold/check.rs`; `the_repository_check_passes_today` on the repository; planted cases in `tests/scaffold/seeded.rs`, `unsafe_code_not_forbidden`, and `tests/attack_g09.rs`). It accepted a crate root's `#![forbid(unsafe_code)]` in place of the manifest's lint, and that attribute reaches the library only, not the crate's integration tests, benchmarks and examples, which Miri runs. It now asks for the manifest's lint, which reaches every target; the planted case is a crate whose root forbids unsafe code and whose manifest does not.
- **The table (point 2)** is geom-test's `src/miri-times.tsv`. A row's first field is the seconds Miri took to finish the test, always under the budget, or `native-only over N` (stopped after N s with no verdict) or `native-only N` (finished in N s, at or over the budget). `geom_test::miri::parse_table` refuses a row at or over the budget, a native-only mark with no measured time, a native-only mark of a time under the budget, a test in both states and a test listed twice, each with a planted case. Its one writer is `kernel-quality miri-times`, which reads a measuring run's nextest report (`junit.xml`) at a scale (CI seconds for one of the run's seconds) and refuses to write when the report shows a test failing or killed, naming each: a fault Miri found is read, never recorded as slow. `kernel_quality_workflow.rs` holds the file to the form the tool writes. The measuring profile is `miri-measure` in `.config/nextest.toml`: two periods of one minute, a stopped test counted as timed out.
- **The run.** Each Miri partition lists the run's tests under Miri (`cargo miri nextest list`) and `kernel-quality miri-filter --list` fails on any test with no row, so a new test is measured before it lands; the filterset names the partition's finishing tests one by one, so native-only tests run in every native suite and not under Miri. The partitions are sized from the finishing rows only, and `PARTITIONS` is the fewest within the bound of 900 s a partition (`geom_test::miri::fewest`, held by `kernel_quality_workflow.rs`).
- **The libraries (point 3)** are `geom_test::miri::UNSAFE_LIBRARIES`: every registry library whose own sources hold `unsafe`, among the normal dependencies of the kernel crates (what ships) and the registry crates the kernel's tests name directly, each with a test that drives it. `kernel_quality_workflow.rs` holds that each named test has a finishing row (geom-pool's tests have no rows: Miri runs all of them, under Tree Borrows) and, reading `cargo metadata` and each library's sources as Rust tokens, that every such library is named.

**Measured.** One measuring run on main's 870022ada, on a development machine with the workflow's nightly and flags, a 120 s stop a test (profile `miri-measure`), scaled x 2.3 to a CI runner's seconds as #924's rows were: main's 1,178 tests at the budget and the 6 of its tests that had no row (9 more are ignored under Miri and need none; one row named a renamed test and was dropped), 1,184 in all, plus the new geom-op test that drives serde_json. 136 finished, 1,048 were stopped, none failed. The table now has 3,448 rows, exactly Miri's listing of the kernel's tests: 2,400 finish under Miri and 1,048 are native-only, each `native-only over` its scaled stop. The partitions drop from 239 to 41, the fewest within 900 s.

**Changes:** this record; 32.43's row for the job `miri`; 28.8 and 28.17; `kernel-quality.yml`'s job `miri` (the listing step, the filter's `--list`, `PARTITIONS` and its matrix) and its header; `.config/nextest.toml` (the `miri-measure` profile); geom-test's `miri` module, `miri-times.tsv`, the `kernel-quality` binary (`miri-filter --list`, `miri-times`), `tests/kernel_quality_workflow.rs` and its new dependency roxmltree 0.21.2 (MIT OR Apache-2.0); engine-geometry's scaffold check, its seeded case and its licence record.

## 32.59 The mesh oracles: a model that crosses itself is judged by the exact reference alone

**Decided by the main session, 2026-11-17**, under the rule that every decision is the main session's, recorded with its reason (32.53). Gate 4's record keeps 32.57.

**What it is.** The first run of Manifold and CGAL on Manifold's stored models (the case set `manifold-models`, #935, Gate 4's G156) gave 72 differences, every one traced to its cause, none a fault of the kernel's. Four rules settle them; the comparison (`geom-test`'s `oracle::meshes` and the `oracle` binary) holds each, and `kernel-oracles.yml`'s `meshes` legs run them.

| Rule | What it says | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A model that crosses itself | When a solid of a job, as both sides receive it (on the grid), crosses itself, the job is judged by the exact reference alone: the kernel's exact volume must be the reference's (`flat_reference::six_volume_where`), and the job's outcome is recorded as outside the oracle's stated input, citing the oracle's own documentation. A kernel volume that is not the reference's, or a refusal, fails the job, crossing or not. Whether a solid crosses itself is read by the kernel's own exact winding numbers (`meshes::crosses`: a region of positive volume where the winding number is neither 0 nor 1, an overlap or a pocket turned inside out), never by a hand-made mark; an oracle whose documentation says nothing of such input is compared as on any other job | 57 of the 72: the kernel reads such a surface by its winding number (11.1, 19.5) and equals the exact reference on every one, while both oracles take the surface as it is, outside what their documentation says they take. Manifold's wiki ("Manifold Library", section "Overlaps", <https://github.com/elalish/manifold/wiki/Manifold-Library>) promises a correct result only from input that does not overlap itself (epsilon-valid), and says the triangulator may build output surfaces incorrectly from a self-overlapping input mesh; CGAL's user manual (6.0.1, Polygon Mesh Processing, "Corefinement and Boolean Operations", <https://doc.cgal.org/6.0.1/Polygon_mesh_processing/index.html>) states that the input meshes of its booleans must not self-intersect, and its Nef polyhedron from a polyhedral surface is documented only as the point set that surface represents. A triage entry for each would be 29 fixtures of up to 84 KB, each replaying a whole pair of models, and a public report upstream, for something that is no one's fault |
| A model that is not a valid exact mesh | The case set records each such model with the kernel's refusal of it, exactly (`meshes::REFUSED_MODELS`: its kind and the place it names); its jobs are not sent to the oracle, and `oracle compare` fails a job of it if the kernel accepts the model or refuses it in another way. Not dropped from the set, and not healed here: the route for such a model is import and heal (19.7), tested where healing lives | 12 of the 72: four models (`dedupe_stale_duplicate`, `degenerate_split_quad_first`, `degenerate_swap_cycle_first`, `openscad-nonmanifold-crash`) have faces of no area on the grid or edges of more than two faces, which 19.2 has no place for in an exact mesh, and the kernel is right to refuse them |
| CGAL's harness reads a solid shell by shell | The harness in the oracle repository (`cgal/harness.cpp`) makes each shell its own polyhedral surface and takes them from the largest enclosed volume down, adding a shell that faces out and taking away one that faces in, so a cavity stays a cavity; the image's build runs its tests, a box with a box-shaped cavity among them, each with its exact volume | 3 of the 72 (`Cray_left`, a solid with a cavity): one Nef polyhedron of both shells is the point set they enclose, the cavity filled (spareware-co-op/oracles#3) |
| The distance of the comparison is certified | The sampled distance's point-to-triangle step measures every quantity from the triangle's own corners in intervals (`geom_num::interval`), and where an enclosure cannot decide or is wider than 10^-9 steps and 2^-40 of the distance, in exact rationals; it gives a certified lower bound, so a sampled distance still can only refute | In doubles the closest-point method subtracted products that cancel on a sliver far from the origin: a sample on its own triangle read 3.03 steps away (#951). This makes the judge correct; it does not change what the kernel is held to |

Guarded by planted cases (`geom-test`'s `tests/oracle_meshes.rs`): a solid that crosses itself is found so from its winding numbers alone and its job judged by the reference, whatever the oracle answered; solids that do not cross (a box, a box with a cavity, two boxes apart) are never treated as crossing, and a wrong answer on them is a difference; a crossing model whose kernel volume is off by one cubic step, or which the kernel refuses, still fails; a valid model recorded as refused fails because the kernel accepts it, and a refused model recorded with another refusal fails. `tests/oracles_workflow.rs` holds the `meshes` legs to naming their case set to `oracle compare`, with a planted violation. The distance is held to exact arithmetic on #951's sliver, the same sliver moved to the origin, and a sweep of slivers at the grid's far corner.

**Changes:** this record; 28.10; `kernel-oracles.yml` (the comparison names its case set; the pinned commit of the oracle repository moves to the harness fix); `geom-test`'s `oracle::meshes` (`REFUSED_MODELS`, `recorded_refusals`, `held_refusal`, `crosses`, `stated_input`, `judge_by_reference`, `Judge`, the certified distance), `oracle::geomdl::Compared::Outside`, the `oracle` binary (`jobs` leaves out a recorded refusal's jobs; `compare --cases`), `tests/oracle_meshes.rs`, `tests/oracle_tool.rs` and `tests/oracles_workflow.rs`; in the oracle repository, `cgal/harness.cpp`, `cgal/Dockerfile` and `cgal/tests/`.

## 32.60 Miri's jobs run from one plan, each within 15 minutes

**Decided by the main session, 2026-11-17** (its brief to the lane `miri-plan`), under the rule that every decision is the main session's, recorded with its reason.

**What it was.** With Miri's table measured on GitHub's own runners (#966), a partition's tests were sized right but the job around them was not: cold, 7 to 12 minutes passed before its first test (main's run 44000736866). Every partition listed every test binary under Miri (2.3 to 5.4 minutes whatever the cache), built cargo-nextest from crates.io (2.5 to 4.2 minutes), built kernel-quality to write its filter (0.6 to 1.0), and then nextest listed the partition's own binaries again (20 to 50 s). Partition 1 also ran geom-pool whole under Tree Borrows after its tests, 10.2 minutes, because one test, `attack_g33`'s `attack_rayon_pools_give_the_serial_answer_at_every_thread_count_and_chunk_size`, ran to the 600 s stop every time: the default-miri profile passes a test it stops, so it counted as passed, and it never finished under Miri in any run on record (14, 15 and 17 November), against 0.1 to 1.4 s natively. The plan's model was short too: it took a partition's tests to end by the larger of their total over four and the longest, but nextest starts the next test whenever one ends, in its own order, so a partition could end as late as the others' total over four and then its longest test.

**What it decides.**
1. One plan job, `miri-plan`, lists the run's tests under Miri once, fails on a test with no row, writes every partition's filterset and geom-pool's (`kernel-quality miri-plan`), and hands them on with the crates and the cargo-nextest it built (the same `cargo install --locked` of the pinned version) as one artifact of the run kept one day. The partitions (`miri`) and geom-pool's job run from it and check the binary's version; none lists every test or builds a tool. The partitions are the plan's own matrix, so their number is never written by hand.
2. geom-pool's tests have rows in Miri's table, measured under Tree Borrows as they run, and its job (`miri-pool`, with the doctests) runs the tests the table says finish, by the same rule as every partition. The test that ran to the stop stays under Miri (the main session's decision, not native-only) with a smaller sweep there: under `cfg!(miri)` it runs rayon pools of 2 and 3 threads at chunk sizes 7 and 4,096, three rounds of up to 14 items in which each outcome comes up and the round with failing items is split into chunks, failing items one in a quarter of the largest size, as the native sweep's one in 150 of 600 (a sweep at chunk sizes 1, 7 and 64 over six rounds of up to 48 items still ran to the stop on GitHub, run 44064655057); the native sweep is unchanged. Its row is measured again on GitHub: 28 s (27.5 s, measuring run 44071252392), so all eight of geom-pool's tests finish (2 to 129 s) and run. The measuring run measures geom-pool's tests under Tree Borrows too, so its rows can be measured again.
3. Each partition is packed so that its job ends within 15 minutes on a cold cache and a runner 1.6 times slower (`SLOWER`, 8/5): each test is planned at its row's seconds times 8/5, rounded up, and never over `BUDGET`, since nextest stops it there whatever the runner (`planned`), and what is left after the measured set-up (`FIXED_SECONDS`, 120 s, mostly downloads a slow runner does not stretch) is `PART_SECONDS`, 780 s, for nextest's listing of the partition's binaries (`LIST_SECONDS`, 1 s each, times 8/5) and its planned tests, bounded in any order nextest starts them (Graham's bound for list scheduling). The first proof run, 44075531453, planned for a runner a fifth slower (630 s after the set-up), ran five partitions to 15.4 to 17.3 minutes: their tests ran 1.44 to 1.59 times the table's seconds, and over every partition the ratio was 0.84 to 1.59 at the same concurrency, so GitHub's runners vary that much. `BUDGET`, 600 s, stays the stop and what finishing means: the two are separate constants, and a finishing test, planned at the stop at most, with its binary's listing always fits a partition alone, so no test that finishes leaves Miri. The table's plan is 96 partitions (79 before), the slowest planned at 773 s, and geom-pool's 8 tests at 285 s.
4. A run by hand may ask for Miri's gate alone (`-f gate=miri`, a choice of `all` or `miri`, `all` by default), so proving Miri's jobs runs Miri's jobs and no other gate. Only the dispatch branch of `when` reads the input; it refuses it beside a narrowed mutation run or a measuring run, and its run has a concurrency group of its own.
5. A partition uploads its report (`miri-<partition>`) in two tries, each bounded to 3 minutes: in run 44075531453 partition 31's tests passed in 466 s and then its upload stalled in GitHub's storage until the job's 30-minute limit. The first try's failure or timeout does not fail the job by itself; it starts the second, under the same name (replacing any part the first left), and the second's failure fails the job, so a lost report is never passed over: the job is re-run, the tests' verdict meanwhile in its test step's log.
6. A test stopped at the 600 s budget counts as passed with no verdict today; what may be stopped so is decided by the main session and lands in a pull request of its own, not this one.

**How it is built.** geom-test's `miri` module (`pack`, first fit decreasing under `Rule::Job` or `Rule::Stop`; `planned`; `gate_plan`; `Plan::part_seconds`, `binaries`, `ends_within`; the constants), `kernel-quality miri-plan` in place of `miri-filter`, and `kernel-quality.yml`'s jobs `miri-plan`, `miri` and `miri-pool`. Guarded by `kernel_quality_workflow.rs` (`miris_jobs_run_from_one_plan_each_within_fifteen_minutes`, `a_dispatch_alone_can_run_miris_gate_alone` and the Tree Borrows test, with planted cases: a partition that builds a tool, lists every test or plans itself, an unchecked binary, a matrix of its own, the hand-over kept a month, geom-pool run whole, a partition saving a cache; and `a_partitions_report_is_uploaded_in_two_bounded_tries_and_a_lost_one_fails_the_job`, with planted cases: a try unbounded or bounded to the job's limit, the first try's failure failing the job, the second run every time or only on a failure, hidden, under another name or unable to replace, no second try, the tests' step made to pass), `quality_gates.rs` (drawn tests packed and every part held, in every order an oracle tries, within its job on a runner 1.6 times slower, each test there 8/5 of its seconds or stopped at 600 s; the binary on the real table and on a test with no row) and `queue_wiring.rs` (`an_in_run_hand_over_is_kept_a_day_and_read_in_its_run`: the plan is the second listed in-run hand-over, by exactly its job and name, with planted widenings).

**Changes:** this record; 32.43's rows for `miri`, `miri-plan` and `miri-pool`; `kernel-quality.yml` (its header, the input `gate`, the jobs `miri-plan`, `miri` and `miri-pool`, the measuring jobs' listing, parts and cache, `done`); geom-test's `miri` module, `miri-times.tsv` (geom-pool's rows), geom-pool's `tests/attack_g33.rs` (Miri's sweep), the `kernel-quality` binary, `tests/kernel_quality_workflow.rs`, `tests/quality_gates.rs` and `tests/queue_wiring.rs`.

## 32.63 line_cone's term 38 is checked in 64 pieces, from a saved state that holds its Taylor theorems

**Decided by the main session, 2026-11-18**, under the rule that every decision is the main session's, recorded with its reason (32.53).

**What it is.** HOL Light checks FPTaylor's certificate for line_cone's filter term by term, in shards (28.6, `predicate_certificates::SHARDED`). Term 38 of its 70 could not be checked in one job: alone on a hosted runner it searched its 206,720 cells for over five hours and stopped at GitHub's six-hour limit, twice (run 43797413012). No looser bound is proved in its place and no term or piece is left out. Three things change.

- **The cut.** Term 38's inequality is proved on 64 parts of its box (`// hol-light-cut 38: 6`): the box halved six times where the verifier's own search would first halve it, each piece 3230 cells. A shard proves its pieces by the same call that proves a term and assumes the others, and HOL Light joins the halves into the term's inequality on the whole box (`certificates/fptaylor_cut.hl`). Twenty-two shards hold three pieces each (the last holds one), and ten shards hold the other 69 terms whole; no shard holds both.
- **The second saved state.** Before it checks any cell, the verifier builds the term's Taylor theorems (the first and second partial derivatives of its function, as HOL Light theorems). That takes longer than a piece: 55 minutes on a development machine, where a piece took 14 to 22, so about 245 minutes on the slowest runners, in every job that holds a piece. So one job of the run, `hol-light-cut-states`, restores the loaded state that `hol-light-states` saved, has the verifier build those theorems once, proving no term and no piece, and saves the process again; each shard of pieces (a job of `fptaylor-pieces`) restores that second state and proves its pieces as before.
- **The loaded states are saved one after another.** line_cone's process held 10.4 GB when it was saved on a development machine and lpi_side's about 5, and a runner has 16, so `hol-light-states` loads and saves one problem at a time. The saving session also compacts OCaml's heap before the save (`Gc.compact`), which changes no value: the same process held 3.95 GB once OCaml had compacted its heap after a restore. The phrase was run in a restored state on a development machine; its effect on a first state's size has not been measured, and nothing here depends on it.

**The evidence.** On a development machine, piece 38.1 proved from the second state and proved the old way (HOL Light loaded and the theorems built in the piece's own process) print the same theorem, byte for byte (94 lines, sha256 `53def91cf9fc63dba5349eec70fddd0ede4e123830b0c1a83f96ce23f89a12dd`), and five pieces were proved from the one second state, none building the theorems again. The second state was 4.19 GB and restored in 8 seconds.

**The first run, and how a restored HOL Light is held (amended 2026-11-18).** The first run with these jobs (44076902825) failed in `hol-light-cut-states` six minutes in, with the loaded state restored and nothing wrong with it. In a job, fptaylor.sh is the first process of its container, so the HOL Light it launches has process 1 as its parent when the loaded state is saved. DMTCP restores such a process in a grandchild of `dmtcp_restart`, which itself ends at once with success (v4.2.0, `src/dmtcprestartinternal.cpp`: `isOrphan`, `createOrphanedProcess`). The run that saves again had taken `dmtcp_restart`'s process for HOL Light's: it found that process gone within five seconds, said HOL Light had stopped, and ended the container with HOL Light restored and running in it. The shards never met this, because they restore in the foreground and read HOL Light's output to its end; and on the development machine the saving path had only been run outside a container, where the script is not process 1 and DMTCP restores HOL Light in `dmtcp_restart`'s own process. Now the saving run holds HOL Light as the shards do, by its output: the restart writes into a pipe, and the process the script waits on is the pipe's last reader, which ends when HOL Light does, whichever process DMTCP made it. A saving run that gives up shows the end of HOL Light's output and of the DMTCP coordinator's log, so that a failure names its cause (the first one printed neither). `geom-test/tests/certificates_workflow.rs::a_restored_hol_light_is_known_by_its_output_alone` holds both, with that run's own lines among its planted violations. On a development machine, with the script made the first process of a process namespace (bubblewrap) and the loaded state saved that way, the saving run as the failed run had it gave up five seconds after the restore, as the job did, while the process DMTCP was restoring HOL Light into was still running; the saving run as it is now built term 38's theorems and saved the second state (4.19 GB), and piece 38.1 proved from that state printed the same 94 lines as before (sha256 `53def91cf9fc63dba5349eec70fddd0ede4e123830b0c1a83f96ce23f89a12dd`).

**What a reader newly trusts, and what checks each.** On top of what the first saved state asks (32.43's `hol-light-states` row):

| What is trusted | What it means | Its check |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The second saved state | Until now every theorem a piece's proof uses, beyond HOL Light's libraries and the verifier's tables, was proved in the piece's own process. Now term 38's Taylor theorems are proved once, by HOL Light's kernel, in `hol-light-cut-states` of the same run, from the pinned sources and the run's one certificate, and each shard of pieces continues from a copy of that process's memory. The first state holds no theorem the check relies on; the second holds exactly these | The state is made by a job of the same run and never cached; a shard of pieces restores it only from its run's own artifact, named with no other run or repository, only once the saving job has passed, and only if the file's sha256 is the one that job gave as its output, which fptaylor.sh compares before it unpacks anything; the saving run fails unless it built each cut term's theorems and proved nothing (`geom-test/tests/certificates_workflow.rs::the_second_state_is_made_and_handed_on_within_its_run_only` and `a_shard_of_pieces_never_starts_without_its_state`, with a planted violation of each) |
| DMTCP, out of process | That it saves and restores HOL Light's memory faithfully, now twice in a row for a shard of pieces. It is DMTCP v4.2.0 (LGPL-3.0-or-later) at a pinned commit, built in the job and run as programs of its own around HOL Light's toplevel; nothing of it is in the kernel or linked into it | A state's manifest names HOL Light's and DMTCP's commits, the size of the Taylor tables and the processor flags of each runner that saved it, and a restore refuses a state made with any other, or on a runner that lacks one of those flags. The same theorem text with and without the states, above. The run itself does not check that a restore is faithful: that is trusted, as the runner and the container are |
| "assuming N terms" in a shard's printed theorem | A shard's theorem holds under the terms and pieces it does not prove, so one green shard proves nothing alone: the problem's bound is proved by all its shards of one run together | Every term and every piece is in exactly one shard, and no shard has both kinds (`geom-gen/tests/certificate_problems.rs`, with planted cases); each shard is one job of the same run, of `fptaylor-shards` or `fptaylor-pieces`, from one list that fails unless each shard is in exactly one of the two; each job runs every shard of its list on every event the workflow runs on, and cannot fail or skip its check and pass (`geom-test/tests/certificates_workflow.rs::every_piece_is_checked_in_exactly_one_job_of_the_same_run`, with a planted violation of each); and fptaylor.sh fails a shard unless it proved exactly its own terms and pieces and assumed exactly the rest. So a run is green only when every term and every piece was proved in it. A problem byte for byte its base's, with the scripts and the workflow the base's too, is set aside whole, every shard by the same rule |
| The verifier's memory of a function | That, given a function it has met, the verifier uses the theorems it built for that same function | `certificates/fptaylor_cache.hl` keys on the verifier's parameters, the precision and the function's term, compared whole; and inside HOL Light a piece's theorem is refused unless it has no assumption and is exactly its goal (`fptaylor_cut.hl`) |

Not trusted: any cache, any other run, any file from outside the run.

**The minutes.** No piece has run on a hosted runner, so the jobs are planned from a development machine's minutes times the runners' measured ratios (their loads of HOL Light take 32, 42 or 60 minutes): `hol-light-states` about 110 to 200 minutes, `hol-light-cut-states` about 120 to 265, a shard of three pieces about 65 to 200, a shard of whole terms about 85 to 190, each under its limit of 350; the longest path is about 5 to 11 hours. The pieces are shared out again by measured cost once a run has measured them.
