**The measured minutes, and the shards shared out again (run 44161231205).** The first whole run of the chain passed, 56 jobs: `hol-light-states` 84 minutes (the two loads, 29 and 46, one after the other), `hol-light-cut-states` 34 (term 38's Taylor theorems, 25), the ten shards of whole terms 94 to 191, the 22 shards of pieces 12 to 35, `lpi_side`'s four 27 to 39. A shard spent 4.4 to 5.6 minutes outside its parts, a piece took 3.7 to 9.1 minutes by the runner (5.5 on most), and a term 0 to 138. From those minutes, and the slower of this run and run 44076902825 for each term, the shards are shared out again so that none that can be under 15 minutes is planned over it (`geom-gen`'s `LINE_CONE_SHARDS` and `LPI_SIDE_SHARDS`): each piece a shard of its own (64 jobs, 9 to 16.3 minutes by the runner), `line_cone`'s 69 whole terms in 60 shards and `lpi_side`'s 68 in 24, a shard of several terms filled to 13 planned minutes. Still over 15 minutes, and why: 41 of `line_cone`'s terms and 2 of `lpi_side`'s take over 9.4 minutes alone (the longest, term 70, 138), and a term is the least a shard proves unless it is cut as term 38 is, each cut needing its own proof run first; a piece on the slowest runner (16.3); and the two jobs that save the states, which load HOL Light and build the theorems once so that no shard does. `geom-gen/tests/certificate_problems.rs` holds it: `no_shard_that_can_be_under_fifteen_minutes_is_planned_over_it` refuses the grouping before (three pieces a shard, two, a shard of five whole terms), and `a_shard_list_that_drops_or_doubles_a_part_is_refused` refuses the committed list with any shard left out or twice.

**What was rejected.** A looser target for term 38: fewer cells, but the theorems cost the same, and it is a weaker statement. The cut without the second state: on the slowest runners a job with a piece would spend 60 minutes loading and 245 building the theorems before its first cell. A cache of the states between runs: another run could have written it. Larger or self-hosted runners: the project's runs use GitHub's hosted ones.

**Changes:** this record; 32.43's rows for `kernel-certificates.yml`; `kernel-certificates.yml` (`fptaylor-problems`' two lists, `hol-light-states` one problem at a time, the new jobs `hol-light-cut-states` and `fptaylor-pieces`); `geom-gen`'s `certificates/fptaylor.sh`, `certificates/fptaylor_cut.hl`, `certificates/fptaylor_cache.hl`, `predicate_certificates::LINE_CONE_SHARDS`, `generated/fptaylor-line-cone.fpt` and `tests/certificate_problems.rs`; `geom-test`'s `tests/certificates_workflow.rs`; the method note `projects/kit/notes/G35-filter-certificates.md`.

## 32.64 A circle lying on a plane, cylinder or cone is an overlap by identity

**Decision (issue 974).** `curve_surface` answers a circle that lies on a plane, or about the axis of a cylinder or cone at its radius there, in closed form: one `Item::Overlap` with `Coincidence::Identical` over the whole circle, coverage `TopologyCertified`, as a line lying in a plane and a helix on a coaxial cylinder are answered. Before, such a pair went to the interval engine, whose residual is zero all round the circle: it could drop no box and prove no lone zero, bisected round the whole circle to the resolution floor, and ended with no zero and one `Item::Flagged` over the circle, coverage `CompleteOutsideFlagged`. That cost 550,000 to 1,290,000 work units for one circle of radius 150 to 224, against 13 to 2,481 for a circle that crosses the surface. No answer was wrong; every caller paid, and `quadrics::natural` paid it for each end circle of one surface that lies on the other, once for each piece of its answer.

**Why it is exact.** Whether the circle lies on the surface is decided by the signs of sums of products of the stored doubles (`curve::sign_of`: each product is a double and its error, and their sum is exact), with no tolerance and no rounded comparison. A circle's frame is defined exactly by its stored normal, so the stored data are the circle.

| Surface | Theorem | The exact test |
|---|---|---|
| Plane c + u·a + v·b | A circle of positive radius holds three points not on one line, so one plane holds it: its own | n·a = 0, n·b = 0 and (centre − c)·n = 0, n the circle's stored normal |
| Cylinder | A plane cuts a right circular cylinder in a circle only across its axis, and there in the circle of its radius about the axis; a circle shares at most four points with any other conic | The normal parallel to the axis and the centre on it (cross products, each a sum of products), and the two stored radii equal |
| Cone | The same, the circle at height h over the centre having radius \|r₀ + k·h/s\| | Coaxial as above, and r₀·s + k·h = r·s or −r·s, where the axis runs along a coordinate axis, so that h is a difference of two stored doubles |

Where the surface's domain holds the circle is decided as exactly. A plane's domain holds all of the circle's box in (u, v), or none of it (nothing is answered, proven), or else the pair is the engine's as before. A round kind's domain must be a whole turn about the axis, and the circle's v against an end of the domain is read from an enclosure where that tells, and otherwise from the exact sign of (h − s·end) on a coordinate axis.

**What stays the engine's**, at its old cost and with its old answer, because no sum of products of stored doubles decides it: a circle on a sphere; a cone, or an end of a round domain an enclosure cannot tell from the circle's height, on an axis off the coordinate axes (the unit vector of such an axis is no vector of doubles); a plane or round domain that holds only a part of the circle; and any circle whose stored centre or radius is a rounding of the one that would lie on the surface. `quadrics::edges` builds an end circle's centre as the midpoint of an enclosure and a sphere latitude's radius by rounding a cosine, so on a frame off the grid, and on a sphere's latitudes, `natural` still pays the engine for an edge that lies on the other surface. These are named in issue 974, and their cost is written in the cost lines of `quadrics.rs` and `curve_surface.rs`.

**What a caller sees.** For these pairs `curve_surface` returns an overlap where it returned a flag: the same set of points, now proven, with nothing left unresolved, for a few predicates. `quadrics::natural` reads only where each edge meets the other surface, as a box, and the overlap's box is the circle's as the flag's was, so its answers are the same; a call that the work budget stopped (two coaxial surfaces whose four end circles lie on each other took over 2^22 units) is now answered. `natural` also runs each edge against the other surface once a call, where it ran it once for each piece.

**The cut such an edge gives is kept (measured, not read).** `natural` cuts each piece where a mark of the pair lies on it, and the mark of an edge circle that lies on the other surface is the box of the whole circle. A probe showed that this mark does cut the piece that is that circle, at its parameter 0, once for each edge lying there (twice on each circle of the first stored input). A variant that gives no mark for the overlap of a circle edge was run beside this branch on a family of 452 pairs, each in both orders (904 calls: the four stored inputs; a cylinder or cone whose end lies in a plane, and one beside it, on each of the target's four frames; coaxial cylinder, cone and sphere pairs whose end circles lie on each other). Every answer was the same, character for character; 172 calls took 4, 5 or 20 work units fewer (926 of 208,383,412 in all; the four stored inputs 26, 2,519, 34 and 34 against 46, 2,524, 38 and 38). Against the code before this record the same family differs in 14 answers, each a stop on the budget that is answered now, and its work fell from 387,877,016 to 208,383,412. Why no answer moves: the circle lies on both surfaces all round, so every stretch between cuts is on both and the loop is kept whole; and no other piece crosses it, because two of these surfaces that share a circle about an axis meet only in circles in planes parallel to it. So the cut is not needed, and it is kept: dropping it saves a few units a call and changes nothing a caller sees, so the line that dropped it would be held by a count alone and needs its own test in `geom-intersect`. Issue 974 names it as a piece.

**What is left, by count.** The work of one `natural` call today, by the meter, with the edge that takes it (a probe on each edge's `curve_surface`), dearest first:

| Case | Stored input | Work | The edge that costs | Missing |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| End circle on the axis off the grid, in a plane | not stored (it stops): `022d3c0000030000000000000300` | over 4,194,304: stopped by the budget, at radius 1 and at 150 | the end circle, whose centre `edges` stores 4.3e-14 off the plane | the edge carried as an exact plane and height, not a rounded circle |
| End circle in a plane whose domain holds a part of it | `f7ff83b50660ce60` (a cone's, of radius 600) and `0200004b040000004b0000000000` (a cylinder's, of radius 5, half held) | 1,252,098 and 136,758 | that end circle, flagged once or three times: 1,252,043 and 136,698 | a circle in its plane cut by the plane's domain, as arcs |
| Two cones about one line on the axis off the grid, no edge on the other | `032d3c0009030003000000090300` | 800,571 | the two end circles in the plane of the other cone's apex, clear of it by 150: 353,774 and 441,124, nothing found | a circle about the axis of a cylinder or cone that an enclosure of the two radii proves clear of it |
| Sphere of radius 256 against a cylinder of radius 1 about a line through its centre (the issue's fifth input) | `fdde00f5ffc22502de` | 782,683 | the cylinder's end circle, 0.002 outside the sphere all round: 782,639, nothing found | a circle on or off a sphere, by the exact sign of r² + d² − R² |
| Cone against a plane that holds its axis | `0300004b04000000000000000100` | 484,099 | the plane's edge through the apex, one flag: 483,244 | a line through a cone's apex, which meets it there alone or along a ruling |
| Sphere against a cylinder about a line through its centre, the cylinder's end a great circle | `010000000000000200004b000000` | 19,133 at radius 1 (185,057 at radius 10) | that end circle, flagged all round: 19,090 | the same sign as the row two above: on the sphere, exactly |

A search of the target with the closed form in place (55 minutes, six processes, about 290,000 runs) found no crash and no broken law, and eleven inputs that took over ten seconds a run in the fuzzing build. All eleven are the second row: a cone whose end circle lies in a plane whose domain holds a part of it, the circle's radius 299 to 856, at 1,252,098 to 3,046,840 units a call, all but 55 to 90 of them on that one edge. A run of the target makes three such calls. The cheapest of the eleven is the one stored.

In none of them is the cost right: each pays the engine for something a few signs or one enclosure would decide. The target's test `what_the_closed_form_leaves_costs_no_more_than_today` holds each stored input to its count, so that a call on it can only get cheaper.

**Changes:** this record; `geom-intersect`'s `curve_surface.rs` (`circle_in_plane`, `circle_on_cylinder`, `circle_on_cone`, `ring_overlap`) and `quadrics.rs` (`marks`, found once a call); `tests/circle_on_surface.rs`, one test for each branch, with their rows in Miri's table; `geom-fuzz`'s `natural` target (`work_of`, the issue's four inputs and six of those left stored in its `crashes` folder, and the work of each held to a count).

## 32.69 A circle lying in a plane whose domain holds part of it is its arcs

**Decision (issue 974).** Where a whole circle lies in a plane (32.64's exact test) and the plane's domain holds only a part of it, `curve_surface` answers in closed form: one `Item::Overlap` with `Coincidence::Identical` for each arc of the circle the domain holds, coverage `TopologyCertified`. Before, 32.64 left this pair to the interval engine, which bisected round the whole circle to the resolution floor and flagged it: 1,252,098 to 3,046,840 work units a call on the eleven inputs the search of 32.64 found, all one class (a cone's end circle in a plane that holds part of it). This is the exact core's own closed form, and it stays as the checker of that answer whichever code computes it.

**Why it is exact.** The form is taken only where the plane's two vectors each run along one coordinate axis (any length, either sign). An end `e` of the domain is then the line where one coordinate is c + e·a, and its distance from the circle's centre, c + e·a − centre, is a sum of four doubles (the product is a double and its error), so its sign and its square are exact sums of products of stored doubles (`offset`, `sign_of`).

- **Which arcs.** A half-plane whose line crosses the circle holds one closed arc of it; one whose line misses it holds all or none. The rectangle is the intersection of its half-planes, so the circle meets its edge only at crossings that every other half-plane holds, and between two such points next to each other round the circle the open arc is all inside or all outside: inside exactly when no half-plane's leaving crossing lies on it or at its start (`held_of`). Every comparison is the sign of a sum of products: a crossing's coordinates are held as a sign and an exact square, and the order of two points round the circle is read from those (`round`). A touch from inside cuts nothing.
- **Where each arc lies on the circle's own parameter.** The parameters of an arc's ends are enclosures, and an enclosure cannot say on which side of the circle's own join an end stands when it reaches the join. That side is decided by an exact sign (`past_join`): where the domain starts at a whole number the join lies along the frame's x (even) or against it (odd), and where the frame's stored x runs along one of the plane's two axes the side of the join is the sign of one coordinate of the end, which is held exactly. So an arc that starts on the join starts at the domain's first end exactly, one that ends on it ends at the domain's last end exactly, and there is a stretch past the join only where the arc truly passes it (`stretches_of`).

**The fault this record closes.** The first form of this code cut the enclosure of an arc across the join with `Seam::spans`. An arc ending exactly on the join then gave a second overlap a few ulps long on the far side of the join, holding no part of the arc. `quadrics::marks` made a mark of it, `cut_at` cut at its middle, and `trimmed` kept that cut in place of the exact one from the plane's edge: on 13 pairs of the family below (circles whose centre is on a side or a corner of the domain, so that the arc ends at exact quarter-turns) the start of the answer's curve moved outward by 2 to 24 ulps. All 13 were traced to that sliver; with the end placed by its exact sign there is no sliver, and `quadrics.rs` is unchanged.

**What stays the engine's**, each said in the headers: a plane whose vectors run off the coordinate axes; a circle kind that is an arc, not a whole turn; a lone point of the circle in the domain (a side or a corner touching it from outside); stored doubles whose pair products would not be exact; and an arc's end whose enclosure reaches the join where no exact sign places it (the frame's x off the plane's axes, or a domain that starts off a whole number); and an arc whose two crossings have enclosures that meet (32.80).

**Measured** (one `natural` call, release build, both orders the same). The eleven inputs, work before and after: `9303024b00000d` 1,554,379 and 104; `033f464b00002f` 1,507,313 and 62; `2b3c67b50000ce` 1,736,253 and 62; `f7ff83b50660ce60` 1,252,098 and 62; `ffffffb58a0014` 1,637,547 and 105; `6333b7b4ffb4fffcfc6bff` 3,046,840 and 62; `6333b7b4ffb4fffcfc6bff0100` 3,046,840 and 62; `ff7e3eb51100ce08f7` 1,730,079 and 62; `9b03ff4b7a002400` 1,579,679 and 104; `03bebe4b00006f` 1,465,604 and 62; `03bebe4b15006f` 1,677,532 and 62. The stored cylinder input `0200004b040000004b`: 136,758 and 70. Over a family of 276 pairs (those twelve, 240 cylinder and cone ends at 20 places on three frames, 24 wide cones), each in both orders, 552 answers: all 552 are the same as the engine's, character for character, with the 13 pairs above among them, and the work in all falls from 162,361,624 units to 1,919,412. Neither code stopped on its budget anywhere in the family.

**Started from:** none found. Looked, by code search only, at tangency-labs/solidcore d240ff40cb26f4a59e5e6b01fce99bbeb9eae98d (`geom/geom_utility/p2.rs` `circle_plane_intersect` is a circle crossing a plane, and `surface/step_window.rs` is a face's window) and at SMLib's `SmPlane.cpp`: none cuts a circle lying in a plane by the plane's rectangle.

**Changes:** this record; `geom-intersect`'s `curve_surface.rs` (`circle_in_plane`, `arcs_in_plane`, `held_of`, `past_join`, `stretches_of` and their helpers); `tests/circle_part_in_plane.rs`, a test for each branch, the join, a centre on a side and a centre on a corner with exact parameters; `geom-fuzz`'s `natural` target (the part-held inputs moved from the engine's list to the closed form's, each held to its count).

## 32.70 solidcore taken whole, behind the kernel's own interface

**Decided (owner, 18 Nov 2026):** "ok do that, take it whole". The main session chose the way in (its brief to the lane `solidcore-in`), under the rule that every decision is the main session's, recorded with its reason.

**What it was.** solidcore (Apache-2.0), tangency-labs' Rust port of SMLib, has the curved operations the kernel has not built: booleans of curved solids, plane sections, intersections of solids, primitives, sweeps, blends, offsets, healing and STEP. Porting them one by one meant rewriting about a million lines. The other way is to take solidcore whole, at one fixed commit, behind an interface of the kernel's own.

**What it decides.**
1. solidcore comes in whole, at one fixed commit, behind the kernel's interface. The kernel's architecture, its exact core (numbers, predicates, flat-faced geometry, the checkers and their proofs), its naming and its formats stay its own: no solidcore type appears in the wrapper's public interface.
2. The way in is the project's own fork, `https://github.com/spareware-co-op/solidcore`, its branch `spareware` at the pin. The pin is commit `cb9bee4bb9f56880c9973a9f8e99c83fdec24ad3`, written as `rev` in the wrapper's Cargo.toml, and the fork's address is the only `allow-git` entry of the kernel's deny.toml (the scaffold's cargo-deny rule requires exactly it). Why the fork: a copy in this repository would be a million lines the kernel's gates would have to excuse, and about 170,000 changed lines a week to carry; a bare dependency on their repository breaks the build the day they move or rewrite it. A fork stays and builds whatever happens to theirs, and a fix of ours is a commit on our branch, pinned by its `rev`.
3. The wrapper is its own crate, `geom-solidcore`, in its own workspace with its own Cargo.lock, in a folder beside the kernel's crates (`projects/engines/geometry/geom-solidcore`), excluded from the root workspace and so outside the kernel's no_std gate and outside every kernel job but its own: one compiler process of solidcore takes 10 to 17 minutes optimised and about 6 to 7 GB, and must not be pulled into the kernel's test, coverage, mutants, Miri or Kani jobs. Being outside `crates/`, it is outside the map, the header grammar and the source scan, which read only that folder.
4. solidcore's operations consume their operands, so the wrapper hands them copies (solidcore's own `brep_copy`). Every body that comes back is checked before it is returned, and a failed check is an error from the wrapper, never a silent answer. A failed status from solidcore is an error too, even when it comes with nothing found.
5. Flat-faced booleans stay on the kernel's exact `geom-bool`: solidcore joined two boxes 1e-7 apart, where ours keeps two. The wrapper is for curved work and for operations the kernel has not built. Nothing switches an existing operation over to solidcore.
6. The rules for later. An operation counts as done by solidcore only when every one of the kernel's cases for it passes on Linux and under WebAssembly. A failing case is a named fault (geom-solidcore's `tests/named-faults.json`), fixed on the fork's branch, or that operation stays the kernel's. The pin moves on a schedule, their main merged into our branch once a week, each time through our cases first.
7. `kernel.yml`'s job `solidcore` (Linux x86-64, native) runs cargo-deny on the wrapper's workspace, builds it and runs its tests, and passes only when the tests that fail are exactly the named faults; a named fault that starts to pass, or a case that fails without a name, fails it. Its build cache is keyed on the pin, with the toolchain in every key, and only a push to main saves it. geom-test's `tests/solidcore_job.rs` guards the job, and 32.43's merge-gate table has its row.

**The named faults at the pin.** The repository has no way to record a wrong but valid answer as known: a fixture's `Known` status is for typed errors only (28.16). So a case solidcore answers wrongly keeps a test that expects the kernel's answer and fails by design, behind the named list, and the job compares the failures with the list.
- SC-1 (case bool-20): two unit boxes 1e-7 apart, united, come back as one solid of 6 faces and volume 2.0000001: solidcore's zone tolerance, 1e-5, merges faces closer than it. Expected: two lumps of 12 faces, volume 2. It blocks flat-faced booleans, which stay on `geom-bool`.
- SC-2 (cases isect-36, isect-38, isect-39): two spheres that do not meet (apart, one inside the other, concentric) are an error from `intersect_breps`, "the B-reps do not intersect", not an empty answer. Expected: no curve and no point. It blocks the wrapper's `meet` for solids that do not meet. The measuring adapter of 17 and 18 Nov read that error as "no curve", which is why it counted these three as the same.
- SC-3 (case bldg-10): a wall 1e-7 from another, united, comes back as one solid of 10 faces (volume 4.20000006 in the measuring adapter): the same zone tolerance closes the gap. Expected: two solids of 12 faces, volume 4.2. It blocks the wrapper's `union` where faces come closer than 1e-5 without touching.
- SC-4 (case bldg-22): a window cut 1e-6 below the top of a wall comes back with volume 2 (1.9999999999999993 through the checked body), not 2.0000002: the sliver above the window is lost. It blocks the wrapper's `difference` where a cutter's face comes within 1e-5 of the body's.

**The kernel's own checked body (from 18 Nov 2026).** The wrapper returns the kernel's body: solidcore's record of each result (solidcore's own record writer, `solidcore-convert`, at the same pin), written as geom-topo's rows on the modeller's grid and passed by geom-check at `Level::Full`, or refused. It is written in the kernel's own form: solidcore cuts a face that runs the whole way round a cylinder, cone or sphere along a seam, an edge with a vertex at each end, and the kernel's own bodies have none (geom-make's cylinder is two closed circles with no vertex), so the conversion leaves the seam out, and its vertices where nothing but one closed circle is left at them; no point, curve or surface moves. A case runs on the step the table was written for (Q1), or, when a length of it is not a whole number of those steps, on the coarsest grid where every length is; a length that is a whole number of steps of no grid is refused, never rounded. The named list then holds, beside SC-1 to SC-4, the faults of solidcore that the checked body shows, and entries marked ours, each a kernel piece to open:
- SC-5 (20 tests: isect-21 to isect-27, isect-40, isect-41, bool-22, bool-23, bool-24, bool-27, bldg-12, bldg-14, bldg-15, bldg-24, bldg-28, bldg-29 and `results_are_the_kernels_bodies`): solidcore writes a cap of its cylinder on a plane about 2e-16 off square to the axis (the cylinder of radius 0.5 and height 1 has its top cap's v axis (-1, 0, 2.1410351709324202e-16)), so cap and side do not meet in the one circle they share, and group 3, which certifies that pair only for a plane exactly square to the axis, refuses the cylinder. The same rows with that one plane square pass. It blocks every operation on a cylinder solidcore makes.
- SC-6 (bool-05, bool-06): two solids touching only along an edge or at a vertex, united, come back as two shells each with its own copy of the edge and its vertices (or of the vertex), at the same points, so faces of the two meet where the body has no shared edge or vertex. It blocks such unions, which stay on `geom-bool`.
- WR-1, ours (isect-15 to isect-20): the modeller takes a section plane by a grid point, and these planes, x + y + z = 3a for the table's f64 a, hold no point of any grid, which a test shows by exact arithmetic. It blocks sections by such planes until the modeller takes a plane another exact way.
- CK-1, ours (bool-26): group 3 has no certified test of two spherical faces, so a ball with a concentric spherical cavity is unchecked, as the kernel's own body of it would be.
- CK-2, ours (bldg-25, bldg-26): a flat face with a circle among its edges is not an outline, so group 3 tests its pairs by their carriers alone and has no certified test of two crossing planes, nor of a cylinder and a plane along its axis: a wall with a round opening is unchecked.
- CK-3, ours (bool-25): group 3 has no certified test of two circular arcs meeting at a shared vertex, nor of the faces they bound: a sphere's octant is unchecked.

**Both targets (from 18 Nov 2026).** `kernel.yml`'s job `solidcore-wasm` runs the same tests, built for wasm32-wasip1 and run under wasmtime (the release and pinned action of the kernel's other wasm32 jobs), on every event `solidcore` runs on, and passes only when the tests that fail are exactly the same named faults. The expected answers are IEEE bit patterns, so the one table judges both. wasm32-wasip1 aborts on a panic and has no threads, so each test runs in its own wasmtime process from the module compiled once, and a test that neither passes nor panics (a trap) fails the job. The module is linked with the 8 MiB stack solidcore's README asks for (the wrapper's `.cargo/config.toml`). geom-test's `tests/solidcore_job.rs` guards it, and 32.43 has its row. Run by hand on its branch (run 44220978938, its cache step finding none): 4 min 32 s for the whole job, of which the wasm32 build 2 min 12 s and the 102 tests, one process each, 1 min 54 s; 102 tests ran, and the 6 that failed were the named list, the same 6 as the native job's in the same run (2 min 53 s).

**The building cases.** 28 of the 77 building and family cases measured on 18 Nov are cases of the table (ids `bldg-NN`, by their row there), every one the present interface can make: walls joined (L, T, cross, in line, different sizes, apart by 1e-7 and 1e-4), curved walls (made as one cylinder less another cut to a quadrant by a box) alone, meeting a straight wall and with a rectangular opening, openings in walls (windows, doors, a niche, slivers of 1e-3 and 1e-6, round openings with the wall laid on its side so the opening's axis is the cylinder's), and slabs (openings, cut in two, notched, a wall along an edge, sitting on a wall). Each expected volume is the closed form worked by hand and checked a second way. The other 49 wait for operations the wrapper does not have: mitred corners, sloped slabs and roofs need an extrusion of a polygon profile; the radial opening through a curved wall a cylindrical box or an extrusion of an annular sector; the curved wall united with the same sector made by a revolve, a revolve; the 34 family forms extrusion (with holes), sweep, blend (skin), revolve and swept blend, each solid and as a void.

**Measured (17 and 18 Nov 2026, at the pin).** It builds with the kernel's compiler, 1.99.0. Its normal dependency tree is 11 crates, all MIT OR Apache-2.0 (unicode-ident adds Unicode-3.0), with no unsafe code; cargo-deny 0.20.2 with the kernel's deny.toml on the wrapper's workspace: advisories, bans, licences and sources ok. Of the 71 stored cases (42 intersections of planes, spheres, cylinders and cones; 29 booleans, 22 flat and 7 curved), 70 gave the same answer natively in the measuring adapter, the different one the boxes 1e-7 apart; through the wrapper, which does not read an error as an answer, 67 pass and 4 are the named faults above. Under WebAssembly (wasm32-wasip1, wasmtime 49.0.2) all 71 answers were bit for bit those of the native run. Of 77 building and family cases, 75 were the same and 2 different, both from its tolerances. The wrapper's build, unoptimised without debug information, took 4 min 54 s on a development machine of 8 threads (one compiler process, 5.6 GB at its peak), and its 74 tests 25 s.

**The job's minutes on GitHub.** Run by hand on the branch (`gh workflow run kernel.yml --ref feat/solidcore-in`), run 44214811794, with an empty cache (its cache step found none): 2 min 40 s for the whole job, of which the build of geom-solidcore and its tests, solidcore included, 1 min 40 s, cargo-deny with the fetch of the fork 24 s and the tests 17 s. That run failed its last step for a fault in the step, not in the cases: the shell GitHub runs steps in stops at the first failure, so the tests that fail by design ended it before the comparison; the step now turns that off around the tests. The same four tests failed there as here, and no other. A second run by hand, on the fixed step (run 44215590283), waits behind the first in the by-hand queue and starts with an empty cache too. A run by hand saves no cache, and neither does a pull request, so the minutes with a full cache are first read on main's first push after this lands.

**What is not yet shown.**
- The check on the way out is solidcore's own validity check (SMLib's `AssertValid`, first level), not the kernel's `geom-check`: turning solidcore's bodies into `geom-topo`'s, so that `geom-check` can check them, is not built. Until it is, the interface takes and gives plain coordinates and numbers, not the kernel's surface and body types.
- The 49 building and family cases that need new operations, and the progress page.
- The pin's weekly move, and what each move costs, has not been done once.
- A full-cache run of the job.

**Changes:** this record; `projects/engines/geometry/geom-solidcore` (its Cargo.toml, Cargo.lock, `.cargo/config.toml`, `src/lib.rs`, `tests/cases.rs`, `tests/cases.json`, `tests/case_names.txt`, `tests/named-faults.json`); the root Cargo.toml's `exclude`; the kernel's deny.toml (`allow-git`) and engine-geometry's scaffold rule for it (`tests/scaffold/deny.rs`); `kernel.yml` (its header, the jobs `solidcore` and `solidcore-wasm`, `done`); geom-test's `tests/solidcore_job.rs` and `tests/queue_wiring.rs`; 32.43's merge-gate table.

## 32.71 A case's lengths are exact decimals, and a step is a whole number to the metre

**Decided (main session, 18 Nov 2026, its rulings on pull request #990).** The cases are the judge, and a length of a case is read as the decimal the table writes: a whole number of the case's grid steps exactly, or the case is refused by name. The allowance of 1e-15 that `tests/cases.rs` gave a length read through an f64 is removed.

**What it decides.**
1. `tests/cases.rs` reads every length of a case (its operands' and its section plane's point; a plane's normal is a direction, not a length) as the decimal text of the table, never through an f64: serde_json 1.0.154 (MIT OR Apache-2.0) with its `arbitrary_precision` feature keeps each number's text, and bigdecimal 0.4.8 (MIT OR Apache-2.0) reads it as an exact decimal. Both are development dependencies of the wrapper's own workspace. A length is `x` metres times the step's whole count to the metre (6.4e6, 1.6e8 or 4e9 for Q0, Q1 and Q2), and it must come out whole. What solidcore is given does not change: a whole number of steps becomes metres as before, bit for bit.
2. The conversion of solidcore's record into the kernel's body multiplies each length by that exact whole count, rounding once, and no longer divides by the step in metres. The step in metres is not a binary fraction, so the division rounded twice: on Q0 a metre came to 6399999.999999999 steps, a unit axis was not exactly a whole number of steps, and group 3 refused every cone and cylinder on Q0 that it passes on Q1 (isect-28 to isect-31 and bool-28 when every case was run on its coarsest grid: the one cause, found from the rows written on each grid). With the product, every case gives the same result on Q0 as on Q1, and geom-solidcore's unit test `a_cone_passes_on_q0_and_q1` holds it.

**What moved on the named list.**
- WR-1 stays, with its cause now stated on the decimals: the table writes each coordinate of the plane's point as `a`, the plane's distance `d` along the diagonal over the square root of 3, cut at 16 or 17 digits. The plane x + y + z = 3a passes through (a, a, a), but that point is not on any grid, and no other point of the plane is either: a point of any grid has coordinates summing to a whole number of quarter-nanometres, and 3a times 4e9 is not whole (`diagonal_planes_hold_no_point_of_any_grid`, now by exact decimals). So the case still cannot be put to the modeller, which takes a plane by a grid point.
- bldg-10 moves from SC-3 to WR-2, ours: the table writes the second wall's corner at 0.20000010000000001 m, the binary64 sum 0.2 + 1e-7 as printed, and that decimal is on no grid, so the case is refused before solidcore is reached. With the allowance it ran as 32,000,016 steps of Q1 and showed SC-3. SC-3 is still solidcore's fault, but no case now reaches it; the table writing the gap as 0.2000001 would bring it back.
- SC-2 (isect-36, isect-38, isect-39) leaves the list: it is decided by the kernel's own exact decision. solidcore still reports two solids that do not meet as a failure, and 32.70's rule that a failure is an error stands for every other pair; but the modeller keeps the centre and radius of each ball it makes, and when solidcore fails on a meeting of two balls, the wrapper decides in whole numbers whether their spheres are apart (the centres' squared distance past the squared sum of the radii, or short of the squared difference, concentric included). Apart, the answer is that they meet nowhere; otherwise the failure is the error it was. The fault stays solidcore's, for every other pair of solids that do not meet.
- 77 of geom-solidcore's 112 tests pass (the new unit test among them) and 35 fail, exactly the named faults, on Linux and under wasmtime alike.

**The test scope.** A pull request's change to a file of geom-solidcore ran every kernel crate's tests, because `geom_test::test_scope` placed a file in no workspace crate's folder as one that may change any build. No workspace crate builds geom-solidcore (it is its own workspace, and the kernel's crates do not depend on it), so a folder the root Cargo.toml's `workspace.exclude` names and that holds no package of the workspace's graph is now set apart: a change there runs only the crates that read files outside their folder (geom-test among them, whose `solidcore_job.rs` reads the wrapper's sources), and `kernel.yml`'s jobs `solidcore` and `solidcore-wasm` test the wrapper itself. An excluded folder that does hold a package of the graph is not set apart, so the rule stays over, never under. geom-test's `tests/test_scope.rs` holds it with planted mistakes.

**Changes:** this record; geom-solidcore's Cargo.toml and Cargo.lock (serde_json's feature, bigdecimal), `src/convert.rs` (`per_metre`, the scale, the unit test), `src/lib.rs` (a ball's centre and radius, `meet`'s exact decision for two balls), `tests/cases.rs` and `tests/named-faults.json`; `kernel.yml`'s two jobs ask for at least 112 tests; geom-test's `tests/solidcore_job.rs` (checked arithmetic in its count of the tests), `src/test_scope.rs` (`apart`, `Place::Apart`), `src/fuzz_scope.rs` (`forward` shared) and `tests/test_scope.rs`.

## 32.75 The mesh oracles' images are saved in the Actions cache by main alone

**Decided (main session, 19 Nov 2026, ruling on the cache total).** Read from `gh cache list` on 19 Nov: 75 caches, 10.64 GB against GitHub's 10 GB limit, every cache on main; the two mesh oracle images, cgal 796 MB and manifold 600 MB, held once each. Earlier readings were 12.08 GB in 34 caches, about 6.6 GB of it copies kept per open pull request, which got main's own build caches (the first to be thrown out, being the least recently used) thrown out, so the first jobs of a push to main found no cache and ran 13 to 16 minutes.

1. The image caches are saved only by a run on main (`github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'` on the save step of `meshes-images`). A pull request restores main's copy, which its run can read. A pull request that moves the oracle pin, or whose run finds main's copy thrown out, builds the image in its own run and hands it to `meshes` as an artifact of that run kept one day, never as a cache entry. `meshes` restores the cache, takes the artifact when the cache has none, and fails when it has no image from either.
2. The image is not pinned by an image id. A rebuild gives a new id, so an id pin would turn main red each time GitHub throws the cache out, for no fault. The pin is what it was: the cache key is the oracle folder's tree id at the pinned `ORACLES_COMMIT`, and after this change only main can write that key. The images are still audited at the pin and run with no network.
3. Decision 29.20's rule 8 stands: an image is a file, never pushed anywhere. No package, registry or release asset.
4. A single cache key for the `test` and `suite` release builds was examined and not made. `suite` sets `CARGO_PROFILE_RELEASE_DEBUG_ASSERTIONS=true` for its release legs and the release legs of `test` do not, so the two build different artifacts (a different release profile, every crate again) and one cache would be valid for only one of them. What a job builds is not changed to make keys match.

**Expected total.** The change removes a source of growth (up to 1.4 GB for every pull request whose run found no copy on main) and does not delete any cache: the total after is the 10.64 GB read on 19 Nov, still 0.64 GB over the limit until a further cut. The images are not what holds it over, so the further cut is its own decision.

**Proof (main's by-hand runs).** One run of `kernel-oracles.yml` on a pull request branch whose pin is main's, which must show `meshes` restoring main's copy (cache hit, no upload step run); one run on a branch that changes `ORACLES_COMMIT` or whose key main does not hold, which must show `meshes-images` building, the artifact uploaded and `meshes` taking it, and no cache entry for that key afterwards.

**Changes:** this record and the table row of `meshes-images`; `kernel-oracles.yml` (`meshes-images` save step, the artifact step, `meshes` restore, artifact and check steps); geom-test's `tests/oracles_workflow.rs` (the rules for both jobs, each shown on a planted violation).

## 32.76 solidcore's named faults made ours: the cylinder stays the kernel's, the shared edge was the wrapper's, the upstream files are named

**Decided (main session, 19 Nov 2026, its rulings of 00:1x, 01:2x and 01:4x on SC-5, SC-6 and bldg-10).** The pin stays `cb9bee4bb9f56880c9973a9f8e99c83fdec24ad3`; no patch goes to the fork.

**What it decides.**
1. **SC-5 stays solidcore's, and the cylinder stays the kernel's own, for good.** Measured at the pin (three throwaway variants of a fix on a development machine): in all 1,175 loops of one ellipse that solidcore's suite and stored answers make, the ellipse's frame is square, unit length and right-handed and the loop's point lies in its plane, and in 1,173 the fitted normal and the frame's Z agree to one unit in the last place. Yet a cap written on the exactly square plane (the frame's Z; or X cross Y of the frame; or the frame's Z only where it agrees with the fitted normal to rounding) changes the same 89 of solidcore's own stored answers (`boolean.api_brep` 2, `primitive.api_primitives` 6, `topology.api_files` 64, `topology.assert_valid` 17), some by a lot (a body becomes failure 1001, a measure moves 2%). Those cases sit on an edge that the fitted normal's own rounding decides, so no form of the fix leaves solidcore's stored answers as they are, and a fix there would mean storing new answers for 89 cases, a judgement that is theirs. So the kernel keeps making cylinders, and any body with a one-circle cap, itself, exactly, with `geom-make`, and does not hand that operation to solidcore. The 20 tests stay named; each SC-5 entry of `tests/named-faults.json` carries `stays_ours` with this reason, and `the_kernel_makes_its_own_cylinder` shows it: the same cylinder (radius 0.5, height 1, on Q1) made by `geom-make` passes `geom-check` at its full level while the modeller's, made by solidcore, is refused at its top cap, and every SC-5 entry, and only those, is marked.
2. **SC-6 was the wrapper's, and is fixed in `convert.rs`.** Where two solids touch along an edge or at a vertex, solidcore's body has one edge or vertex both use; its record writer copies it once for each solid on purpose and returns an `Order` naming the one solidcore entity each copy is, which the conversion dropped. It now reads the order: the copies of one solidcore edge or vertex are one row of the kernel's body, shared by both shells, by that identity and never by where the copies lie (no weld by geometry). Where solids touch the body is non-manifold, and is published as the kernel's kind General (chapter 8.1: mixed and non-manifold), as it is: when an edge has more than two fins (an edge solids touch along has four, which a solid's edges may not, two each, the checker's group 1), or when the faces round a vertex do not make one fan, its corners lying in two shells or in two fans of one shell (a vertex solids touch at); otherwise it is a solid. `convert::tests::what_two_solids_touch_along_stays_one` (failed before: the record's 24 edges and 16 vertices came over as they were; and, in the main session's second round, the shared vertex's body came over as a solid) holds both bodies, each General and passed by the checker, and the fan test on rows made for it; bool-05 and bool-06 pass and leave the list. Of every body the tests make (226 conversions), the kind of one case moves with the vertex rule, bool-06 (and the unit test's own vertex case); bool-05 was General already, and the named list is otherwise unchanged. The checker itself refuses the kind Solid on a body with a four-fin edge (group 1, fin count) but passes a Solid whose two shells share a vertex: published as Solid, bool-06's body passed `Level::Full`, so a Solid's vertices are not yet held to one fan.
3. **SC-7 is named: solidcore's own suite needs upstream test files its public repository does not hold.** At the pin, solidcore's stored-answer run (`goldens --all --goldens`) gives 283 units, 151 passed and 132 failed: 68 stop at loading for an upstream test file not found, one finds no upstream file at all, and 57 have broken cases each reading an upstream file that is not there (SMLib's `upstream/TestFiles/pt_TestFiles`, which solidcore's CI checks out and its public repository does not ship); three units (two whose known failures name a case they do not make, one with 95 changed answers and nothing broken) and the changed answers of three others are not shown to come from the missing files, and the entry says so; and its whole suite fails one test, `wr_621_a_shallow_crease_too_small_to_round_is_refused_before_solving`, which reads `Fillet/nurb_cone.smb`. They are named one by one and counted under the list's `upstream` entry, not excused: each stays a named fault until it runs. `what_needs_upstream_files_is_named_and_counted` holds the counts to the lists. Until they run, a pin move cannot be judged by solidcore's own checks.
4. **The two far-off fitted normals are named, and nothing is said about whose fault they are.** Of the 1,175 one-ellipse loops of the measuring variant (its log, one line a loop, eight numbers: the first is 1 - |cos| of the fitted normal and the frame's Z, the seventh the radius and the eighth the loop's place in its call, as the variant's description gives them), two are far off: line 1,171, 1 - |cos| = 0.6666666666666667, so 70.53 degrees (|cos| = 1/3), and line 1,173, 1 - |cos| = 0.4226497308103744, so 54.74 degrees (|cos| = 1/sqrt(3)); each a circle of radius 0.9999999999999999, the first loop of its call, its other columns as every other line's. The log writes no file or case name, so which unit and case made each is not known from it.
5. **bldg-10 could not be done as ruled, and nothing was changed for it.** The ruling read a number of the building program's table as the shortest decimal naming the same double, expecting 0.20000010000000001 to be 0.2000001. It is not: the double the program held, 0.2 + 1e-7, is `0x3fc9999a70592e70`, and Rust's own printing of it is 0.20000010000000001, the table's own text; 0.2000001 is the next double below, `0x3fc9999a70592e6f`. A scan of every length of `tests/cases.json` finds no number written longer than its double's shortest decimal, so the rule moves nothing: bldg-10 stays WR-2, refused before solidcore is reached. Whether the table should write the gap as 0.2000001 (a different case) is the main session's to rule.

**Counts.** geom-solidcore has 115 tests (three new); on Linux 82 pass and 33 fail, exactly the named list (33 entries: SC-1, SC-4, SC-5 20, WR-1 6, WR-2, CK-1, CK-2 2, CK-3). `kernel.yml`'s jobs `solidcore` and `solidcore-wasm` ask for 115.

**Changes:** this record; geom-solidcore's `Cargo.toml` and `Cargo.lock` (geom-make and geom-op as development dependencies, this repository's own), `src/convert.rs` (`firsts`, `rows`, `form` and `body_of` read the order; the General kind; the unit test), `src/lib.rs` (the order passed on), `tests/cases.rs` (two tests) and `tests/named-faults.json` (SC-5's `stays_ours`, bool-05 and bool-06 out, `upstream`); `kernel.yml`'s two counts.

## 32.79 atan2's `Tint::mul` is its two steps, each with its own proof

**Decided by the main session, 19 Nov 2026** (its brief to the lane `proofs-2`), under the standing order that no job on a push to main runs over 15 minutes.

**What it was.** Kani's `math::atan2::proofs::mul_keeps_every_carry` took 1656, 1918, 2153 and 1934 seconds (27.6 to 35.9 minutes) on main's push runs 44244068917, 44299434285, 44312155060 and 44322372914. `Tint::mul` formed its six 64 by 64-bit partial products inside, the proof formed them again for its reference, and the solver had to find the two copies equal. #994 measured what does not shorten it: a cut by the word of the answer, the SMT solvers z3 and cvc5, and a copy of `mul`'s steps tied to `mul` (each ran to the job's 120-minute limit). The steps fed the proof's products once took 5.2 minutes (run 44246797569).

**What it decides.**
1. `Tint::mul` is `sum_partial_products` on `partial_products`, and nothing else. `partial_products` forms the six checked products, word for word the lines `mul` had; `sum_partial_products` holds every other line `mul` had, word for word: the carries, the exponents' sum, the signs' exclusive or and the normalisation. No line of arithmetic is added, removed or changed, so atan2 answers as it did, bit for bit.
2. Each step has its own proof, and neither forms the products twice. `mul_keeps_every_carry` keeps its name, its operands (every normalised or zero `Tint` twice, either sign, exponents below 2^60), its solver, its unwinding and every assertion on the sum: it runs the two steps as `mul` runs them, takes the six products its reference adds from the one `partial_products` the sum is given, and proves that `sum_partial_products` on them answers and that its answer is the reference's on those same six (457 seconds, 7.6 minutes, in run 44342665715). It does not prove which six products they are. `partial_products_are_the_six_products` proves that, for every pair of the same operands: `partial_products` answers exactly the six products written out in the proof itself, `ah·bh`, `ah·bm`, `am·bh`, `ah·bl`, `am·bm` and `al·bh` in that order, and `None` exactly when one of them overflows.
3. Why the two proofs are the old claim. Before, one proof said: for every pair of operands, `mul` answers, and its answer is the reference's on the six products the proof wrote out for itself; so a wrong pair of words in `mul` failed it. Now `partial_products_are_the_six_products` says that `partial_products` answers exactly those six written-out products (a wrong pair of words fails it), and `mul_keeps_every_carry` says that `sum_partial_products` on whatever `partial_products` answers is the reference's answer on them. `mul`'s body is those two calls and nothing else (its doc comment says it must stay so), so for every pair of operands the two proofs give `mul`'s answer as the reference's on the six written-out products: the old claim, with nothing assumed that it did not assume. The first push of this pull request had only the second proof, and so proved less: which six products are formed was then only tested (the main session's ruling on #999).
4. The quality ledger's seven mutants of the moved lines name `Tint::sum_partial_products`, where cargo-mutants now finds them; their line texts and reasons are unchanged.

**Changes:** this record; geom-num's `math/atan2.rs` (`Tint::mul`, `Tint::partial_products`, `Tint::sum_partial_products`, `proofs::mul_keeps_every_carry`, `proofs::partial_products_are_the_six_products`); `quality-ledger.json` (seven mutant names); `kernel-fuzz.yml`'s comment on the proofs and the new proof's row in its matrix.

## 32.82 One macOS x86-64 suite part built on macOS arm64 and run from its archive (a trial)

**Decided by the main session, 19 Nov 2026** (its brief to the lane `mac-cross`), under the rule that no job on a push to main takes more than 15 minutes. A trial on one part; whether the other 24 follow is main's ruling on the numbers below.

**What it was.** On main's pushes the macOS x86-64 release parts of `suite` ran over 15 minutes (two at 16 on the last push read). The work that runs long is the compile: 85 to 97% of each part, on an Intel runner whose speed swung about 2.1 times between runs (lane `mac-parts`' two readings). Part 21 (geom-bench's test targets `b` to `z`, 68 tests, one ignored) read 648, 717, 1,224 and 681 s on main's pushes 44322373019, 44312154950, 44299434218 and 44251982046, its test step 581, 664, 1,160 and 590 s (cargo's `Finished` 9 min 18 s of the first; the tests 13 s). The arm64 twin of the same part ran 3.5 to 11.9 minutes.

**What it decides.**
1. `suite` leaves out part 21 on macOS x86-64 alone (`exclude`, the whole entry and target). Its other parts, its other targets and the job `test` are unchanged.
2. `suite-cross-build` builds that part on macOS arm64 (`macos-latest`, `suite`'s macOS arm64 runner) for `x86_64-apple-darwin`: the same crates (the listing step is `suite`'s, byte for byte), the same test targets, `--release --locked`, the same `RUSTFLAGS` (`-C overflow-checks=on`) and debug assertions, packed with `cargo nextest archive` (cargo-nextest 0.9.146, Apache-2.0 OR MIT, already pinned) and handed to the run as an artifact of the run kept a day. With `--target`, cargo passes `RUSTFLAGS` to the target's code only, not to build scripts and proc macros; natively they had it, so the build sets `CARGO_PROFILE_RELEASE_BUILD_OVERRIDE_OVERFLOW_CHECKS=true` and the build overrides keep their overflow checks. Its cache (`suite-cross-release`) is saved by a push to main only, as every other.
3. `suite-cross` runs every test of the archive on the Intel runner (`macos-15-intel`), after the build, with the checkout as its workspace (`--workspace-remap .`, the same path as on the arm64 runner): `cargo nextest run --archive-file … --workspace-remap . --no-fail-fast`, no filter, no partition. The test binaries are the Intel runner's own target's, so what runs there is still x86-64 code under an Intel processor; only the compiler ran elsewhere.
4. A part with doctests (`doc`) is not moved this way: an archive holds no doctest. Part 21 has none.
5. No new action or tool: the artifact actions, the toolchain action and cargo-nextest are the pins the workflow already runs.

**How it is guarded.** geom-test's `tests/queue_wiring.rs`: `a_cross_built_part_runs_every_test_of_the_part_it_replaces` (`cross_part_problems`) holds `suite`'s exclusions to entries of its own release parts on macOS x86-64 alone, without doctests, and the two jobs' parts to those exclusions in order, their listing to `suite`'s, the build to macOS arm64, x86_64-apple-darwin, `suite`'s RUSTFLAGS and debug assertions and the build overrides' overflow checks, and the run to the Intel runner, after the build, every test of the archive; each rule shown to fail on a planted violation (a second part left out with no cross job, a part left out on Windows, a part that is none of `suite`'s, the build's or the run's part changed, a part with doctests, either listing changed, the build on the Intel runner, without debug assertions, without the target installed, for arm64, without RUSTFLAGS or the build overrides' checks, the run on arm64, not waiting for the build, a test filtered out, half the archive run). The archive is the fourth listed in-run hand-over (`an_in_run_hand_over_is_kept_a_day_and_read_in_its_run`, planted: kept a month, read by another job); the last job judges both jobs and requires them on a push, a run by hand and in the queue (planted: left out of `done`'s needs and of the queue's list). `tests/test_scope.rs` holds both jobs to the selection (planted: the archive made with no crate selected, the run without `NEXTEST_NO_TESTS`), and `tests/attack_g43.rs` to a row each in 32.43's table.

**What was measured.** By-hand run 44358724331 on 77deaaaea (19 Nov 2026). `suite` ran no native part 21 on macOS x86-64 (the exclusion holds; the run's other 445 jobs as before). `suite-cross-build` passed in 300 s on a cold cache (a branch saves none): set-up 3, checkout 5, toolchain and target 10, cache 8 (none found), cargo-nextest 1, listing 0, build and archive 258 (cargo's `Finished` 4 min 13 s, 115 crates compiled), upload 9. Main's last four readings of the same part on the Intel runner, job and build-and-test step: 648 and 581, 717 and 664, 1,224 and 1,160, 681 and 590 s, each with main's cache. The archive is 28,161,859 bytes (14 files: the part's six test binaries, geom-bench's four other binaries and nextest's metadata). `suite-cross` passed in 244 s after 2,498 s in the queue behind the run's other macOS x86-64 jobs: set-up 3, checkout 8, toolchain 10, cargo-nextest 1, listing 1, download 181 (the archive arrived in 9 s and its digest was checked; the action then sat 172 s before it ended, as it did in 8 other jobs of the same run, on Linux and on the Intel runner, so it is GitHub's and not this change's), tests 34 (nextest's summary 25.9 s). It ran the same tests as main's part 21 on run 44322373019: 68 run, 68 passed, 1 skipped, and the 68 names the same, one for one (the one skipped is the only ignored test of those targets, geom-bench's `ember` `the_pairs_digests_are_golden_after_both_passes`). Both jobs under 13 minutes: 5.0 and 4.1.

**What is not yet done.** Readings on more than one run (one run read; the runner swings). The other 24 parts: 13 of the 25 (1 to 9, 14, 20, 22 and 24) run doctests, which an archive does not hold, so moving them needs a decision on where their doctests compile; the other 11 can move as this one did. Predicted for all 25 parts, from the arm64 twins' readings (lane `mac-parts`: per-run medians 3.8 to 4.8 minutes a part, maxima 8.0 to 11.9) and this run: 25 more jobs (50 for the leg); each build job about 4 to 5 minutes, at most about 12 on the slowest arm64 reading; each run job about 1 minute of set-up plus the part's tests (median 23 s, part 14 up to 153 s, both before a slow runner's 2.1 times), so about 2 minutes, at most about 6; Intel minutes a push from about 260 to 320 (25 parts at medians of 10.4 to 12.9) to about 50, arm64 minutes up about 125, so about 115 fewer runner minutes a push in all; artifacts of the order of 2 GB a push kept one day (about 4 MB a test binary here, some 490 test binaries in the release suite), a cost of artifact storage main must weigh. A prediction, not a measurement.

**Changes:** this record and 32.43's rows for `suite-cross-build` and `suite-cross`; `kernel.yml` (`suite`'s `exclude`, the jobs `suite-cross-build` and `suite-cross`, `done`); geom-test's `tests/queue_wiring.rs` and `tests/test_scope.rs`.

## 32.83 orient2d's filter claim is proved one step of its check at a time

**Decided by the main session, 19 Nov 2026** (its brief to the lane `proofs-3`), under the standing order that no job on a push to main runs over 15 minutes.

**What it was.** Kani's `orient2d_proof_filter` (geom-fuzz's `tests/orient2d.rs`) took 570, 659, 658, 943 and 904 seconds of job time on main's push runs 44244068917, 44299434285, 44312155060, 44322372914 and 44351077673. In the last (job 134967462570) set-up took 34 seconds, the build 66 and Kani's verification 801, of which kissat's last solve, every remaining property at once, took 723: the solver, not set-up or the build. The harness proves `check_claim` with `Claim::Filter`, which is `check_with` on the reference's sign standing in for the predicate, the generated filter, and the reference standing in for the exact path. So the reference determinant is computed three times, once by `check_with` and once by each stand-in, and that solve had to find the three copies of its `i128` products equal while it proved the filter's error bound. Measured on a development machine (two harnesses at a time): the stand-ins alone, with the filter declining, 1213 seconds; the filter's step alone on one reference 564; the whole claim on one eighth of the domain (the three x coordinates' signs all positive) 1026. Cutting the domain does not separate the two costs, so this is a cut by step, not by case.

**What it decides.**
1. `check_with` is the reference and then its three steps, in the order it had them, each on its own function's answer for the case: `checked_reference`, then `exact_step` (the exact path returns the reference, every bit), `predicate_step` (the predicate answers the reference's sign) and `filter_step` (the filter declines or gives that sign). Each step holds, word for word, the lines and the finding `check_with` had for it; no check is added, removed or loosened, and `check`, `check_claim` and the fuzz targets judge every input as before. `check_with`'s comment says it must stay these four calls.
2. `check_filter_step(case, step)` takes the case's reference once, as `check_with` does first, and then one step on the function that step has in the filter's claim: `FilterStep::ExactStandIn`, the exact path's step on the reference computed again; `FilterStep::PredicateStandIn`, the predicate's step on the reference's sign computed again; `FilterStep::Answer`, the filter's step on `geom-num`'s generated filter. `FILTER_STEPS` lists the three.
3. Kani proves `check_filter_step` for each step, one harness and one job each: `orient2d_proof_filter_exact_stand_in`, `orient2d_proof_filter_predicate_stand_in` and `orient2d_proof_filter_answer`. Each has the old harness's domain (every draw of `proof_draws(PROOF_BOUND)`, no assumption narrowing it), its solver (kissat), its unwinding (10) and Kani's default checks of every arithmetic step, index and unwrap. `orient2d_proof_filter` keeps its name and its body as the whole claim, a test on every operating system, no longer a Kani harness; the test `the_filter_proofs_cover_every_step` holds `FILTER_STEPS` to the three harnesses, and the unit test `the_filter_steps_are_its_claim` checks the steps against the whole claim on fixed and drawn cases and that each step function fails on a wrong answer.
4. Why the three proofs are the old claim. The old proof said: for every case of the domain, `check_with(case, reference_sign, filter, reference)` answers `Ok` and nothing on its way panics or overflows. `check_with` is `checked_reference` and then the three steps, each step reached only when the ones before it passed, and every function it calls is a function of the case alone (no state, nothing shared between calls). So it answers `Ok` exactly when the reference is computed and each of the three steps passes on its function's answer for the case, which is what the three harnesses prove one step each, every one after the same `checked_reference`. Every check Kani made in the old proof is in one of the three: the reference's in all of them, each stand-in's and step's in its own harness, the filter's in `orient2d_proof_filter_answer`. A step is now reached even on a case where an earlier step would have failed, so each harness checks at least what the old one checked on its way to that step. Nothing is assumed that the old proof did not assume.
5. The guards follow the new rows: the matrix lists the three harnesses where it listed `orient2d_proof_filter` (geom-fuzz's `attack_g14` already holds the matrix to the proof tests), and the planted edits of `attack_g14` and geom-test's `fuzz_scope` that removed or replaced the old row now remove or replace `orient2d_proof_filter_answer`.

**Proof.** The pull request's own `Kernel fuzzing and proofs` run gives each of the three jobs' times; each must be under 13 minutes, and the predicate's parts, the exact path and the fuzz target must stand as they were.

**Changes:** this record; geom-fuzz's `src/orient2d.rs` (`check_with` and its steps, `FilterStep`, `FILTER_STEPS`, `check_filter_step`, the module header, a unit test), `tests/orient2d.rs` (the three harnesses, `orient2d_proof_filter` as a test, `the_filter_proofs_cover_every_step`), `tests/attack_g14.rs` (two planted edits), `src/lib.rs` and `README.md`; geom-test's `tests/fuzz_scope.rs` (one planted edit); geom-num's `src/predicate.rs` (one line of its header); `AGENTS.md`'s row for the fuzz targets; `kernel-fuzz.yml`'s comment on the proofs, its matrix and the example of its by-hand input.

## 32.91 solidcore's extrusion behind the kernel's own interface, and the building cases that are extrusions

**Decided by the main session, 19 Nov 2026** (its brief to the lane `solidcore-forms`), under decision 32.70's rules: an operation is solidcore's only when every one of the kernel's cases for it passes on Linux and under WebAssembly, every body that comes back is checked before it is returned, and a case that fails is a named fault with its one cause. The owner's rule for an operation not yet built held: the building program's cases were written as tests first, and every one failed before the operation existed ("the modeller has no extrusion yet").

**What it decides.**
1. geom-solidcore's `Modeller::extrude(profile, by)` is chapter 13.4's Extrude with distance ends and no taper, made by solidcore's linear sweep (`create_linear_sweep`, capped at both ends). It takes the kernel's own terms and gives the kernel's body: a `Profile` is an outer polygon of grid points (`geom_num::P3`) and its holes, each a `Hole::Polygon` of grid points or a `Hole::Circle` of a grid centre and a radius in whole steps (`geom_num::Steps`); `by` is the extrusion's vector in whole steps. No solidcore type is in the interface.
2. The order of a loop's corners does not matter. The wrapper decides each loop's turn exactly, from the corners' whole steps in i128 (the polygon's Newell normal against `by`), and hands solidcore the outer polygon turning positively about `by` and each hole negatively; a circle hole is written on a frame in the profile's plane turning negatively too. Before solidcore is reached it refuses, as the new `Error::Invalid`, an outer polygon or hole of fewer than three corners or with no area, a corner or circle centre off the outer polygon's plane, a vector in that plane, and a profile too large to decide that in i128.
3. The result is checked as every result is: solidcore's own validity check, then the record, the conversion into geom-topo's rows and geom-check at its full level (`checked`, `body_of`).
4. Booleans of extruded solids are made by the wrapper's booleans in the cases, as the earlier building cases are; decision 32.70's rule that flat-faced booleans stay on `geom-bool` is unchanged.

**The cases.** 21 of the 49 building and family cases 32.70 left waiting are extrusions, and each is a case of the table now (`bldg-NN`, by its row in the building program's table), with the closed form worked by hand and checked a second way as its expected answer: walls mitred at 30, 45, 60 and 90 degrees (two trapezoids on the bisector, extruded and united, bldg-04 to bldg-07); the sloped slab, alone and with a vertical round opening (bldg-32, bldg-33); seven roofs, two halves at a ridge, two thick planes at a ridge, a hip, a valley, a gable end wall and an eave wall each cut by roof planes, and a dormer (bldg-36 to bldg-42); and the family's four extruded forms, a rectangle, an L, a square with a round hole and a square with a square hole, each alone and as a void cutting a box, the two voids with holes leaving the core as a second solid (bldg-43 to bldg-50, faces given for the two-solid answers, 13 and 16). The mitre at 90 degrees is written exactly (every corner a grid point); the building program's own trigonometry gave cos 90 = 6.1e-17 and cot 45 = 1.0000000000000002, which are not the case. A unit test, `extrude_takes_its_loops_either_way_round`, holds point 2: bldg-49's profile given the other way round, or extruded downwards, is the same solid of volume 9 and 10 faces, and the three refusals are `Error::Invalid`.

**Measured (19 Nov 2026, a development machine, at the pin).** 15 of the 21 pass: bldg-07, bldg-32, bldg-36 to bldg-46, bldg-49 and bldg-50, every extrusion of a polygon profile, with polygon holes or none, and every boolean of them. geom-solidcore has 137 tests; on Linux 98 pass and 39 fail, exactly the named list; under wasmtime 49.0.2 (wasm32-wasip1, each test in its own process, as the job runs them) the same 137 ran, 98 passed and the same 39 failed, each with the same message, and none trapped. `kernel.yml`'s jobs `solidcore` and `solidcore-wasm` ask for 137.

**The named faults the extrusion cases add.**
- SC-8 (bldg-47, bldg-48): solidcore's linear sweep writes a round hole's circle and the top cap off the profile's plane. The hole's circle, given on the exact frame x (1, 0, 0), y (0, -1, 0) at z = 0, is turned a half-turn about x in binary64 and written with y axis (-2.7755575615628914e-16, 1, -1.2246467991473532e-16), the z part the binary64 sine of pi, so both circles leave their flat faces' planes; and the top cap is written on a plane with v axis (-1, 0, 8.564140683729681e-16) through z = 2.9999999999999987. Group 3 then cannot separate the circle from the square's sides of its own face (16 faults, the first "group 3, edge 5: no certified test of this pair exists yet (edge 4)"). A diagnostic run, not committed, with the record's numbers within 1e-12 of a quarter snapped to it, found what stands behind it: with the circles and vertices snapped, SC-5's signature (the cap against the side, "face 0 (face 1)"); with everything snapped, CK-2's (12 faults, the first "face 0 (face 3)", as bldg-25's wall with a round opening). It blocks the extrusion of a profile with a round hole; polygon holes are not affected.
- WR-3, ours (bldg-33): the conversion has no row for a record's ellipse (`convert.rs` writes lines and circles; geom-curve has an Ellipse kind), and the sloped plane cuts the vertical cylinder in two. The sloped slab itself and that cylinder both pass. It blocks every result with an elliptical edge until the conversion writes the kernel's ellipse.
- WR-4, ours (bldg-04, bldg-05, bldg-06): a mitre's corners at 30, 45 and 60 degrees are irrational, and the table writes the building program's binary64 values of them, which are whole numbers of steps of no grid; the modeller takes a profile's corners as grid points, so the case is refused before solidcore is reached, as bldg-10 is (WR-2). Whether the case is restated at an angle whose corners are grid points (a slope of whole numbers, such as 3 in 4) is the main session's to rule.

**So extrusion is solidcore's for polygon profiles with polygon holes, and not yet for a profile with a round hole** (SC-8, and behind it SC-5 and CK-2), on Linux and under WebAssembly as measured above, and on GitHub when the pull request's by-hand `kernel.yml` run shows both jobs.

**What is not yet shown.** The by-hand run of `kernel.yml` on the pull request (its jobs `solidcore` and `solidcore-wasm`). The other 28 waiting cases: the radial opening through a curved wall (a cylindrical box or an extrusion of an annular sector, whose profile is not a polygon), the revolve, and the family's sweeps, blends, revolves and swept blends. Taper, "up to" ends and symmetric ends (13.4). Faces named by role (`cap.start`, `cap.end`, `side`), which the converted body does not carry yet.

**Changes:** this record; geom-solidcore's `src/lib.rs` (`Profile`, `Hole`, `Modeller::extrude`, `Error::Invalid`, the module header), `tests/cases.rs` (the shape `extrusion`, the operation `extrude`, 21 cases and the unit test), `tests/cases.json`, `tests/case_names.txt` and `tests/named-faults.json` (SC-8, WR-3, WR-4); `kernel.yml`'s two counts.

## 32.84 A solid's vertex is one fan of one shell (issue #1005)

**Decided by the main session, 19 Nov 2026** (its brief to the lane `checker-vertex`), from chapter 8.1 (General is "mixed and non-manifold") and chapter 16 (faces that share only a vertex are non-manifold).

**What it was.** The checker refused a solid with an edge of more than two fins (group 1, `FinCount`), but passed a solid whose vertex two shells share, or where the faces of one shell make two fans: two cubes touching at a corner, united, were a solid that passed the full check (found by the work on #1001).

**What it decides.**
1. A corner is where a fin ends and the next fin of its loop starts. Two corners at a vertex are of one fan when a chain of edges at the vertex, each used by both of two corners in the chain, joins them. In a body of kind Solid every vertex's corners are of one fan and one shell; a vertex with corners in two shells, or in two fans of one shell, makes the body General, never Solid. It is the rule geom-solidcore's conversion gives a body its kind by (#1001's `non_manifold`), so the two agree, an edge that starts and ends at one vertex having one end there in both.
2. Group 1 holds the rule beside the fin partners it already judges against the body's kind (`geom-check/src/combinatorial.rs`, `fans`), with two fault kinds, `VertexShells` and `VertexFans`, each at the vertex and naming the first fin whose corner differs from the vertex's first. A solid whose checks so far found nothing is first walked fan by fan (`one_fan_each`): from a fin's corner to the corner of its next fin's partner, each fin once, with one word of scratch per fin and one per vertex. When no vertex is where two walks start, every vertex's corners are one set and one shell, so the body has no fault of this rule and no sets are built. Only a body where a second walk starts, or one with a fault already, is judged by the sets: linear in the corners plus the set operations with path halving, and its scratch two words per edge and two per vertex, which is now the cube's largest need (40 words, not 36). The sets alone, run on every solid, took `instructions::query::exactness` from 1,757,732 instructions to 2,841,700 (+61.7%, over the count gate's 5%): each fin's corner was read up to three times, in two passes, with a meter charge per fin in each and one per vertex. Counted in retired instructions on a development machine, the cheap check of a cube rose from 29,342 to 48,748 with the sets and of a triangular prism from 22,710 to 37,305 (a rise the gate's own count matches to within half a per cent); with the walk the cube's is 30,888 and the prism's 23,915, about 5.0% on the gate's 64 boxes and prisms. Group 1's loop check now reads each fin's ends once, keeping the next fin's for it, where it read them twice: the same faults, and 1,610 instructions fewer on the cube and 1,192 on the prism, so the two together leave the cube at 29,278 and the prism at 22,723, about what they cost before the rule.
3. The checker's work units rise by one per fin of a solid, charged once before the walk, and by two per fin and one per vertex more for a solid judged by the sets. On decision 32.48's 30 bodies less than exact the full set is now 4,479 units against the cheap groups' 2,292, and the largest of each kind are a box 546 (cheap 251), a prism 546 (251), a cylinder 84 (52), a cone 48 (29), a sphere 12 (6) and a torus 58 (44); `geom-test/tests/shipped.rs` holds them in place of 32.48's "With G134" numbers.

**Found, not changed here.** geom-bool's unions of boxes that touch only at a corner are published as solids of two shells sharing that vertex (geom-poly's `publish_with` reads a pinch within one shell only), and the checker now refuses them: geom-bool's `boxes_that_share_only_an_edge_or_a_corner_are_not_nudged_apart`, `any_boxes_keep_their_volumes` and `many_at_once_is_one_after_another_in_any_order`, and geom-test's `laws_booleans` tests that build such unions. Each is that operation's fault, for its own issue. Fixed with issue #1009: geom-poly's `pinched` reads a vertex as pinched when it has more than one fan, whatever the shells, so such a union is published General, and geom-bool's test of it expects General.

**Changes:** this record; geom-check's `combinatorial.rs` (`fans`, `one_fan_each`, `corner`, `slot`, `words`, `loops_close`), `report.rs` (`VertexShells`, `VertexFans`), `run.rs` (its cost), tests `faults.rs`, `memory.rs`, `attack_g102.rs`, `attack_g134.rs`; geom-test's `tests/shipped.rs`; chapter 8.3's group 1 text; MAP.json.

## 32.93 The conversion writes solidcore's ellipse as the kernel's own; bldg-33 then meets the checker's want of an ellipse test

**Decided by the main session, 19 Nov 2026** (its brief to the lane `wr3-ellipse`), under decision 32.70's rules: the named fault WR-3 of 32.91 was the wrapper's own, `convert.rs` having no row for a record's ellipse, so the difference of bldg-33 (the sloped slab less a vertical cylinder, whose sloped faces cut the cylinder in two ellipses) was refused as unconverted.

**What it decides.**
1. `geom-curve` has the kind: `CurveKind::Ellipse`, c + cos πt·u + sin πt·v by its parameter, the eccentric angle, in half-turns (chapter 7). solidcore's record writes an ellipse as origin + radii[0] cos t·x + radii[1] sin t·y on a right-handed orthonormal frame, on the same parameter. So the conversion writes it as the kernel's `Ellipse` on `Axes::Frame`, the record's frame (the same `Frame` a circle is written on), its semi-axes the record's radii in steps, and each edge's ends the eccentric angles of its two vertices, exactly as a circle's arc is written from the angles of its ends: an arc runs forward from its start's parameter to its end's, a closed edge one turn from its vertex. No point, curve or surface is moved. Its box is the frame's centre widened on every axis by the larger semi-axis.
2. A seam's end goes with the seam when what is left at it is one closed ellipse, as it does for one closed circle (32.70's seam rule in `form`), and that ellipse is then the full turn with no vertex, `full`. The body is checked as every result is: `checked`, geom-check at its full level.
3. **bldg-33 does not pass, and stays named, now as CK-4, the checker's.** With the ellipses written, the converted body reaches the checker, which refuses it: 2 faults, the first "group 2, curve 7: no certified check of this carrier exists yet (edge 7)". Group 2 certifies a line and a circle as an edge's carrier and nothing else (`geom-check`'s `Ends`; chapter 8.3 names an ellipse among the carriers that are a fault, `Unchecked`, never a pass), so each of the two ellipses is unchecked, as the kernel's own body of the same shape would be. bldg-33 leaves the conversion's list and joins the checker's: what it would take is a geom-check piece certifying an ellipse on a plane and on a cylinder (and at its vertices' ends) in group 2, and, behind it, group 3's pairs of an ellipse with its faces' other edges and of a sloped plane and a cylinder meeting along one. What stands behind group 2 is not measured.

**Tests.** `convert::tests::an_ellipse_is_the_kernels_ellipse` (failed first: "Unconverted(\"an ellipse\")") holds point 1 on a tilted frame: the kernel's ellipse on the record's frame and semi-axes in steps, an arc's parameters and its ends at the record's own points, an arc through the angle's wrap, a closed edge and the full turn. bldg-33 failed first as WR-3 ("an ellipse", unconverted) and fails now as CK-4.

**Counts.** geom-solidcore has 138 tests (one new); on Linux 99 pass and 39 fail, exactly the named list, the same 39 tests as before; under wasmtime 49.0.2 (wasm32-wasip1, each test in its own process, as the job runs them) the same. `kernel.yml`'s jobs `solidcore` and `solidcore-wasm` ask for 138.

**Changes:** this record; geom-solidcore's `src/convert.rs` (the ellipse's row in `curve` and `full`, `ellipse`, `reach`, the seam's end at a closed ellipse, the module and `form` documentation, the unit test) and `tests/named-faults.json` (bldg-33's entry, WR-3 to CK-4); `kernel.yml`'s two counts.

## 32.92 macOS x86-64's development crates built on macOS arm64 and run on the Intel runner

**Decided by the main session, 19 Nov 2026** (its brief to the lane `dev-cross`), under the rule that no job on a push to main takes more than 15 minutes (aim 13). The way is 32.82's, applied to `kernel-dev.yml`'s `native` job, whose macOS x86-64 run was the only one of its six over the line.

**What it was.** `Development crates (macOS x86-64)` took 18.3 minutes on main's push 78eed9501 (run 44382890859, job 135079615038, 1,097 s) and 6.6 to 11.2 on main's six pushes around it, with main's cache. The one cause of the 18.3 was the cache: its key was not found, thrown out by GitHub with the repository over its 10 GB of caches, though the pushes before and after restored the same key. With none, the job was nearly all compiling on the Intel runner: the dependency rule's step 451 s (cargo's `Finished` 7 min 15 s, its tests 14 s), the fuzz target's 259 (4 min 13 s, tests 5 s), the generator check's 131 (2 min 4 s), and the cache's save 50. The arm64 job of the same push was cold too and took 284 s (the same three compiles 1 min 27 s, 52 s and 29 s). What the job runs once built is short: the two test targets about 19 s and the four tools about 150 s (the generator check 6, the map 31, the ledger 38, the scan 75).

**What it decides.**
1. `native` runs on five of kernel.yml's six runners; macOS x86-64 leaves its matrix. Its steps are unchanged. Its build-cache step goes: since the repository's caches went over 10 GB only macOS x86-64's saved, so the other five found none and the step did nothing.
