2. `native-cross-build` builds on macOS arm64 (`macos-latest`, `native`'s macOS arm64 runner) for `x86_64-apple-darwin` exactly what each step of `native` builds, each as its step does and in its order: the two test targets with `cargo nextest archive --locked <the step's selection> --target x86_64-apple-darwin`, one archive each (so each is built alone, as its `cargo test` step builds it, with the same features), and the four tools with `cargo build --locked <the step's selection> --target x86_64-apple-darwin`. The profile is the steps' own (`test` for the archives, as `cargo test` uses; `dev` for the tools, as `cargo run` uses), and no flag is added: `native` sets none. The archives and the four binaries go to `native-cross` as one artifact of the run (`dev-macos-x86-64`), kept a day. Its cache (`dev-cross`) is saved by a push to main only; it takes the place of macOS x86-64's.
3. `native-cross` runs on the Intel runner (`macos-15-intel`), after the build: it makes the four tools executable (an artifact keeps no file mode), fetches the locked crates' sources (`cargo fetch --locked`: the dependency rule runs `cargo metadata` at test time), runs every test of each archive with `cargo nextest run --archive-file … --workspace-remap . --extract-to . --extract-overwrite --no-fail-fast`, no filter and no partition, and then each tool with its step's arguments (`check`), each a step named as `native`'s step is. The checkout is at the same path on both runners, so the archives' remapped workspace and the path each tool reads for the workspace (`env!("CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR")`) are this checkout. What runs is x86-64 code under an Intel processor; only the compiler ran elsewhere.
4. The tests run under nextest, one process a test, where `native` runs them under `cargo test`: the same 9 tests (the dependency rule's 6, the fuzz target's 3), none ignored.
5. No new action or tool: the toolchain, cache and artifact actions and cargo-nextest 0.9.146 are the pins kernel.yml already runs.

**How it is guarded.** geom-test's `tests/queue_wiring.rs::the_development_crates_built_on_arm64_run_every_step_of_native` (`dev_cross_problems`) derives what the two jobs must run from `native`'s own steps, so a step added to `native` that the cross jobs do not carry fails it: `native` and `native-cross` together on kernel.yml's six runners, each once; the run on macOS x86-64's runner and after the build, the build on macOS arm64's with the x86_64-apple-darwin target; neither job setting anything in its environment but the nextest pin (no RUSTFLAGS, no profile setting, no nextest filter); the build running exactly the folder's creation, each step's archive or build in `native`'s order and the tools' copy, and the run exactly the tools' mode, the fetch and each archive's whole run and each tool's run in `native`'s order; no step of either conditional or allowed to fail; and the build's cache saved by a push to main alone. The hand-over is one of `HAND_OVERS`, held by `hand_over_problems` to its job, its name, one day and its one reader. Each rule is shown to fail on a planted violation (22 planted edits, among them a seventh step in `native`, a test filtered out or half an archive run, a tool built but not run or run with `write`, a target built for arm64, a tool built in release, RUSTFLAGS or a nextest profile set, a step skipped, the run on arm64, a seventh runner, and two of the hand-over's). geom-test's `tests/attack_g16.rs::ci_runs_the_ledger_and_the_scan_on_every_runner` now reads the ledger, the scan and the map in `native` on five runners and in `native-cross` on macos-15-intel. 32.43's table has the two jobs' rows.

**What was checked.** No GitHub run was started for this record. On a development machine (Linux x86-64, natively, without `--target`): the two archives and the four tools built with these jobs' commands into a hand-over folder; from it, with the commands of `native-cross` in a checkout of the same commit, both archives ran every test and passed, 6 and 3, the same 9 names `cargo test --list` gives for the two targets, and each of the four tools exited 0. The dependency rule's archive also holds geom-test's 11 binaries (cargo builds a package's binaries for its integration tests), unpacked beside the test; the tools are still built on their own, as `cargo run` builds them, without the development dependencies' features a test build unifies in. On that machine, with the binaries' debug information inside them, the archives were 547 and 119 MB and the tools about 240 MB each, and the larger archive took 21 s to unpack; on macOS the debug information stays in the build's object files, so the hand-over there should be smaller, but its size was not measured.

**Predicted.** The build on macOS arm64: about 4 to 5 minutes with no cache (the three compiles of the arm64 native job were 2 min 48 s cold; building for another target compiles the build scripts and procedural macros for the host besides, as 32.82's build, 258 s for 115 crates cold, did), about 2 with main's. The run on the Intel runner: about 4 minutes, with or without a cache, as it compiles nothing (set-up, checkout and toolchain about 45 s, download, unpacking and fetch about 45 s, the tests about 25 s, the tools about 150 s); about 7 if the download waits as it did in 32.82's run (172 s). Against 6.6 to 11.2 minutes warm and 18.3 cold today. Each job under 13.

**What is not yet done.** A run on GitHub: a run by hand cannot be started (the workflow has no `workflow_dispatch`), so the first reading is this pull request's own run, whose `Development crates (macOS x86-64, built on macOS arm64)` must show the 9 tests of main's `Development crates (macOS x86-64)`, compared name for name, and both jobs' times.

**Changes:** this record and 32.43's rows for `native-cross-build` and `native-cross`; `kernel-dev.yml` (`native`'s matrix and cache, the jobs `native-cross-build` and `native-cross`, the header's comment); geom-test's `tests/queue_wiring.rs` and `tests/attack_g16.rs`.

## 32.90 Jobs that build the same dependencies share one build cache, named without the runner's own compilers

**What it was.** On 19 Nov the repository's Actions cache held 10,715 MB in 73 caches, every one saved by main, against GitHub's 10 GB limit, so GitHub threw caches out and jobs ran cold (`Development crates (macOS x86-64)` took 18.3 minutes on main's push 78eed9501). The list was already short of what main saves: main's push run of dde37567e restored five caches that were gone hours later (kernel-gate's `recursion` 459 MB, `clippy` 195 MB, `rules` 121 MB and `deny` 121 MB, and `solidcore-wasm` 414 MB) and saved kernel-quality's `when` (121 MB), so what main asks the cache to hold is about 12,145 MB in 79 caches.

**The cause.** Swatinem/rust-cache names each cache after the job that saves it, not after what it builds: the job's id and `key`, then an environment hash, then the hash of the lock files. So:
1. Jobs that build the same dependencies each save their own copy. cargo's unit graph (`--unit-graph`, which lists every unit cargo compiles without building) shows the same 109 units outside the workspace, the development build of geom-test's dependencies on Linux x86-64, for kernel-gate's `rules` and `deny`, `kernel.yml`'s `scope`, kernel-fuzz's `select`, kernel-golden's `store`, kernel-quality's `when` and kernel-release's `freeze` (seven names, about 121 MB each, for one build); and the same 132 units, geom-test's release test build, for `kernel.yml`'s `large-models` and kernel-oracles' `ember-build` (107 MB each). A test build reuses a development build's dependencies (cargo hashes a profile without its name), so `cargo test` and `cargo run` here compile the same units.
2. The environment hash counts `rustc -vV` of every toolchain installed on the runner, the runner image's own stable among them, besides the pinned one every job uses. GitHub rolls new images out over days, so one build gets two names: four caches held a second copy on 19 Nov (`builds`, kernel-dev's `wasm`, `release-shipped` and `suite-release` on Linux arm64, about 560 MB), and a job on the other image finds nothing (part 3 of `linux` found no cache under 5646d1c3, with 1.100.0 installed beside 1.99.0, in the same run in which part 1 restored the cache under face36f3, with 1.99.1 beside it). The hash also counts each workflow's own `CARGO_*` variables, so jobs of two workflows could not share a name even when they built the same.

The other caches are different builds: the wasm32 parts build four profiles (the guard's release, release with overflow checks, release as shipped, and debug), each system's goldens build two release profiles and a debug one, and `test` and `suite` differ in their release profile (decision 32.75, rule 4).

**What it decides.**
1. Two shared build caches. `geom-test-dev-<toolchain>` is saved by kernel-gate's `rules` (which runs on every push to main) and only restored (`save-if: false`) by `deny`, `scope`, `select`, `store`, `when` and `freeze`. `geom-test-release-tests-<toolchain>` is saved by kernel-oracles' `ember-build` and only restored by `large-models`. Each names the pinned toolchain (`RUST_TOOLCHAIN`), and its hash of the lock files counts `rust-toolchain.toml`, so a new toolchain is a new cache.
2. Every rust-cache step of the pinned compiler in `kernel.yml`, `kernel-gate.yml`, `kernel-golden.yml`, `kernel-fuzz.yml`, `kernel-oracles.yml`, `kernel-dev.yml`, `kernel-release.yml` and kernel-quality's `when` sets `add-rust-environment-hash-key: false`: a build is named once, whatever image the runner has. The lock file hash still renames a cache when a lock file, a manifest, `rust-toolchain.toml` or a `.cargo/config.toml` changes. kernel-quality's other caches (Miri and coverage on pinned nightlies that `rust-toolchain.toml` does not name), kernel-bench's (named by their toolchains already), `kit.yml` and `construct-server.yml` are unchanged.
3. No job is slower on a warm cache: each reader restores a cache holding exactly the dependency units it compiles (the same as its own copy held), and no step, test, filter or check is changed. A reader of a shared cache restores nothing the first time it runs after this lands, before its saver has run on main, as a job did whenever its own cache was thrown out.
4. Guarded by geom-test's `queue_wiring`: `jobs_that_build_the_same_dependencies_share_one_cache_one_saves` holds the two groups, exactly their jobs, one saver each, the rest `save-if: false`, no environment hash and no `key` of their own, one runner, and no other shared key, each rule shown on a planted violation; `a_run_by_hand_writes_nothing_later_runs_read` reads `save-if: false` as writing nothing, with a planted reader made to save.

**Expected total.** Each cache keeps its size but these: the seven development caches become one of about 121 MB, the two release test caches one of about 107 MB, and the second copies under another environment hash are not made. From 12,145 MB in 79 caches (19 Nov, with the five thrown out) to about 10,875 MB in 69: 1,270 MB less, and no longer doubled by an image rollout. That is still over the 8 GB this was meant to reach. What is left is builds that differ, so sharing cannot take it further: the oracle images (1,396 MB, kept in the cache by decision 32.75), `linux` (718 MB), `recursion` (459 MB), kernel-bench's `checks` (427 MB) and the two solidcore caches (834 MB) are the largest. Going under 8 GB needs a ruling of its own, for example on caches whose restore costs about what they save: in main's run of dde37567e the 685 MB `linux` cache took 12 to 20 seconds to restore on each of its 16 parts, and the part that found none compiled its dependencies in about 24 seconds more than a part that restored it.

**Old names.** The per-job caches that are now shared are no longer restored, so GitHub drops them after seven days unused (or they can be deleted by hand); `cache-prune.yml` drops the old copy of every other cache, whose name without its trailing hashes is unchanged, on its next daily run.

**Proof (main's by-hand runs).** None is started by the lane. After this lands, main's next push runs show `rules` saving `v0-rust-geom-test-dev-1.99.0-Linux-x64-<lock hash>` and `ember-build` saving `v0-rust-geom-test-release-tests-1.99.0-Linux-x64-<lock hash>`, and on the push after it `deny`, `scope`, `select`, `store` and `when` restore the first and `large-models` the second with a full match and no dependency compiled; the cache list then holds one copy of each.

**Changes:** this record; the rust-cache steps of `kernel.yml`, `kernel-gate.yml`, `kernel-golden.yml`, `kernel-fuzz.yml`, `kernel-oracles.yml`, `kernel-dev.yml`, `kernel-release.yml` and `kernel-quality.yml` (`when`); geom-test's `tests/queue_wiring.rs` (`SHARED_CACHES`, `shared_cache_problems`, the new test, the writer rule for `save-if: false` and its planted violations).

## 32.89 Every macOS x86-64 release part built on macOS arm64, and two Intel jobs for their doctests

**Decided by the main session, 19 Nov 2026** (its briefs to the lanes `mac-cross` and `mac-cross-2`), under the rule that no job on a push to main takes more than 15 minutes, and because GitHub's queue waits on Mac runners: on 19 Nov main read 51 Mac jobs running and 233 waiting, and none waiting on Linux.

**What it was.** Decision 32.82 moved part 21 of `suite`'s macOS x86-64 release leg to a build on macOS arm64 and a run from its archive on the Intel runner, and left the other 24 parts to a ruling on its numbers. Thirteen of the 25 parts (1 to 9, 14, 20, 22 and 24) also run their crates' doctests, which an archive does not hold.

**What it decides.**
1. `suite` leaves out all 25 release parts on macOS x86-64 (`exclude`, entry for entry). Its other targets and modes and the job `test` are unchanged.
2. `suite-cross-build` and `suite-cross` take all 25, as 32.82 set them out for part 21: the same crates, test targets, profile and flags, built on macOS arm64 for `x86_64-apple-darwin`, and every test of each archive run on the Intel runner, none filtered.
3. `suite-cross-doc` runs the doctests of the 13 parts that have them on the Intel runner, `suite`'s own release doctest step with its flags, in two jobs of whole parts: parts 20 and 24 (geom-bench and geom-fuzz) in one, parts 1 to 9, 14 and 22 (shares 1 to 8 from the job `scope`, geom-num, geom-test, geom-format) in the other, so the libraries a job's parts share compile once. The jobs are cut for a run with no cache (main's ruling of 19 Nov), because main's run has none whenever GitHub throws the cache out, as it threw out the macOS x86-64 development crates' cache before main's push 78eed9501 on 18 Nov. Each job's Rust cache is its own, saved by main's pushes alone.
4. No new action or tool.

**How it is guarded.** As 32.82, and geom-test's `tests/queue_wiring.rs` holds the doctest jobs' crates to the moved parts that have doctests, each such part to exactly one job, the jobs numbered 1 to n of n, their runner to the Intel one, their step to `suite`'s, and their cache to main's pushes (`a_run_by_hand_writes_nothing_later_runs_read`); each rule is shown to fail on a planted violation (a crate set apart or a share dropped, a crate kept by no job, a part in no job or in two, a part's number without its spaces, the jobs misnumbered). 32.43 has their row.

**What was measured.** By-hand run 44390876908 (19 Nov 2026) on a trial ref of this change on c86cc0527 with one more commit that let the doctest job save its cache, so that one guard failed by design: geom-test's `queue_wiring` `a_run_by_hand_writes_nothing_later_runs_read`, in the seven jobs that run it, and nothing else failed. Against main's push run 44351078119 on the same base: every one of the 25 parts ran the same tests, name for name (4,952 tests; the trial's part 18 has one more, 32.82's own guard), and the doctest job ran the same 50 crates' doctests, the same 4 doctests, all passing. With no cache (a branch has none): the 25 builds took 114 to 346 s (median 281, 110 minutes in all, on arm64), the 25 runs 40 to 333 s (median 101, 49 minutes in all, on the Intel runner), and the one doctest job then over all 13 parts 953 s (its step 883 s, cargo's `Finished` 14 min 30 s, 207 crates, 156 of them other projects' crates). Main's native parts on the pushes 44351078119 and 44382890859 took 298 and 303 Intel minutes, 531 to 1,311 s a part. So Intel minutes a push go from about 300 to about 75 (with the two doctest jobs), arm64 minutes up about 110, and every moved build and run job is under 6 minutes.

**How the doctest jobs were cut.** With no cache, a doctest job's compile time follows the compile work of the crates it needs (its crates, their development dependencies and everything those depend on). The work of each crate was read from cargo's own timings of the same build (`--timings`, one cold release build of the 50 crates with `suite`'s flags on a development machine, 20 min 3 s on 2 threads; 2,321 s of compile in all) and the crates each needs from `cargo metadata`. A straight line through the three cold doctest jobs measured on the Intel runner (all 13 parts: 2,321 s of work, cargo 870 s; part 5 alone: 1,429 s, 584 s; part 14 alone: 1,650 s, 617 s) is cargo seconds = 89 + 0.334 x work, within 23 s of each. Nearly every crate needs geom-test (495 s of compile, the largest by far) through its development dependencies, so every job pays about 1,430 s of shared work. Predicted cargo seconds, and the job's with about 80 s of set-up, cache and doctests: geom-fuzz alone 734 (about 13.6 minutes), geom-bench with geom-fuzz 749 (13.8), parts 1 to 9, 14 and 22 together 709 (13.2). No cut puts geom-fuzz's job under 13 minutes, so more jobs than two would not lower the longest (three jobs, geom-fuzz alone, would give 13.6, 12.9 and 12.7 at about 10 more Intel minutes a push). A prediction, not a measurement: main's by-hand run reads it.

**What is not yet done.** The two doctest jobs have not run: their times above are predicted. The trial's saved cache was gone within three hours (the repository's caches stood at 10.67 GB, over GitHub's 10 GB, and only main's were left), which is why the jobs are cut for no cache. The two jobs' caches are two more of main's (about 150 MB each).

**Changes:** this record and 32.43's row for `suite-cross-doc`; `kernel.yml` (`suite`'s `exclude`, the matrices of `suite-cross-build` and `suite-cross`, the jobs `suite-cross-doc`, `done`); geom-test's `tests/queue_wiring.rs` and `tests/test_scope.rs`.

## 32.97 A profile's corner on no grid is the exact fraction it is: the mitres by what they mean

**Decided by the main session, 19 Nov 2026** (its brief to the lane `wr4-mitre`): find the rule for a profile's corner that is on no grid, never by rounding a number silently and never by loosening the exact reading of 32.71. The rule below is the lane's finding, with its evidence; it is the main session's to accept.

**What it was.** WR-4 (bldg-04, bldg-05, bldg-06, 32.91): the walls meet at 30, 45 and 60 degrees, so the mitre's outer corner (-t cot(phi/2), -t) and the second wall's far corners are irrational, and the table wrote them as the building program's binary64 values. The modeller took a profile's corners as grid points only, and those values are whole numbers of steps of no grid, so each case was refused before solidcore was reached.

**The three candidates, weighed by exact arithmetic on the table** (the lane's script, `fractions` and 80-digit decimals):
- **(b) The binary64 value as an exact dyadic on a grid fine enough to hold it: no.** Each irrational corner is k / 2^e m with e from 44 to 54, and a grid holds it only with a step of at most 2^-54 m. The frame's grids are 1 / (2^11 5^k) m (Q0, Q1, Q2: 6.4e6, 1.6e8 and 4e9 steps to the metre, 32.71), and a coordinate is at most 2^40 - 1 steps, so such a step would give the frame a range of 6.1e-5 m. No grid of the modeller's design holds a building's binary64 corner, and a new grid is the numbers chapter's, not this crate's. And the value is not the corner meant: the binary64 second wall is not even square, the dot product of its two sides being about 3.6e-16, 4.7e-16 and 2.4e-16 m^2 exactly, not 0, for the three angles. Taken exactly, it is exactly a shape nobody meant, its deviation unstated.
- **A commit of the meant corners to the grid (chapters 7 and 13: a profile placed by a frame that is not a grid rotation is rounded to the grid first, and Certified): not for these cases.** It is the kernel's rule for its own grid-point geometry, and not silent, but it cannot meet the case's check unloosened: the meant second wall committed to the finest grid, Q2, moves each volume by 1.6e-11, 3.3e-11 and 4.3e-11 of itself, past the flat cases' 1e-12. Judging these cases by the commit's bound instead would loosen their check.
- **(a) The profile by what the building program means, its corners derived exactly: yes.** The building program means two walls of length L and thickness t from a shared inner corner, the second turned by phi, mitred on the bisector. phi itself has no exact rational form, so the turn is chapter 7's rational rotation, "exact as given, or the closest within the requested angle, and then Certified in angle" (32.14): the turn whose half-angle has the tangent n / m, its cosine (m^2 - n^2) / h and sine 2mn / h with h = m^2 + n^2, exact. n / m is the first convergent of tan(phi/2) within 5e-16 of it (7865521 / 29354524, 15994428 / 38613965 and 21489003 / 37220045 for 30, 45 and 60 degrees), so the turn is within 1e-15 rad of phi, and every corner is then an exact fraction of steps: the mitre's outer corner (-t m / n, -t), the second wall's far corners L (c, s) + t (-s, c) and L (c, s). The wall is square, the mitre on the bisector and shared by both walls, exactly; the one thing not exact, the angle, is stated with its bound and proved.

**What it decides.**
1. geom-solidcore's profile corner is `Corner`, a point given exactly as a fraction of the modeller's steps, `num / den` along each axis with `den` positive and every coordinate within the grid's 2^40 - 1 steps; a grid point is the corner of denominator 1 (`From<P3>`). `Profile::outer` and `Hole::Polygon` take corners; a circle's centre stays a grid point. A corner the caller derives, such as where two of the profile's sides meet, is given as the fraction it is, never rounded to the grid or to an f64 first.
2. The extrusion decides everything it decided before exactly on those fractions (num-rational 0.4.2's `BigRational` of num-bigint 0.4.8's integers, MIT OR Apache-2.0; num-traits 0.2.19 for their traits): each loop's turn about `by`, a loop with no area, a corner or circle centre off the outer polygon's plane, and a vector in that plane. Arbitrary precision removes the refusal of a profile too large to decide in i128, which no longer exists.
3. solidcore is handed each corner as the f64 nearest it in metres, num-rational's conversion, which rounds once and correctly: the same one rounding a grid point has always had at solidcore's door (`to_metres`, 32.70), and for a grid corner the same f64 bit for bit. Nothing is rounded before it. The result is checked and recorded Certified as every result is (`convert`), its bound solidcore's own tolerance and a step, which the corner's half unit in the last place of an f64 does not reach.
4. The table states the three mitres by what they mean: each wall is the shape `mitred_wall` (its length, thickness and height, and the turn: the angle in degrees, `tan_half` n and m, the quadratic whose root in (0, 1) is tan(phi/2), and `radians_within` 1e-15), and `tests/cases.rs` derives the corners as exact fractions. The expected answers, the closed form at phi and its bits, are unchanged. `mitre_turns_are_within_their_angle` proves each turn's bound by exact arithmetic: the quadratic (x^2 - 4x + 1, x^2 + 2x - 1, 3x^2 - 1) has opposite signs at 0 and 1, so one root in (0, 1), and opposite signs at n / m - 5e-16 and n / m + 5e-16, so its root is within 5e-16 of n / m and, atan being 1-Lipschitz, the turn within 1e-15 rad of phi; that each root is the tangent of the angle named is tied in f64 alone.

**Results.** The three cases pass, each within 1e-12 of its closed form, on Linux; WR-4 leaves the named list. bldg-07, the mitre at 90 degrees, passes as before. Tests first (commit c18c606a2: the cases restated and derived exactly, on the modeller that took grid points alone): bldg-04, bldg-05 and bldg-06 failed as WR-4, at "the corner [-939344768000000, -251696672000000, 0] / 7865521 steps is not a point of Q1" and its like.

**Counts.** On main: 137 tests, 98 pass, 39 fail, exactly the named list. Tests first: 138, 99 and 39 (the angle test passes). With the change: 141 tests (four new: the angle test; `a_profile_s_corners_are_exact_fractions_of_steps`; `a_triangle_on_each_axis_plane_is_decided_exactly`, a triangle on each axis plane off the origin, swept along and against its axis, whose prism's first vertices turn positively about the vector, so solidcore was handed it counter-clockwise; and, as a module test in `src/lib.rs`, `a_round_hole_is_handed_to_solidcore_on_its_frame`, below), 105 pass and 36 fail, exactly the named list of 36 entries; every test that passed on main passes, and the other 36 named tests fail as before, each for its own named cause.

**Mutants.** cargo-mutants 27.1.0 on every line of `src/lib.rs` the change touches (`--in-diff`), each judged by the integration tests that reach the extrusion (every extrusion case and the four extrusion tests, less the named faults): 51 mutants, 43 caught, 5 unviable, 3 missed, the 3 on the circle frame's x axis along the outer polygon's first edge (`xa`), a line the diff holds as context: each turns a round hole's frame within the profile's plane, which moves where the circle starts and nothing else, and every extrusion with a round hole is refused at SC-8, so no extrusion's result can show it. A test that held SC-8's report as its answer was not kept (the main session's ruling: the named list holds which cases fail and why). Instead the frame is built, unchanged, in `Modeller::handed_circles`, which returns what solidcore is handed for each circle, and the module test `a_round_hole_is_handed_to_solidcore_on_its_frame` holds it exactly: bldg-47's profile, at z = 0 and 1 m, is refused neither as an input nor by solidcore; and on eight outer polygons (bldg-47's square at both heights, and squares of side 3 m off the origin on a plane square to each axis, their first edge along each axis and once against one) each circle's origin and radius are exactly its centre and radius in metres, its x axis exactly the unit vector along the first edge, and x cross y exactly the opposite of the polygon's unit Newell normal. Then cargo-mutants 27.1.0 on every line of `src/lib.rs` that the diff against main adds or holds as context, each judged by the whole suite less the named faults: 100 mutants, 95 caught, 5 unviable, 0 missed. The frame's 44 mutants are all caught by that test alone; without it, 41 of them are missed. Two lines were written so that their mutants can be seen: `dot` is the sum of the components' products, and the circle frame's normal is the Newell normal of the outer polygon as solidcore is handed it, with no sign of its own. `kernel.yml`'s jobs ask for at least 137 tests, which still holds, so the workflow is not changed.

**What is not yet shown.** The run under wasmtime of the same tests on GitHub (the pull request's by-hand `kernel.yml` run, the main session's). WR-2 (bldg-10, a gap the table writes as 0.20000010000000001 m) is a length, not a derived corner, and stays as 32.76 left it: the rule here gives the table a way to write a derived corner exactly, not a reason to read a length any way but exactly. WR-1's planes take a point, not a profile, and stay. A modeller that derives a profile's corners from its sides (chapter 15's depth-one points) rather than taking them derived. Found on the way, not a case of the table: the triangle (1, 0, 0), (0, 1, 0), (0, 0, 1) m, a profile on a plane square to no axis, swept by (0, 0, 1) m or by (1, 2, 1) m, comes back with a spline edge, which the conversion refuses by name ("a spline curve"); every profile of the table lies on a plane square to an axis.

**Changes:** this record; geom-solidcore's `Cargo.toml` and `Cargo.lock` (num-bigint, num-rational, num-traits), `src/lib.rs` (`Corner`, `Profile`, `Hole`, `Modeller::extrude`, `Modeller::corner`, `Modeller::handed_circles`, the exact `diff`, `dot` and `newell`, the module header, one module test), `tests/cases.rs` (the shape `mitred_wall`, `lengths`, `handed_turn`, three tests, corners in the extrusion tests), `tests/cases.json` (bldg-04, bldg-05, bldg-06) and `tests/named-faults.json` (WR-4 out).

## 32.100 solidcore's pin moves to the fork's exact half-turn and exact level caps; ten SC-5 cases pass

**Decided by the main session, 19 and 20 Nov 2026** (its brief to the lane `sc-pin` and its rulings on that lane's readings), under decision 32.70's rules: the pin moves only through every one of the kernel's cases first, and a fault of solidcore is fixed on the fork's branch.

**What it decides.**
1. The pin moves from `cb9bee4bb9f56880c9973a9f8e99c83fdec24ad3` to `1d8e14b46dedccbb03572956e7c708782fef1b8f`, the head of the fork's branch `ours/sc8-half-turn`, four commits on the old pin: the linear sweep and `arc_sweep_cylinder` turn a frame a half-turn exactly (`Axis2Placement::half_turn_about_x`, where `180 * PI / 180` radians rounded the Y and Z axes by the binary64 sine of pi); `OrderCurvesIntoLoops` fits a flat face on a loop whose segments close exactly with Newell's sums about the first curve's start, and takes a coordinate every loop point shares as the base point's exactly, so a cap on loops at one height lies on that height, square to its axis; and solidcore's own stored answers that this moves, each read to its one cause.
2. The kernel's cases: the cylinder of radius 0.5 and height 1 on the origin now has both caps exact and passes the kernel's checker as solidcore makes it, and ten tests leave the named list: isect-21 to isect-27, isect-40, isect-41 and `results_are_the_kernels_bodies`. `convert::tests::a_cylinder_fails_on_its_top_cap_alone` becomes `a_cylinder_has_its_caps_exact` (both caps' planes square to the axis, through z = 0 and z = 1 exactly, and the body passes), and `the_kernel_makes_its_own_cylinder` becomes `a_cylinder_passes_as_solidcore_makes_it` (the modeller's cylinder and geom-make's both pass at the checker's full level, three faces each, no entry of the named list is marked `stays_ours`, and every SC-5 entry names the cause that is left).
3. **32.76's first point is replaced.** It kept the cylinder the kernel's own for good because a cap written square moved 89 of solidcore's stored answers that no one could judge. Here every stored answer that moves is read and judged (below), so `stays_ours` goes from the list and its sentence from the list's `about`. Whether the cylinder becomes solidcore's operation is 32.70's rule: when every one of its cases passes, which eight do not yet.
4. **What is left of SC-5 (eight tests: bldg-12, bldg-14, bldg-15, bldg-24, bldg-28, bldg-29, bool-22, bool-24) is solidcore's rational evaluator.** A cap is fitted through points of its circle, which are the circle's rational quadratic B-spline evaluated by `crv_eval` (solidcore's `nurbs/b_spline_curve/p1.rs`, through `Curve::evaluate_point`); it evaluates homogeneously and so can round a coordinate every control point shares by a unit in the last place. Measured (a probe, not committed): the cylinder of radius 0.5 on (1, 1, -1), height 4, has its top cap through z = 3.000000000000001 with u axis (0, 1, -1.1418854244972906e-15); bldg-28's cylinder on (4, 2, -0.1) has its top cap through 0.35000000000000003 and its bottom cap tilted; while the cylinders of the unit case, isect-21, bldg-12's inner one, bool-23 and bool-27 have both caps exact. SC-8 (bldg-47, bldg-48) is the same cause at the extrusion's top cap: its circles are now exact in their faces' planes (the half-turn), and the cap through the hole's circle translated to z = 3, which `crv_eval` evaluates at z = 3.0000000000000004, is not; it fails with 22 faults, the first "group 3, edge 0: no certified test of this pair exists yet (edge 4)".
5. **bool-23 and bool-27 move from SC-5 to CK-3, the checker's.** Their cylinders pass now, and each difference is refused at a pair group 3 has no certified test of: in bool-23 two half-circles of one circle meeting at their two vertices (39 faults, the first "edge 11 (edge 12)"), in bool-27 the column's circle and the tool's, tangent, each with its one vertex (3 faults, the first "edge 2 (edge 0)").

**solidcore's own suite at the new pin** (a development machine, goldens profile as the fork's CI builds it; SMLib's upstream test files are absent at both commits, SC-7). The stored answers: 283 units, 151 pass and 132 fail, the same units as at the old pin (the failing ones read missing upstream files, or move at the old pin already), and no case moves from the old pin but by the lines blessed for the reasons here. The cap commit as first written moved 206 stored answers; read against exact arithmetic, two of its changes were wrong and were narrowed before the pin moved: the normal of a chain whose segments do not close is a different exact quantity about another point (one revolve's two exact normals were 26.6 degrees apart, and its status changed), so such a loop keeps upstream's sum; and the base point's average about that point was less exact in the coordinates the points do not share, so it is upstream's average there. What stays moved, blessed: `topology.api_files` 57 files of cylinders and cones (their caps), `topology.assert_valid` 17 cone checks (a vertex's largest gap from 4.97e-16 to 4.44e-16), and `primitive.api_primitives` 17 cases whose bodies are the same valid bodies at both commits (solidcore's own check passes them, their counts, vertices and edges equal) with an edge's curve run the other way, and one pyramid at coordinates of 5e9 refused before (upstream's sums gave its side planes 2e-8 radians off) and built now, valid, the pyramid at the origin moved there within half a unit in the last place. The test `wr266_public_heal_stage3_failure_does_not_quarantine_the_face` compares its healed body's region void flags as a set: their order is the rounding's (at the old pin the same flat cylinder lists its void region first at six origins and last at (10, 10, 10)). The whole workspace's tests, doctests, rustfmt and clippy with warnings denied were run at the new pin; the tests that fail are the 85 that fail at the old pin, every one reading a missing upstream file.

**Counts.** geom-solidcore has 142 tests (main's, with 32.97's). At the old pin, on Linux and under wasmtime 49.0.2 (wasm32-wasip1, each test in its own process, as the job runs them): 106 pass and 36 fail, the named list. At the new pin: 116 pass and 26 fail, the named list of 26 entries (SC-1, SC-4, SC-5 8, SC-8 2, WR-1 6, WR-2, CK-1, CK-2 2, CK-3 3, CK-4), the same on both targets, none trapping. `kernel.yml`'s two counts stay 142; the two cache keys name the new pin.

**Changes:** this record; geom-solidcore's `Cargo.toml` and `Cargo.lock` (the pin), `src/lib.rs` (the method line), `src/convert.rs` (the unit test), `tests/cases.rs` (the test) and `tests/named-faults.json`; `kernel.yml`'s two cache keys.

## 32.95 Group 2 certifies an ellipse on a plane and on a cylinder, and at its ends

**Decided by the main session, 19 Nov 2026** (its brief to the lane `ck4-ellipse`, issue #1023), under chapter 8.3's rule that a carrier is certified with bounds, not sampled. Decision 32.93 found the want: with solidcore's ellipse written as the kernel's own, bldg-33 (the sloped slab less a vertical cylinder) reaches geom-check, whose group 2 refused it, "group 2, curve 7: no certified check of this carrier exists yet (edge 7)", named fault CK-4.

**What it decides.**
1. **An ellipse is one of group 2's curves.** `geometric::Ends` takes `CurveKind::Ellipse` beside the line and the circle: its ends are its domain's, each vertex must lie within the body's bound of its point there, and each fin's edge must lie within the bound of the fin's face. Nothing else in groups 1, 2 or 4 changes.
2. **On a plane, the circle's bound, widened to two vectors.** With p(t) = c + cos πt·u + sin πt·v (u = a·x̂ and v = b·ŷ on the ellipse's exact frame, or the vectors stored when a transform that is not a similarity made them), (p − o) · n = (c − o) · n + cos πt·(u · n) + sin πt·(v · n), whose largest size over a turn is |(c − o) · n| + √((u · n)² + (v · n)²). Divided by |n| it bounds every point's distance from the plane, and it is that largest value, not a step wider, so an ellipse exactly a step off its plane fails at a bound of nothing and passes at a bound of one. The circle's bound is the case u = r·x̂, v = r·ŷ.
3. **On a cylinder, the ellipse's shadow across the axis.** (p − C) × n̂ = (c − C) × n̂ + cos πt·X + sin πt·Y, with X = u × n̂ and Y = v × n̂. So a point's distance from the axis is within δ = |(c − C) × n̂| of |cos πt·X + sin πt·Y|, which runs over [B, A], the semi-axes of that shadow: A² and B² are the extremes m ± s of |X|² cos² + |Y|² sin² + 2 X · Y cos sin, with m = (|X|² + |Y|²) / 2 and s = √(((|X|² − |Y|²) / 2)² + (X · Y)²), and B = |X × Y| / A, since their product is |X × Y|². A point's distance from the cylinder is |ρ − R|, so every point lies within δ + max(|A − R|, |B − R|) of it. For every plane's cut of a cylinder the centre is on the axis and the shadow is the circle of radius R, and the bound is 0. The square root of s is taken as a norm, which cuts a lower end that widening pushed below zero: the first run failed closed there, a sum of subnormal squares widened below zero, on a frame whose x̂ is (4, 0, 3) / 5.
4. **Ends as a circle's.** The point at t is enclosed from certified sines and cosines of half-turns, so an end holds to the step they widen by, as a circle's end does.
5. **Elsewhere, unchecked.** An ellipse on a cone (whose radius changes over the height the ellipse spans), a sphere or a torus is `Unchecked`, at its curve and with the face, never passed and never `EdgeOffSurface`. An ellipse whose numbers cannot be enclosed (a square past a double) is `EdgeOffSurface`, fail-closed, as a line's is.
6. **Group 3 is unchanged and fails closed.** `self_intersection`'s shape of an edge is none for an ellipse, so every pair of an ellipse with another edge of its face is `PairUnchecked`, and a sloped plane and the cylinder it cuts are left unknown, as before.

**What group 3 needs for bldg-33, for the main session to rule.** bldg-33's body has two sloped faces, each with an outer loop of four lines and the ellipse as its hole, a cylindrical face between the two ellipses, and four side planes. Group 3 would need:
- (a) *A sloped plane and the cylinder sharing a full ellipse.* A plane not parallel to a cylinder's axis meets it in exactly one ellipse, so faces on them that share a full ellipse edge meet only along it, as `Pair::Circle` reasons for a plane square to the axis. This follows from these pieces: the normal's dot product with the axis certainly not zero, the slope exactly zero, and a full-turn ellipse edge shared. A test must also show a plane within the bound of an ellipse yet parallel to the axis (a tangent plane and a thin ellipse) is not passed. Not built here.
- (b) *The two ellipses of the cylindrical face, in the two parallel sloped planes.* One is certainly apart from the other when point 2's bound, taken signed against the other's plane (normal u × v), certainly excludes zero; and a seam line from one to the other, if solidcore writes one, leaves each ellipse's plane from their shared vertex, the circle's shared-vertex test with that normal. This follows from these pieces too. Not built here.
- (c) *The ellipse against the four lines of its own sloped face.* They lie in the ellipse's plane, and the circle's test of a segment in its plane needs that plane's test to be exactly zero, which a sloped frame's enclosures never are. It needs a new piece: a test in the face's own plane (the segment certainly farther from the centre than the ellipse reaches, or the segment mapped by the conjugate axes onto the unit circle). Not built.
- (d) *CK-2.* Each sloped face holds an ellipse, so it is not an outline, and group 3 tests it against the four side planes that cross it by carriers alone, which certify nothing for crossing planes; and the cylinder against the side planes, parallel to its axis, has no test either. Both are CK-2's (bldg-25 and bldg-26 wait on the same), not an ellipse's. So bldg-33 cannot pass on (a) to (c) alone: it waits on CK-2 whatever is done here.

**Tests** (geom-check's `tests/attack_g85.rs`, through the public interface). They failed first (commit 21f2d4ee5: 18 run, 13 passed, 5 failed, each ellipse `Unchecked` at its curve with its edge) and pass now (18 of 18):
- `an_ellipse_on_a_plane_and_on_a_cylinder_passes_group_2`: a cylinder of radius 4 cut square below and on the plane of normal (−3, 0, 4) above, its top an ellipse of semi-axes 5 and 4, on its frame and as the vectors (4, 0, 3) and (0, 4, 0), exact in the second form at a bound of nothing; and a flat ellipse on a flat sheet both ways.
- `an_ellipse_a_step_off_its_surface_is_refused_with_its_group_and_fault`: lifted a step off its plane, tilted a step off it, a semi-axis a step wide of the cylinder, short of it, long of it on a frame, and raised up the axis off the sloped plane: `EdgeOffSurface`, group 2, named in words, at the bound below and not at the bound.
- `an_ellipse_whose_ends_miss_its_vertices_is_refused`: the vertex 3 steps out (off at 2, on at 4), the ellipse turned to start at its short axis (both ends √52 off), the cylinder's top vertex a step down the seam: `VertexOffCurve`, group 2, named in words.
- `an_ellipse_on_a_surface_without_its_check_is_unchecked_never_off_it`: on a cone and a torus, `Unchecked` with the face; numbers past a double, off the plane and the cylinder.
- `carriers_without_a_certified_check_are_reported_never_passed`: its ellipse case, which held only while every ellipse was unchecked, is now an ellipse on a sphere, unchecked at the dome.

**Cost.** Each check is one closed form: about 40 interval operations on a plane, 95 with three square roots on a cylinder, 30 with a certified sine and cosine at an end; constant per fin and per edge, nothing allocated, one meter unit per row as before.

**Changes:** this record; chapter 8.3's as-built text (`08-topology.md`); geom-check's `src/curved.rs` (`Oval`, `Carrier::holds_ellipse`, `at_ellipse_end`, the module documentation), `src/geometric.rs` (`Ends::Ellipse`, `edge_on` answering none for an ellipse it has no test of, the fin's `Unchecked` fault, the module documentation) and `src/self_intersection.rs` (an ellipse has no shape, the module documentation); `tests/attack_g85.rs`.

## 32.96 Group 3 certifies a flat face holding a circle against a crossing plane, and a cylinder against a plane along its axis

**Decided by the main session, 19 Nov 2026** (its brief to the lane `ck2-circle`, issue #992, named fault CK-2), under chapter 8.3's rule that a pair is certified with bounds, not sampled, and is otherwise a fault. The building cases bldg-25 and bldg-26 (a wall with a round opening) were refused by group 3 with 12 faults, "no certified test of this pair exists yet": each of the wall's two faces round the opening against each of the four sides, and the opening's cylinder against each side. A flat face with a circle among its edges is no outline, so it was tested against a crossing plane by the carriers alone, which certify nothing for crossing planes; and nothing certified a cylinder against a plane along its axis. Lane `sc8-fork` found the same 12 pairs on bldg-47's solid made exact (a square of side 2 with a round hole of radius 0.5, extruded 3).

**What it decides.**
1. **A flat face that keeps off a plain face's plane but for what they share** (`planar::keeps_off`). A flat face with no outline is apart from a plain face (one read on its plane with every corner a grid point) when every corner of it lies on one side of the plain face's plane or on it, each by an exact sign, every circle of it lies certainly on that side, and what it has on the plane is one vertex the plain face shares or one side along the plane that is an edge of the plain face (and its two ends). Group 3 already reads a flat face as lying among the mixes of its boundary's points (`curved::pair`: "every point of it a mix of corners that do"), so the plane's equation, of one sign or zero along the whole boundary, is so over the face, and zero only at mixes of the boundary's points on the plane: that one vertex or that one side. The plain face lies in its plane, so the two meet only there. The face need lie on no plane exactly, which is what bldg-25 needs (point 4).
2. **A circle's side of a plane** (`curved::circle_side`). Over the whole circle the plane's equation n · p + d runs over n · c + d ± r·|ẑ × n|, its largest affine reach, as group 2's bound of a circle on a plane reads it; the circle is on a side when that whole range certainly is. The plane is the plain face's written plane given by integers, which must be the plane its outline was read on: the very `geom_num` plane the corners are signed against, so the corners' exact signs and the circle's enclosed one are of one plane, its normal the same way round. Its integer coefficients (up to 2^125) are enclosed by the doubles either side of each one's nearest, so the circle's sign is certified, not exact. Taking that plane, and not the face's carrier, leaves no case where the surface is not a plane (the coverage gate found the branch for one, which no input could reach). An arc is taken by its whole circle.
3. **A flat face on its integer plane exactly** (`planar::rests_on_flat`). A flat face with no outline whose every vertex and edge, circles among them, lies on its written plane at a bound of nothing, that plane given by integers, lies in that plane; a plain face that reaches the plane only at vertices and along edges the two share (the test `planar::rests` asks of two plain faces) meets it only there. This settles a plain face standing on a holed face from one side, which point 1 cannot, the holed face having corners on both sides of the plain face's plane.
4. **A cylinder and a plane along its axis** (`Cone::beside`, in `curved::pair`). A plane whose normal's dot product with a cylinder's axis is exactly 0, the slope exactly 0, is apart from it when ((C − o) · n)² > (R + s)² · |n|², s being what the two faces may stray (the body's bound for a face not on its surface exactly, nothing otherwise, as for two parallel planes), on R + s's certified upper end. Every point of the cylinder is within R of the axis, and every point of the axis is at one distance from such a plane, so no point of the one face comes within s of the other. A plane at exactly the radius is not apart, nor one that leans by any amount (it meets the cylinder somewhere), nor a cone.
5. **Everything else stays `PairUnchecked`**: a face with corners or circles on both sides, a corner on the plane that the plain face does not share, two corners on it that are not the ends of one side along it (the segment between them is among the mixes, and the plain face need not hold it), an edge of another kind, a plane not given by integers for point 3, a face that may stray from its plane for point 3.

**Found, not changed.** bldg-25's body as the conversion writes it has its top circle's centre 7.5e-9 steps above the top's plane (solidcore's 0.20000000000000004 m), and its bottom circle exactly on its plane is still refused by group 2 at a bound of nothing: the circle's bound on a plane divides by |n|, which for spanning vectors of 1.6e8 steps is no double, and the quotient's enclosure of 0 widens to a subnormal. Both are within the body's bound of 3202 steps, so group 2 passes them, but point 3 cannot use either face; point 1 needs neither. The division is group 2's to tighten (the comparison could be made, as `flat` makes it, against b · |n|), for its own issue.

**Tests** (geom-check's `tests/attack_g85_group3.rs`, through the public interface). They failed first (commit 06354df3f: 15 run, 12 passed, 3 failed, each on `PairUnchecked` of the pairs named) and pass now:
- `a_wall_with_a_round_opening_passes`: the wall 40 by 30 by 10 with an opening of radius 4, at bounds of 1 and 0, near each side, and through its ends (the faces holding the circles listed after the sides); bldg-25's own numbers on its grid, the top circle 7.5e-9 steps off its plane, at its bound of 3202; bldg-47's solid made exact as `sc8-fork` made it, the circles' normals pointing down; and an opening touching a side, whose cylinder and that side stay unchecked while the face holding the circle is apart from every side.
- `a_flat_face_holding_a_circle_is_apart_from_a_face_that_keeps_off_its_plane`: a square standing a step above and a step below the holed square passes; one standing on its plane along a line it holds, one crossing it, and the same square above the hole lifted or tilted a step within the bound are unchecked.
- `a_flat_face_holding_a_circle_keeps_off_a_plane_only_where_certified`: a plane beyond the holed square passes; one through its corner that it does not share, one standing on its side sharing the two vertices but not the edge, and a notched face sharing two corners and no side are unchecked; a disc against a plane at its rim, on either side, is unchecked, and a step beyond its rim passes.
- `a_cylinder_and_a_plane_along_its_axis_are_apart_only_beyond_its_radius`: a step beyond the radius either side and across passes; at the radius, inside it, leaning a step over a height of 1000, beside a cone, and a bent square that may stray onto the cylinder are unchecked; the bent square a step further out passes.
- Two tests moved with the check: `pairs_without_a_certified_test_are_reported_never_passed`'s holed square against a plane wholly beyond it now passes, and the same plane across it stays unchecked; `attack_g134`'s `a_bent_face_with_a_second_loop_has_no_star` has its neighbour across the bent face (x = 5), where a star would find the crossing, since at x = 20, beyond every corner, point 1 now certifies the pair without a star.

**Measured (19 Nov 2026, a development machine).** geom-check: 191 tests, all pass. geom-solidcore at the pin: bldg-25 and bldg-26 pass and leave `tests/named-faults.json`; on Linux 137 run, 100 pass and 37 fail, exactly the named list (CK-2's entries gone); under wasmtime 49.0.2 (wasm32-wasip1, each test in its own process, as `solidcore-wasm` runs them) the same 137 ran, 100 passed, the same 37 failed and none trapped. `kernel.yml`'s count of 137 tests is unchanged. The cost, on bldg-25's solid and bldg-47's exact one in geom-check (release build, 2,000 full checks each, on a machine other jobs shared): 553 meter units each, against main's 471 (which stopped at 12 unchecked pairs), no logical bytes either way, and about 315 and 335 µs a check against main's 224 and 235; a box, whose path did not change, took 522 units both ways and 151 against 120 µs, so a part of the time is the machine's load. Each new test is linear in the fins of the face it reads, one meter unit each.

**Mutants.** Every changed line of `src/` against geom-check's integration tests (cargo-mutants in diff mode with the repository's settings, release build): 44 mutants, 41 caught, 0 missed, 0 timed out, and 3 unviable, the `Default::default()` replacements of `pair`, `circle_side` and `bare_meets_plain`, whose types have no default.

**Changes:** this record; chapter 8.3's as-built text (`08-topology.md`); geom-check's `src/planar.rs` (`rests_on_flat`, `keeps_off`), `src/curved.rs` (`circle_side`, `Cone::beside`, `pair`), `src/self_intersection.rs` (`bare_meets_plain`, the module documentation), `tests/attack_g85_group3.rs`, `tests/attack_g134.rs`; geom-solidcore's `tests/named-faults.json` (bldg-25, bldg-26); MAP.json.

## 32.98 Group 3 certifies two faces on spheres apart when the spheres, widened by the faces' stray, are apart

**Decided by the main session, 19 Nov 2026** (its brief to the lane `ck1-sphere`, issue #991, named fault CK-1), under chapter 8.3's rule that a pair is certified with bounds, not sampled, and is otherwise a fault. The case bool-26 (a ball of radius 1 less a concentric ball of radius 0.5) was refused by group 3 with one fault, "no certified test of this pair exists yet": nothing certified two faces on spheres, so the outer sphere and the cavity were `PairUnchecked`, as the kernel's own form of a hollow ball (two shells, each one sphere face with no edge, as `geom-make` writes a ball, the cavity's reversed) would be.

**What it decides.**
1. **Where a face on a sphere lies.** A face lies on its sphere but where its boundary strays, and no farther from it than its boundary does, which group 2 has held within the body's bound; a face whose every vertex and edge lies on its sphere at a bound of nothing, a whole sphere with no edge among them, strays by nothing (`Surface::holds_face`, as for two parallel planes since issue #783). So every point of a face on the sphere about c of radius r, straying by at most s, is in the shell r − s ≤ |p − c| ≤ r + s.
2. **Two such shells are apart** (`curved::spheres_apart`, in `curved::pair`) when, with d the distance between the centres, r and r' the radii and s the two faces' stray together: each lies outside the other, d² > (r + r' + s)²; or the second lies inside the first's hollow, r − r' − s > 0 and (r − r' − s)² > d², or the first inside the second's with r and r' exchanged. Each is compared on the certified ends of its interval forms (d² on its lower end against the sum's square on its upper end, and so on), squared so every comparison is an exact sign on exact data, as two parallel planes are compared. Then the faces cannot meet, and the pair is apart. Where the stray of both faces at the body's bound together (twice the bound) already clears, the faces' own stray is not asked: a pair apart at a larger stray is apart at a smaller one.
3. **Two faces with no edge are not one wall** (`self_intersection::back_to_back`). In a general body, two faces with the same edges, each run the opposite way, are the two sides of one wall and are not compared (11.7). Two faces with no edge at all, such as two whole spheres, met that rule with nothing to share, so two such balls in a general body were passed whether they crossed or not; the tests below found it on main, two touching spheres in a general body passing with no fault. A wall now needs at least one edge, and two faces with none are tested as any other pair.
4. **Everything else stays `PairUnchecked`**: two spheres that touch from outside (d = r + r') or from inside (d = r − r'), two that cross, one sphere twice (d = 0, r = r'), and two whose gap is within the faces' stray, inside or outside. Two faces on one sphere, which may share edges, stay unchecked too: no test here asks where on the sphere they lie.

**Tests** (geom-check's `tests/attack_g85_group3.rs`, through the public interface). They failed first (commit 9624ce069: 16 run, 12 passed, 4 failed: three on pairs of sphere faces reported unchecked that should pass, and one on two touching balls in a general body passed with no fault) and pass now:
- `a_hollow_ball_passes`: bool-26 on its grid (radii of 1.6e8 and 8e7 steps about the origin) at the converted body's bound of 3202 steps; a ball of radius 10 with a cavity of radius 5, and one a step thick, at bounds of 0 and 1; a cavity off the centre by 5, along (3, 4, 0), inside a wall 6 thick, and one off the grid by √0.3125 inside a wall a step thick; each with the outer shell listed first and the cavity's first.
- `two_balls_each_outside_the_other_pass`: two balls a step apart along an axis and along (3, 4, 0).
- `two_spheres_that_touch_cross_or_coincide_stay_unchecked`: two balls in a general body, touching from outside and from inside, each along an axis and along (3, 4, 0); crossing from outside and from inside; one sphere twice, about the origin and off it. Each gives exactly one `PairUnchecked` of the two faces.
- `spheres_are_apart_only_beyond_what_their_faces_may_stray`: a cap of a sphere of radius 10, its rim on the sphere, is apart from a sphere half a step inside it and from one a step outside it, and not from one touching it; with its rim 0.4 off its sphere at a bound of 1 it may stray a step, so spheres half a step and a step inside it and a step outside it are unchecked, and spheres 1.25 and 2.5 inside it and 1.5 and 3 outside it are apart.

**Measured (19 Nov 2026, a development machine).** geom-check: 191 tests, all pass. geom-solidcore at the pin: on Linux 137 run, 99 pass and 38 fail, exactly the named list, against main's 98 and 39; bool-26 passes and leaves `tests/named-faults.json`, and no other case changed; under wasmtime 49.0.2 (wasm32-wasip1, each test in its own process, as `solidcore-wasm` runs them) the same 137 ran, 99 passed, the same 38 failed and none trapped. `kernel.yml`'s count of 137 tests is unchanged. The cost, on bool-26's body in geom-check (release build, 20,000 full checks, on a machine other jobs shared): 25 meter units and about 2.0 µs a check, no logical bytes, against main's 17 units and 1.2 µs, which stopped at its one unchecked pair before group 4; a box, whose path did not change, took 522 units both ways (158 against 189 µs, the machine's load). The test itself is one interval form of about 20 operations per pair of sphere faces.

**Mutants.** Every changed line of `src/` against geom-check's integration tests (cargo-mutants in diff mode with the repository's settings, release build): 13 mutants, 12 caught, 0 missed, 0 timed out, and 1 unviable, the `Default::default()` replacement of `pair`, whose type has no default.

**Changes:** this record; chapter 8.3's as-built text (`08-topology.md`); geom-check's `src/curved.rs` (`spheres_apart`, `pair`, `Pair::Apart`), `src/self_intersection.rs` (`back_to_back`, the module documentation, `other_faces_meet`'s), `tests/attack_g85_group3.rs`; geom-solidcore's `tests/named-faults.json` (bool-26); MAP.json.

## 32.74 A circle about a line through a sphere's centre that stands off the sphere meets nothing

**Decision (issue 974).** `curve_surface` answers a circle whose axis (the line through its centre along its normal) passes through a sphere's centre, and which does not lie on the sphere, in closed form: no item, proven, coverage `TopologyCertified`. Before, no closed form took a circle against a sphere, and the pair went to the interval engine. On the issue's fifth input (32.64's table: a sphere of radius 256 against a cylinder of radius 1 about a line through its centre) the cylinder's end circle stands 0.002 outside the sphere all the way round, and the engine shrank its boxes under that gap in three variables: 782,639 of the call's 782,683 work units. The answer was right and the cost was not. A run of the `natural` fuzz target makes three such calls and took 15 seconds on that stored input in the fuzzing build, over libFuzzer's line of 10 seconds for one input, so a search of the target stopped on it at once.

**Why it is exact.** Every point p of a circle of centre q and radius r is the same distance from any point s of its axis: |p − s|² = r² + |q − s|². So with the sphere's centre s on that axis the circle lies on the sphere of radius R where r² + |q − s|² = R², and no point of it is on the sphere otherwise, whatever the two domains. Whether s is on the axis is the sign of each component of (s − q) × n, n the circle's stored normal (`quadrics::off_line`), and whether the two sides are equal is the sign of r·r − R·R + Σ (qᵢ·qᵢ + sᵢ·sᵢ − 2·qᵢ·sᵢ): each a sum of products of stored doubles (`curve::sign_of`), with no tolerance and no rounded comparison. A circle's frame is defined exactly by its stored normal (32.64), so the stored data are the circle.

**What stays the engine's**, with its old answer and at its old cost: a circle that lies on the sphere (the sign is zero; 32.64's table still owes it an overlap, as its last row says); a circle whose axis misses the sphere's centre; and stored doubles whose pair products would not be exact.

**Measured.** One `natural` call on the stored input `fdde00f5ffc22502de`, in both orders: 782,683 work units before and 23 after, and the answer is the same in every field but the work it records. No benchmark digest moves.

**Started from:** none. The theorem is Pythagoras on the circle's axis; no other code was read for it.

**Changes:** this record; `geom-intersect`'s `curve_surface.rs` (`circle_about_sphere` and its arm in `closed_form`) and `quadrics.rs` (`off_line`, now shared within the crate); two tests in `tests/circle_on_surface.rs` (off the sphere outside it, inside it, about its own centre and on an axis off the coordinate axes; on the sphere and about another line, the engine's); `geom-fuzz`'s `natural` target (the fifth input moved from the engine's list to its own, held at 23 units).

## 32.80 An arc whose crossings the enclosures cannot tell apart is the engine's

**Decision (issue 1002).** Where 32.69's closed form would answer two stretches of a circle lying in a plane whose enclosures meet, it answers `None` and the pair is the engine's. The case: a side of the plane's domain within a rounding of touching the circle, so that the side crosses it twice, exactly (32.69's signs), at two points so close that the enclosure of each reaches the point where the side would touch. The unit circle about the origin in z = 0 against the plane z = 0 whose domain's lower side stands one to four steps above -1 crosses it at x about plus and minus 1.5e-8; `across` encloses each x from r² − b² in intervals, whose enclosure reaches 0, so each x is held as reaching 0 and each angle as reaching the lowest point, 1.5 half-turns. The arc the domain holds, round the top from one crossing to the other across the join, was written as two stretches, about [1.4999999999999991, 2] and [0, 1.500000000000002], which meet at 1.5, and `Intersection::new` refused them as `Internal` (12.15: items of one kind whose enclosures meet are a bug in the method). Now the engine answers, as it did before 32.69: one flagged region of higher-order contact, 5,088 work units on each of the four pairs.

**Why.** The closed form cannot tell the two crossings apart, and an answer whose items meet is not an answer. The engine's flagged region is honest, and it is what the code before 32.69 gave on these pairs, so this record changes no answer that existed before it. Cost: the engine's work, about 5,000 units where the closed form refused after 12, in a band of sides one rounding wide.

**Later, not done now.** `across` could enclose r² − b² from its exact square, a sum of products of stored doubles, to a step, which would hold each x to its true size and tell these crossings apart; every crossing's enclosure in 32.69's closed form would narrow, so its answers would move in their last bits and its family would be measured again (a separate issue).

**The angle of a crossing.** In the same change, `angle` writes a point of the lower half as two half-turns less the angle of its mirror in the first axis, which `turn_of` writes in place in the upper half. It replaces a threshold on the enclosure's lower end (`lower_half`), whose boundary no input could reach: an enclosure written in place had its lower end above 0.5 + 3e-9 and one written a turn back below 0. The mirror has no threshold. Its cost: where x is surely negative, the enclosure is up to two units in the last place wider at its lower end and one at its upper (measured on 5,418 points), and it is an enclosure of the true angle everywhere. The code is new with 32.69, so no older answer is kept to the bit.

**Changes:** this record; 32.69's list of what stays the engine's; `geom-intersect`'s `curve_surface.rs` (`arcs_in_plane`, `angle`; `lower_half` removed) and `result.rs` (`ParamBox::meets` visible to the crate); `tests/side_at_the_bottom.rs` and `tests/lower_half_arcs.rs`.

## 32.85 A line along a plane off the grid is decided by exact signs of the stored doubles

**Decision (issue 995, group P).** `curve_surface` decides whether a line runs along a plane, and whether it lies in it, by exact signs of the stored doubles on any data, not only on whole numbers. Lying in it: one `Item::Overlap` with `Coincidence::Identical` over the part of the line the plane's domain holds, as on the grid. Along it and off it: nothing, proven. Any other line: the one point it was, where an interval puts it. Before, the exact signs (`orient3d` on the stored points) needed every coordinate of the plane and the line to be a whole number. Off the grid the code fell back to an enclosure of the step n·d, n = a × b, whose true value for a parallel line is exactly zero, so the enclosure always straddled zero and the pair went to the interval engine. The engine found a zero residual all along a segment lying in the plane, or a gap of under a unit to a few units along one beside it, and bisected the whole segment toward the floor.

| Input | Shape | Before | After |
|---|---|---|---|
| the piercing target's stored timeouts `001133ce`, `6f26df6f`, `ecf1bf17` (14 November) | a segment along y lying in a plane through the origin whose first vector holds a half | 1,048,577 units each, stopped by the target's budget of 2^20 (at 2^24: two flagged near-coincident or higher-order items over the whole segment at 16,144,444 and 16,003,564 units, and one still stopped) | one overlap over the whole segment, 1 unit |
| three slow units of a search on the Phase 5 train | the same segment shape along a plane 0.48, 8.3 and 3.97 off it | 913,098, 720,686 and 919,038 units, nothing found | nothing, 0 units |
| a fourth slow unit | a segment lying in its plane | 1,017,940 units, one flagged item of higher-order contact over the segment | one overlap, 1 unit |
| the natural target's `004b000000ca0000000000caf7` | a plane's edge lying in a plane square to (0.6, 0.8, 0) | 1,042,049 units a call (1,041,980 on that edge) | 60 units, the answer the same in every field but the work, in both orders |

**Why it is exact.** The plane is c + u·a + v·b and the line o + t·d in their stored doubles, so these doubles are the two kinds exactly, whatever their values. n·d is the sum of six products of three of them (w · (a × b) written out), and n·(c − o) of twelve (n·c and −n·o). Each product a·b·c is exact as four doubles: a·b is a double and its error (`two_prod`), and each of those times c a double and its error; the kernel's exact sum (`ExactSum`) adds them and rounds once, so the sign of the rounded sum is the sign of the true one. This holds while no factor is near either end of the doubles' range: every factor zero or between 10⁻⁷⁵ and 10⁷⁵ in size (`tame`, as in 32.69), so that no product or error overflows or falls below the smallest double; a pair whose doubles are not all so is answered as before. No tolerance and no rounded comparison is used. Whole-number data keep `orient3d` first, so their answers are the same, character for character.

**The enclosures first.** The exact sums cost every off-grid line with a plane 18 products of three, and the benchmark gate measured it: `reduced_wall` 7,118,977 to 8,377,333 instructions (+17.7%), past its 5% limit. So `line_plane` first encloses n·d and n·(c − o) in intervals from the same stored doubles (`plane_enclosures`), every operation rounded outward, and forms an exact sum only where its enclosure holds zero. This is sound because an enclosure holds the true value: where it excludes zero every real in it has one sign, the true value's among them, so that sign is the one the exact sum would give (`apart`). A step whose enclosure excludes zero is a line across the plane, and no exact sign is asked, on the grid or off it; where it holds zero the exact signs decide as above, and the offset's enclosure, where it excludes zero, gives the side after the same range check (`sign_of_triples`, the exact sum now `exact_sign_of_triples`, the twelve terms `side_terms`). The same signs, so the same answers, and still no tolerance. On `reduced_wall`'s 64 pairs (128 calls, all off the grid, all reaching the exact sums before) both enclosures exclude zero on every call, their signs the exact ones, so no exact sum is formed. A module test holds the enclosures' signs to the exact sums on 20,000 random pairs, a third of them parallel by construction, and `tests/line_plane_exact.rs` holds a crossing told by its enclosure alone at the closed form's 4 units, and pairs whose enclosures straddle zero (lying in a plane, and parallel one unit in the last place off it) told by the exact signs.

**What stays as it was.** A line with a plane on whole-number data; a line whose stored doubles are not all within that range; and a line that is not parallel but so nearly so that the enclosure of the step still straddles zero, which the exact sign says is not zero, goes to the engine as before. The overlap's box is `in_plane`'s, unchanged: the line's whole parameter span, with (u, v) the hull of its ends' enclosures held to the domain.

**Measured** (one call each, native debug build, the target's own decoding and budget). The table above. The answers that existed before are the same: the parallel units' nothing, and the natural target's curve, character for character but its work. No benchmark digest moves.

**Started from:** none. The theorem is the triple product of three vectors, and the exact products are the kernel's own `two_prod` and `ExactSum` (32.64, 32.74); no other code was read for it.

**Changes:** this record; chapter 12.6's as-built line; `geom-intersect`'s `curve_surface.rs` (`plane_signs`, now `grid_signs` then `stored_signs`, with `triple_terms` and `sign_of_triples`) and its header; `tests/line_plane_exact.rs`; `geom-fuzz`'s `piercing` target (`answer_of`, the four slow units stored in its `crashes` folder and all seven held to their answers and counts) and `natural` target (the input stored, its count held at 60).

## 32.86 A circle across a parallel cylinder's or cone's axis meets it where it meets the circle of its plane

**Decision (issue 995, group 3).** `curve_surface` answers a circle whose normal is parallel to the axis of a cylinder or cone, and which does not lie on it (32.64), in closed form, coverage `TopologyCertified`: nothing where the circle's plane is past the surface's domain in v, and otherwise the points where it meets the circle the surface cuts in that plane: nothing for two circles apart (one beside the other or inside it), two `Item::Point`s that cross, or one `PointContact::IsolatedTangent` where they touch. Before, such a pair went to the interval engine, which ran round the whole circle: on the five `natural` inputs of issue 995's group 3 (two cones of slope 2 on parallel, distinct axes along (0.6, 0.8, 0), each end circle of one, of radius 344 to 856, taken against the other) it paid 258,258 to 1,759,429 work units a circle, for nothing or two points, and 3,106,700 to 4,193,766 a call, at the edge of the target's 2^22 stop.

**Why it is right.** A plane square to the axis of a right circular cylinder or cone cuts it in the circle about the axis of the radius there, |r₀ + k·v| (r₀ for a cylinder), or, at a cone's apex, in that one point; and a circle in that plane meets the surface where it meets that circle. Two circles in one plane whose centres are D apart, of radii r and ρ, cross at two points where (r − ρ)² < D² < (r + ρ)², touch at one where D² is either end and D is not zero, and are apart where D² lies past both ends. The circle's normal parallel to the axis is an exact sign (`quadrics::parallel`, each cross-product component a sum of products of stored doubles); a circle's frame is defined exactly by its stored normal (32.64), so the stored data are the circle and its plane.

**How it is decided, never by a tolerance.**

- The plane's v, the circle's height over the centre along the unit axis over s, is placed against the domain as 32.64 places a circle lying on the surface (`height_across`, shared with `ring_overlap`): past an end by an enclosure, or by the exact sign of (h − s·end) on a coordinate axis, is nothing. A plane clear of both ends by its enclosure goes on; so does one whose enclosure reaches an end without being past it, held to the domain, for nothing only (below).
- How the two circles stand is read from enclosures of D², (r + ρ)² and (r − ρ)² where they are clear of each other. Where they are not, and the axis runs along a coordinate axis, it is read from exact signs: the height is then a difference of two stored doubles and D² a sum of their products, so s²·(D² − (r ± ρ)²) is a sum of products of doubles, each factor first written as a double and its error (`exactly`, 96 products, held on the stack).
- Each crossing is found as two circles' crossings in one plane are found for curves (`curve::Crossing`, Kahan's stable Heron), from the enclosures, and is kept only if the two crossings' boxes are apart.

**What stays the engine's**, at its old cost and with its old answer: a circle or a round domain short of a whole turn; two circles that cross or touch where the plane's enclosure reaches an end of the domain and is not past it (the plane may be on the end, inside, or, where no exact sign places it, past it, and the closed form does not place points there; two circles apart there are nothing, below); a plane through a cone's apex, or one whose cut radius an enclosure cannot hold from zero; two circles the enclosures cannot tell on an axis off the coordinate axes, such as a touch there; stored doubles whose products would not be exact; and two crossings whose boxes meet, as where the circles cross by a hair. A circle lying on the surface is 32.64's overlap as before; this form is taken only where 32.64's says no closed form.

**Measured** (one `natural` call, debug build, both orders the same). The five inputs, work before and after: `330b00feffff0effffffff006bff` 4,151,095 and 1,377; `7f03000080ffffffff00ffba03ff` 4,193,766 and 1,377; `2b03e6f6d803f6e7e7e7e7e703f6e7e7e7` 3,736,405 and 1,377; `9303000302ff2f4b41fffefffff7` 3,748,566 and 3,984; `6333b733b7ffffff7fb7d4b46bff` 3,106,700 and 3,984. Each end circle against the other cone: nothing for 0 units where the engine took 258,258 to 351,377, and two crossings for 648 units (the circle's box in space at each point) where it took 1,172,816 to 1,759,429. The answers of the first three are the same as before in every field but the work, character for character. In the last two the four circles that cross give two points each, as the engine's did, and each closed-form point's box in t, u and v lies inside the engine's; `natural` cuts its curves at the middles of those boxes, so the ends of the answer's two curves move, 9 and 14 numbers by up to 4e-10, the same curves on narrower cuts. Issue 974's stored input of two coaxial cones off the grid (`032d3c0009030003000000090300`, on the engine's list of 32.64's table) falls from 800,571 to 1,697 with its answer the same: the end circle in the plane of the other cone's apex is built a rounding off that plane, where the cut is a circle an enclosure holds clear of zero, apart from it. No benchmark digest moves (@@).

**A plane at an end (issue 995, group R).** A plane whose enclosure reaches an end of the domain without being past it was first left to the engine whatever the two circles did. The `natural` input `73ffffffdffffffffffffddf3fce` is that case: two cones of slope 2 on parallel axes along (0.6, 0.8, 0), of start radius 224 and 0, whose centres differ by 8 square to the axis, so each end circle lies in the plane of an end of the other cone's domain, where that cone cuts a circle of radius 600 8 from it. The circles, of radius 376 or 824, are nested and apart by 216, and the engine ran round each for nothing: 460,442 to 942,607 units a circle, 2,756,742 a call. Every point of the surface in a plane square to the axis lies on the circle it cuts there, whether the plane is inside the domain, on its end or past it (past it there is no point at all), so two circles apart for every v of the plane's enclosure, held to the domain, meet nothing wherever the end lies. So at an end the two circles are still decided, by enclosures over that v or, on a coordinate axis, by `exactly`'s signs at the exact height: apart is nothing, by the same theorem and with no point to place. Crossing or touching there stays the engine's, as before: a point on or near an end is a boundary contact the closed form does not place. The input now takes 1,377 units a call, its answer the same as before in every field but the work (the SHA-256 of its items', coverage's and diagnostics' Debug text is the engine's answer's). Changes: `height_across`'s `Unplaced` carries the enclosure held to the domain; `circle_across` reads a plane at an end for nothing only; two tests in `tests/circle_on_surface.rs` (circles apart at an end meet nothing, 20 cases that cost the engine 1,308 to 980,159 units, now 0; crossing or touching at an end stays the engine's, which flags it); the input stored in `natural`'s `crashes` folder and held at 1,377 units with its answer's digest.

**Started from:** none found. Searched tangency-labs/solidcore at e93fa8423025b035baa30645667e5c0d691d8f41 by code search (circle with cylinder or cone, curve-surface intersection): its circle meets a round surface through its general surface-surface and curve-surface paths, and nothing there cuts a circle across a parallel axis by the circle the surface cuts in its plane. The theorem is the plane section of a surface of revolution and two circles in one plane; the crossing points are `curve::Crossing`'s, already in this crate.

**Changes:** this record; chapter 12.6's as-built line; `geom-intersect`'s `curve_surface.rs` (`circle_across`, `exactly` and their helpers; `height_across`, factored out of `ring_overlap`; their arms in `closed_form`) and `curve.rs` (`Crossing::between`, the crossings from enclosures, which `Crossing::of` now calls); seven tests in `tests/circle_on_surface.rs`; `geom-fuzz`'s `natural` target (`answer_of`; the five inputs stored in its `crashes` folder, each held to its count and its answer's curves, and the coaxial input moved from the engine's list to theirs, held at 1,697).

## 32.94 A piece no edge cuts is kept whole only on a middle proven inside both domains

**Decision (issue 1018).** In `quadrics::trimmed`, a curve of a natural pair that no mark cuts (no edge of either surface meets the other on it, and no apex lies on it) is kept whole only where the box of its middle point is proven inside both domains: on each surface, each parameter's enclosure strictly inside its range, or reaching only an end that is a join (a whole turn's seam, a sphere's pole). Where that box is wholly past an end of either domain, the curve is nothing, as before. Where a plane's box reaches an end of its domain, its parameters are read again as the point's offsets from the corners of its domain, so that a plane whose origin stands far from its points (whose parameters a double holds no closer than its whole domain) still places a point well inside (`range_screen`: a domain of one unit 2^52 out).
